Supplementary File S2 · Extracted data tables

Co-existing health needs in transgender and gender diverse populations

This file presents the data extracted from studies included in the integrative review, organised across four tables: mental health, neurodiversity, physical health and social health. Each entry can be expanded to view the study aim, design, sample characteristics and key findings, and each table can be searched by author, design, population or finding.

Citation: [Authors]. [Title of review]. [Journal, year]. This supplementary file should be read alongside the main manuscript and Supplementary File S1.

Table S2.1 Mental health Study characteristics and extracted findings for studies reporting mental health outcomes, including self-harm, suicidality, depression, anxiety and eating disorders. 155 studies Table S2.2 Neurodiversity Study characteristics and extracted findings for studies examining neurodiversity, including autism and ADHD. 33 studies Table S2.3 Physical health Study characteristics and extracted findings for studies reporting physical health outcomes. 56 studies Table S2.4 Social health Study characteristics and extracted findings for studies examining social determinants of health. 131 studies

Table S2.1

Mental health

Study characteristics and extracted findings for studies reporting mental health outcomes, including self-harm, suicidality, depression, anxiety and eating disorders.

Filled markers indicate the outcome domains reported by each study. Select a study to view its full extracted data.

Study aim

To assess the health and wellbeing of transgender young people accessing specialist gender-affirming healthcare and identify strengths and gaps in current services.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–23
SampleN=100; trans female (n=31), trans male (n=65), non-binary (n=4)
Validated toolsK10

Key findings

  • High psychological distress: 74% of transgender young people reported high/very high distress, around five times higher than the general youth population.
  • Self-harm and suicidality: 56% reported lifetime self-harm; 59% reported suicidal ideation; 15% reported a suicide attempt in the past year.
  • Access to care: 39% were unable to access mental health support when needed; unmet need was associated with higher distress.
  • Protective and risk factors: Family support was associated with lower distress, while bullying and discrimination were common and linked to poorer mental health.
  • Service engagement: Distress remained high but was lower than in community samples of transgender youth without access to specialist services.

Study aim

To compare the prevalence and correlates of smoking, cardiovascular disease, and venous thromboembolism between transgender and cisgender adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥40
SampleN=1,078; trans women (n=70), trans men (n=25), non-binary (n=19), cis women (n=517), cis men (n=447)
Validated toolsK6

Key findings

  • Higher psychological distress: Transgender adults reported significantly higher psychological distress than cisgender adults (mean K6 7.2 vs 4.3).
  • Discrimination and trauma: Mean everyday discrimination scores were higher (1.92 vs 1.60), and adverse childhood experiences were more common (mean ACE score 2.56 vs 2.14).
  • Mental health–physical health link: Psychological distress was associated with increased odds of cardiovascular disease for both transgender (aOR 1.15) and cisgender adults (aOR 1.07).
  • Risk behaviours: Among transgender participants, higher psychological distress and ACEs were associated with current smoking, a key health-risk behaviour.
  • Implications: Findings support the gender minority stress model, highlighting cumulative psychosocial stress as a key driver of mental health burden and downstream health risk.

Study aim

To determine the prevalence of depressive symptoms among transgender men and identify associated risk and protective factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–64
SampleN=205; female-to-male (FTM) spectrum
Validated toolsCES-D (≥16 threshold)

Key findings

  • Depressive symptomatology: 66.4% of FTM Ontarians met CES-D criteria for depression (score ≥16).
  • Comorbid mental health diagnoses: 63.0% reported a diagnosed mental health condition (mostly anxiety disorders).
  • Key risk factors: Higher exposure to transphobia (each unit increase ↑ odds of depression by ~17%) and planning but not yet starting medical transition (≈6× higher odds).
  • Protective factor: Greater sexual satisfaction (≈50% reduction in odds per unit increase).
  • Context: Depression prevalence substantially higher than the general Ontario population (~4.8%).

Study aim

To estimate the prevalence of depressive symptoms among transgender women and examine associated risk and protective factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional community survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)17–77
SampleN=186; male-to-female (MTF) spectrum
Validated toolsCES-D (≥16 cutoff)

Key findings

  • Depressive symptomatology: 61.2% met CES-D criteria for depression.
  • Mental health diagnoses: 28.8% reported a diagnosed mental health condition, most commonly anxiety disorders.
  • Risk factors: Depression was more prevalent among those living outside metropolitan Toronto, unemployed, and with a history of childhood physical or sexual abuse (72.3%).
  • Minority stress: Higher levels of transphobia were associated with increased odds of depression.
  • Protective factors: Greater social and identity support were associated with reduced odds of depression.

Study aim

To describe baseline physiological and psychosocial characteristics of transgender youth presenting for gender-affirming care.

DesignQuantitative (prospective observational cohort; baseline assessment)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–24
SampleN=101; transmasculine (n=52), transfeminine (n=49), outside binary (n=10)
Validated toolsBDI-II

Key findings

  • Depression: 35% reported depressive symptoms in the clinical range on the BDI; 20% fell in the moderate–severe range.
  • Suicidality: 51% reported lifetime suicidal ideation and 30% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Early awareness, delayed disclosure: Youth recognised gender incongruence at a mean age of 8.3 years, but disclosed to family much later (mean 17.1 years), indicating prolonged psychosocial distress prior to care.
  • Risk behaviours: High lifetime substance use was reported (alcohol 76%, tobacco 58%, cannabis 62%), co-occurring with elevated mental health burden.
  • Clinical implication: High baseline depression and suicidality highlight substantial mental health needs at first presentation to specialist services.

Study aim

To examine the prevalence of suicide attempts, self-injurious behaviours, and body dissatisfaction among transgender adolescents and emerging adults.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–22
SampleN=96; FtM (n=54), MtF (n=31), non-binary/gender fluid (n=15)
Validated toolsStructured psychosocial interview; EMR diagnoses

Key findings

  • Depression and anxiety: Mood (37%) and anxiety (28%) disorders were the most common diagnoses, affecting over half of the sample.
  • Self-harm and suicidality: Lifetime self-harm (41.8%) and suicidal ideation (30.3%) were common, with substantially higher prevalence than population norms.
  • Minority stress: Experiences of discrimination and victimisation were significantly associated with higher distress and depressive symptoms.

Study aim

To describe the sexual health of transgender men who are gay, bisexual, or who have sex with men.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey; respondent-driven sampling)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥16
SampleN=433; trans men (n=227); includes gender fluid/bigender AFAB participants
Validated toolsCES-D

Key findings

  • Depression: Clinically significant depressive symptoms were reported by approximately 60–70% of participants.
  • Anxiety: Anxiety disorders were among the most frequently reported mental health conditions, affecting over half of the sample.
  • Substance use co-occurrence: Mental health symptoms commonly co-occurred with substance use, particularly alcohol and illicit drug use.
  • Unmet need: A substantial proportion reported unmet mental health care needs despite high symptom burden.

Study aim

To examine the prevalence of non-medical prescription drug use and hormone misuse among transgender people and assess associations with substance use and psychiatric symptoms.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–65
SampleN=155; MTF (67.1%), FTM (32.9%)
Validated toolsBSI-18, Rosenberg Self-Esteem

Key findings

  • Non-medical prescription drug use: 26.5% reported lifetime non-medical use of prescription drugs.
  • Psychiatric symptoms: Among those reporting non-medical prescription drug use, 37% scored in a range indicating probable clinical mental health concern.
  • Depression and anxiety: Users reported significantly higher depression, anxiety, and somatic symptom scores than non-users.
  • Psychosocial correlates: Higher discrimination exposure and lower self-esteem were independently associated with worse mental health outcomes.

Study aim

To explore the health and social needs of transgender people of colour, including identity development and HIV-related concerns.

DesignQualitative (in-depth interviews and focus groups)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–50
SampleN=43; transgender (MTF/FTM), trans, queer, questioning, androgynous, gender fluid
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • High psychological distress: Participants described pervasive experiences of depression, anxiety, fear, and emotional exhaustion linked to intersecting racism, transphobia, poverty, and social exclusion.
  • Suicidality and self-harm: Suicidal ideation and suicide attempts were frequently discussed, particularly during periods of housing instability, family rejection, and economic precarity.
  • Trauma exposure: Narratives highlighted repeated exposure to physical and sexual violence, often associated with survival strategies such as sex work and unsafe living environments.
  • Substance use as coping: Alcohol and drug use were commonly described as coping mechanisms in response to chronic stress, trauma, and marginalisation.
  • Barriers to care: Mental health needs were often unmet due to discrimination within healthcare services, lack of insurance, and fear of mistreatment.
  • Protective factors: Peer support, community connection, and access to culturally competent, affirming services were identified as critical buffers against worsening mental health.

Study aim

To evaluate dementia risk factors and risk scores among cisgender, transgender, and non-binary adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional population survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=955; AMAB, AFAB, gender non-binary (plus cisgender controls)
Validated toolsBRFSS

Key findings

  • Depression prevalence: Depression was reported by 49.8% of non-binary adults and 39.8% of transgender men, compared with 30.7% of transgender women, 24.2% of cisgender women, and 14.7% of cisgender men.
  • Group disparities: Non-binary adults had the highest prevalence of depression across all gender groups, followed by transgender men.
  • Mental health as a dementia risk factor: Depression was identified as a key modifiable risk factor for later-life dementia, with mid-life depression associated with a substantially increased risk of Alzheimer’s disease (as cited by authors).
  • Inequity pattern: Mental health burden was disproportionately concentrated in transgender and non-binary groups, aligning with broader gender-based health disparities observed in the study.
  • Implication: Findings highlight significant mental health disparities among transgender and non-binary adults that may contribute to downstream cognitive and physical health risks.

Study aim

To assess whether multiple psychosocial factors are additive in their association with sexual risk behaviours and HIV status among young transgender women.

DesignMixed-methods (survey and qualitative interviews)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–24
SampleN=151; transgender women
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Psychosocial burden: Multiple co-occurring mental health and psychosocial problems were common, including low self-esteem, polysubstance use, victimisation, and intimate partner violence, frequently clustering within individuals.
  • Substance use: >90% reported lifetime substance use; 50% reported past-30-day alcohol use and 38% past-30-day marijuana use. Polysubstance use (≥3 substances lifetime) was a key mental health–related vulnerability.
  • Violence and victimisation: 21–68% reported forced sexual intercourse and 37–65% reported physical abuse (childhood or adulthood), both strongly linked to psychological distress.
  • Self-esteem: Low self-esteem was common and used as a mental health indicator within the syndemic model; lower self-esteem co-occurred with other psychosocial stressors.
  • Syndemic effect: Increasing numbers of psychosocial problems were associated with worse overall mental health and additive risk profiles; individuals with 3–4 syndemic factors showed markedly higher vulnerability.
  • Social marginalisation link: Mental health burden was strongly associated with incarceration (52%), homelessness (43%), and sex work (67%), highlighting structural drivers of distress.

Study aim

To examine clinical and mental health characteristics of adolescents presenting to a transgender clinic and compare findings with previous cohorts.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–18
SampleN=203; AFAB (n=156), AMAB (n=47)
Validated toolsBDI-II, MASC2, UGDS

Key findings

  • Depression and anxiety: 37.4% reported a depressive disorder; 28.1% an anxiety disorder; 33.9% had used mood-disorder medication.
  • Suicidality and self-harm: 33.0% reported suicidal thoughts and 30.5% reported self-harm.
  • Symptom severity: Severe depressive symptoms (BDI-II >29) in 41.6% AFAB and 34.4% AMAB; clinically significant anxiety in 44.4% AFAB and 30.0% AMAB.
  • Neurodevelopmental comorbidity: ASD in 5–6%; ADHD in 14.9% of AMAB youth.
  • Follow-up: Mental health scores did not worsen after puberty suppression in those with follow-up data

Study aim

To report the prevalence of gender groups among secondary-school students and describe associated health and wellbeing outcomes.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional school-based survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)<15 to ≥16
SampleN=8,166; transgender (n=96), unsure (n=202), did not understand (n=137); majority cisgender
Validated toolsReynolds Adolescent Depression Scale

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms: 41.3% of transgender students had clinically significant depressive symptoms, compared with 11.8% of non-transgender peers (aOR 5.7, 95% CI 3.6–9.2).
  • Self-harm and suicidality: 45.5% reported self-harm in the past 12 months and 19.8% reported a suicide attempt, versus 23.4% and 4.1% respectively among non-transgender students.
  • Psychosocial risk: Transgender students had markedly higher exposure to bullying (17.6% weekly) and fear of harm at school (53.5%), factors closely linked to mental health burden.
  • Access to care: 39.2% were unable to access healthcare when needed, compared with 17.8% of non-transgender students.
  • Overall pattern: Transgender students showed substantially poorer mental health and wellbeing across all indicators relative to non-transgender peers.

Study aim

To assess whether gender minority stress changes following initiation of gender-affirming hormone therapy and identify associated social and hormonal predictors.

DesignQuantitative (prospective longitudinal survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 30)
SampleN=85; trans men (n=47), trans women (n=38)
Validated toolsSTAI, BDI-II, SSI

Key findings

  • Baseline mental health burden: Trans participants showed significantly higher depression, state and trait anxiety, suicidal ideation, suicidality, perceived stress, and stigma consciousness compared with cisgender controls at baseline.
  • Change over time with GAHT: After ~7.7 months of GAHT, trait anxiety significantly decreased in trans participants; other mental health indicators remained elevated relative to cis controls.
  • Suicidality: Trans participants consistently reported higher suicidal thoughts/attempts and suicide ideation scores than cis participants at both baseline and follow-up.
  • Social factors: Larger social networks were associated with fewer suicidal thoughts/attempts, higher perceived social standing, and greater resilience, though also with higher perceived stress.
  • Hormonal associations: In trans women, higher estradiol levels were associated with lower trait anxiety and suicidality, and higher resilience; no parallel associations were observed with testosterone in trans men.

Study aim

To estimate health indicators and socioeconomic status by transgender status in a representative population sample.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional population survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–64
SampleN=131; male-sounding, female-sounding voices
Validated toolsBRFSS

Key findings

  • Poor mental health days: 14.3% of transgender adults reported ≥15 days of poor mental health in the past 30 days, compared with 9.8% of non-transgender adults.
  • Emotional support: 30.4% reported rarely or never receiving needed emotional support, versus 18.2% of non-transgender adults.
  • Life dissatisfaction: 14.4% of transgender adults reported being dissatisfied with life, compared with 5.6% of non-transgender adults.
  • Overall pattern: Mental health indicators suggested greater psychosocial burden among transgender adults despite otherwise limited health differences in this household sample.

Study aim

To investigate associations between testosterone levels and aggression in treatment-seeking transgender people, considering mental health and social support.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)19–45
SampleN=144; trans men (n=61), trans women (n=83)
Validated toolsHADS

Key findings

  • Anxiety and depression: At baseline, median HADS anxiety scores were elevated (overall median 7–9), and depressive symptoms were common (median ~6–7).
  • Change over time: Depression scores significantly decreased after 1 year of gender-affirming hormone therapy in both transgender men (−1.42 points, p = .019) and transgender women (−1.05 points, p = .013). Anxiety scores did not significantly change over time.
  • Aggression and mental health: Changes in self-reported aggression were not associated with testosterone levels, but were positively correlated with increases in anxiety (TM: r = 0.539; TW: r = 0.359, both p ≤ .001).
  • Psychosocial factors: Lower perceived social support (particularly from friends) was associated with higher aggression scores, highlighting the role of psychological and social factors rather than hormones in mental health outcomes

Study aim

To describe the transgender Medicare beneficiary population and compare chronic disease burden with cisgender beneficiaries.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study using administrative data)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 53.1)
SampleN=7,454; transgender Medicare beneficiaries
Validated toolsCenters for Medicare & Medicaid Services; Chronic Conditions Data Warehouse

Key findings

  • Depression: 76.3% of transgender Medicare beneficiaries had a diagnosis of depression, compared with 28.8% of cisgender beneficiaries.
  • Anxiety disorders: 62.4% of transgender beneficiaries had diagnosed anxiety disorders, versus 20.2% of cisgender beneficiaries.
  • Severe mental illness: Diagnosed schizophrenia or other psychotic disorders were present in 26.7% of transgender beneficiaries, compared with 5.6% of cisgender beneficiaries.
  • Other psychiatric conditions: Bipolar disorder (39.9% vs 4.9%), PTSD (22.7% vs 1.6%), and personality disorders (25.3% vs 1.6%) were substantially more common among transgender beneficiaries.
  • Disability context: Mental health conditions were particularly prevalent among those entitled to Medicare via disability, with depression affecting 76.1% and anxiety 71.8% in this subgroup.

Study aim

To analyse the clinical characteristics of individuals attending a multidisciplinary consultation for gender dysphoria.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (median 30.2)
SampleN=114; FtM and MtF
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depression prevalence: 22.3% of individuals had a diagnosed depressive disorder; 19.3% were receiving active treatment with antidepressant medication.
  • Comorbidity profile: Depression was the most common mental health comorbidity, exceeding rates of other reported medical conditions.
  • Psychosocial vulnerability: A substantial proportion reported early onset of gender dysphoria (63.2% before age 10), with authors emphasising the need for ongoing psychiatric and psychological support.
  • Care implications: Findings highlight the importance of integrated mental health input within multidisciplinary gender services, particularly given the observed burden of depression and self-medication behaviours.

Study aim

To assess psychiatric comorbidity among patients receiving pharmacological treatment alone versus those undergoing gender-affirming surgery.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective medical record review)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)18–21
SampleN=158; FtM (n=107), MtF (n=47), non-binary (n=4)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Psychiatric comorbidity: At baseline, there were no significant differences between treatment groups in rates of depression, ADHD, bipolar disorder, anorexia, or bulimia nervosa.
  • Anxiety: Higher anxiety prevalence was observed in adolescents receiving hormone therapy plus surgery compared with hormone-only treatment (p = 0.002).
  • Gender dysphoria severity: Mean dysphoria scores decreased substantially after treatment in both groups (hormones only: 8.11 → 4.48; hormones + surgery: 8.75 → 2.11).
  • Mental health implication: Greater dysphoria reduction was observed in the hormone-plus-surgery group (p < 0.001), suggesting improved psychological wellbeing following intervention.

Study aim

To describe sociodemographic characteristics of individuals seeking gender transition and compare features between transgender women and men.

DesignQualitative (clinical and psychodiagnostic assessment)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 32.6)
SampleN=140; MtF (n=92), FtM (n=48)
Validated toolsSCL-90-R, BUT, BRSI

Key findings

  • Overall psychiatric comorbidity: 30.9% reported any lifetime psychiatric disorder and 19.4% any current psychiatric disorder.
  • Mood and adjustment disorders: Most common diagnoses (10.8% overall); major depressive disorder was uncommon (2.9%).
  • Anxiety disorders: Reported infrequently (5% overall), with no single anxiety diagnosis exceeding 3%.
  • Substance-related disorders: Rare (3.6% lifetime); psychotic disorders were very uncommon (1.4%).
  • Personality disorders: Axis II disorders identified in 4.3%, lower than rates reported in general population samples.
  • Group differences: No significant differences between transmasculine and transfeminine participants in psychiatric diagnoses or psychotropic medication use.
  • Psychological distress: Generally low on psychometric measures, with minor group differences (e.g. slightly higher phobic anxiety among transfeminine participants).

Study aim

To examine associations between social determinants of health and experiences of transgender-related discrimination.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 37.1)
SampleN=350; MtF and FtM
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Psychological distress and mental health need: 25% of participants reported needing counselling or psychotherapy in the past 12 months but were unable to obtain it, indicating substantial unmet mental health need.
  • Violence-related trauma: 27% reported a history of forced or unwanted sexual activity and 38% reported being physically attacked since age 13, exposures strongly linked to psychological distress.
  • Substance use and mental health: 23% reported a past or current alcohol problem, 23% a lifetime tobacco problem, and 13% a current tobacco problem, reflecting co-occurring mental health vulnerability.
  • Discrimination and mental health: 41% reported transgender-related discrimination; discrimination was significantly associated with unmet mental health care, substance use, and history of violence in adjusted models.
  • Social support: 31% reported family support that was “not at all” or “not very” supportive, a factor associated with poorer mental health and higher odds of discrimination.

Study aim

To explore hormonal, physical, and psychological changes during gonadotropin-releasing hormone analogue treatment in transgender adolescents.

DesignQuantitative (prospective longitudinal clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)11–17
SampleN=36; AMAB (n=14), AFAB (n=22)
Validated toolsYSR, BUT, MAST, BDI-II, BAI

Key findings

  • Overall psychological functioning: Total psychopathology scores (CBCL/YSR) significantly decreased after GnRHa initiation compared with the pre-treatment assessment phase (p < .01).
  • Depression: Depressive symptom scores increased during the assessment period but decreased significantly following GnRHa treatment, falling below clinical cut-off levels at follow-up (p < .05).
  • Anxiety: Anxiety scores significantly decreased after GnRHa initiation (p < .05), with no significant improvement observed during assessment alone.
  • Suicidality: Scores reflecting suicidal ideation (e.g. “repulsion by life”) improved significantly after treatment initiation (p < .05).
  • Internalising symptoms: Internalising problems (anxious–depressive symptoms, somatic complaints) reduced significantly following GnRHa treatment (p < .01).
  • Externalising symptoms: Externalising behaviour scores decreased significantly post-treatment (p < .05).
  • Body-related psychological distress: Body uneasiness scores increased during assessment but improved significantly after GnRHa treatment (p < .01).
  • Temporal pattern: Across outcomes, no significant mental health improvements occurred prior to GnRHa, whereas multiple domains improved significantly after treatment initiation.

Study aim

To examine physical and mental health among older transgender adults and identify modifiable risk factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥50 (mean 61.0)
SampleN=174; transgender older adults
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depressive symptomatology: Transgender older adults reported significantly higher depressive symptoms than non-transgender LGB peers (mean CES-D 10.34 vs 7.20, p < .001).
  • Perceived stress: Higher perceived stress was reported among transgender participants (mean 1.56 vs 1.22, p < .01).
  • Mental health disparities: Transgender identity remained independently associated with poorer mental health after adjustment for demographic factors.
  • Stigma and victimisation: Internalised stigma and lifetime victimisation explained up to 89% of the association with depressive symptoms and 94% of the association with perceived stress.
  • Healthcare access: Fear of accessing healthcare mediated 43% of the association with depressive symptoms and 56% with perceived stress.
  • Protective factors: Lower social support and community belonging were associated with worse mental health outcomes.

Study aim

To describe associations between sexual orientation identity and medical morbidity among transgender women.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective medical record review)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–76
SampleN=330; MtF
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depression prevalence: Approximately 30% of MTF patients reported a history of depression. Sexual orientation differences: Depression was more common among patients attracted to women (38%) compared with those attracted to men (22%) or both (29%).
  • Adjusted risk: Patients attracted to women had higher odds of depression compared with those attracted to men (adjusted OR 2.36, 95% CI 1.26–4.40).
  • Suicidality: A history of suicide attempt or ideation was reported by 2% of the sample, with no statistically significant differences by sexual orientation.
  • Anxiety: Anxiety disorders were reported by 12% of patients, with no significant variation by sexual orientation identity.
  • Mental health patterning: While most medical comorbidities did not differ by sexual orientation, mental health outcomes—particularly depression—showed clear variation across subgroups.

Study aim

To evaluate symptoms of social distress, anxiety, and depression among transsexual individuals.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–61
SampleN=187; MF and FM transsexuals
Validated toolsSADS, HADS

Key findings

  • Social distress: Mean social anxiety/distress scores (SADS) were in the normal range overall but were significantly higher in untreated patients compared with those receiving cross-sex hormone therapy (F = 4.36, p = .038).
  • Anxiety symptoms: Clinically relevant anxiety symptoms were reported by 61% of untreated patients versus 33% of hormone-treated patients; mean anxiety scores were significantly lower in treated patients (F = 14.59, p = .001).
  • Depressive symptoms: Depressive symptoms were reported by 31% of untreated patients compared with 8% of treated patients; mean depression scores were significantly lower among those receiving hormones (F = 9.52, p = .002).
  • Symptom severity: Across groups, mean anxiety and depression scores were generally subclinical, indicating elevated distress rather than categorical psychiatric disorder.
  • Treatment status: Length of time on hormone therapy was not correlated with anxiety, depression, or social distress scores, suggesting differences were related to treatment status rather than duration.
  • Surgical status: Among hormone-treated patients, mental health scores did not differ between those who had and had not undergone gender-affirming surgery.
  • Overall pattern: Untreated individuals showed consistently higher levels of anxiety, depression, and social distress than those receiving cross-sex hormone therapy.

Study aim

To assess quality of life among transsexual individuals receiving hormone therapy compared with untreated individuals and the general population.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 34.7)
SampleN=61; MtF and FtM
Validated toolsBDI, SF-36

Key findings

  • Depression prevalence: 25% of participants met criteria for depression (mild to moderate); no severe depression reported. Quality of life (mental component): Hormone therapy was independently associated with higher mental quality-of-life scores on the SF-36 mental composite score.
  • Depression impact: Depression was strongly associated with lower mental health, vitality, social functioning, and emotional role scores across SF-36 domains.
  • Predictors of mental QoL: In multivariate analysis, hormone therapy (positive) and depression (negative) were the only independent predictors of mental QoL.
  • Comparison with controls: Overall mental QoL did not differ significantly from age- and sex-matched population controls, except for specific subdomains.
  • Non-hormone group vulnerability: Individuals not receiving hormones had significantly poorer emotional role functioning compared with both hormone users and controls.

Study aim

To describe patterns of access to gender services, reasons for discharge and re-referral, and quantify detransitioning.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study using clinical records)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (median 25)
SampleN=175; MtF (n=101), FtM (n=59), non-binary (n=14)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Pre-existing mental health diagnoses: 72.4% had ≥1 diagnosed mental health condition prior to assessment; anxiety and/or depression were most common (63.2%).
  • Self-harm history: 42.8% had a documented history of self-harm; 25.4% had at least one prior suicide attempt.
  • Mental health concerns during treatment: 42.9% had mental health concerns documented while engaged with the service.
  • Secondary mental health service use: 26.0% had contact with mental health services during treatment; 17.9% accessed secondary mental health services specifically.
  • Unmet mental health need: 8.7% were advised to access mental health services but did not receive them.
  • Self-harm during treatment: 14.5% had documented self-harm episodes while in care. Suicide attempts during treatment: 8.1% attempted suicide during treatment.
  • Completed suicide: 1.7% (n=3) died by suicide during the treatment period.
  • Neurodevelopmental conditions: 12.7% had a diagnosed neurodevelopmental condition (e.g. ASD, ADHD), rising to 20.9% in those under 25 years.
  • Predictors of poorer service outcomes: Mental health concerns during treatment were independently associated with not completing care (OR 2.2, 95% CI 1.1–4.4).
  • Adverse childhood experiences (ACEs): 75.7% reported ≥1 ACE; increasing number of ACEs showed a dose–response relationship with poorer outcomes.

Study aim

To describe the clinical and psychosocial profile of transgender women in Italy and evaluate post-surgical satisfaction.

DesignMixed-methods (clinical assessment and follow-up survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)21–59
SampleN=139; MtF
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation: 50% of participants reported having contemplated suicide at some point prior to surgery.
  • Suicide attempts: 2% reported at least one suicide attempt before surgery, and 0.7% reported a suicide attempt after surgery.
  • Psychological preparation: 100% completed a period of psychological counselling prior to surgery, with a mean duration of approximately 32 months.
  • Emotional support: 94% reported receiving satisfactory emotional support from family during the presurgical period, a factor linked to psychological stability.
  • Decision certainty: Only 2% reported doubts or hesitation during the treatment process, suggesting high psychological commitment to transition.
  • Post-treatment psychological adjustment: The vast majority reported no regret after surgery (94%), indicating favourable long-term psychological adjustment.
  • Overall pattern: Despite high lifetime suicidal ideation, actual suicide attempts were rare, and postoperative psychological outcomes were generally positive within this highly selected surgical cohort.

Study aim

To estimate overall and cause-specific mortality among transgender and gender-diverse people compared with cisgender populations.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study using primary care databases)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,315; transfeminine (n=1,951), transmasculine (n=1,364)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Overall mortality linked to mental health–related causes: Transgender and gender-diverse (TGD) adults had higher overall mortality than cisgender comparators, with 34–75% increased risk, largely driven by deaths from external causes, including suicide and accidental poisoning.
  • Suicide and homicide: TGD individuals were 3.34 times more likely to die from suicide or homicide than cisgender men (MRR 3.34, 95% CI 1.70–6.54) and 5.62 times more likely than cisgender women (MRR 5.62, 95% CI 2.65–11.91).
  • Accidental poisoning: Mortality from accidental poisonings was elevated, with TGD individuals 2.28 times higher than cisgender men and 5.20 times higher than cisgender women, suggesting overlap with substance-related and mental health vulnerabilities.
  • Sex-specific patterns: Transfeminine adults showed higher mortality from external causes compared with cisgender women (MRR 1.92), while transmasculine adults had an even higher relative risk (MRR 2.77 vs cisgender women).
  • Mental and behavioural disorder mortality: Deaths coded under mental and behavioural disorders were uncommon and not significantly elevated, indicating excess mental health burden is reflected primarily through external causes rather than psychiatric diagnoses on death certificates.
  • Highest-risk subgroup: TGD individuals with unknown sex assigned at birth (including many non-binary people) had the highest mortality risk, suggesting heightened vulnerability linked to marginalisation and unmet mental health needs.
  • Interpretive framing: Authors attribute excess suicide and external-cause mortality to minority stress, discrimination, and social exclusion, rather than intrinsic psychopathology.

Study aim

To assess guideline concordance in feminising and masculinising gender-affirming hormone therapy initiation within the Veterans Health Administration.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study; 2006–2018)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥20
SampleN=4,676; MtF (n=3,547), FtM (n=1,129)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depression: Highly prevalent across the cohort; 65.6% of veterans receiving feminizing GAHT and 62.5% receiving masculinizing GAHT had a documented diagnosis of depression.
  • Post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD): More common among those receiving masculinizing GAHT (43.0%) compared with feminizing GAHT (33.9%).
  • Military sexual trauma (MST): Positive screening was substantially higher in the masculinizing GAHT group (33.5%) than the feminizing GAHT group (16.8%).
  • Substance use disorders: Alcohol use disorder was reported in 12.7% (feminizing) and 14.2% (masculinizing); drug use disorder in 15.2% and 15.9%, respectively.
  • Suicide attempts: Documented suicide attempts were reported in 12.4% of those receiving feminizing GAHT and 10.8% of those receiving masculinizing GAHT.
  • Mental health service use: The majority had mental health visits either before or during GAHT initiation (≈83% prior to initiation; ≈81–82% during GAHT), with very few having no mental health contact (<4%).

Study aim

To examine associations between transgender identity and violence victimisation, substance use, suicide risk, and sexual risk behaviours among high-school students.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional school-based survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=131,901; transgender (1.8%), unsure (1.6%)
Validated toolsYRBS

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms: 53.1% of transgender students reported feeling sad or hopeless for ≥2 weeks in the past year, compared with 20.7% of cisgender males and 39.3% of cisgender females.
  • Suicidal ideation: 43.9% of transgender students reported seriously considering suicide in the past 12 months, versus 11.0% of cisgender males and 20.3% of cisgender females.
  • Suicide planning: 39.3% of transgender students reported making a suicide plan, compared with 10.4% of cisgender males and 16.0% of cisgender females.
  • Suicide attempts: 34.6% of transgender students reported attempting suicide in the past year, compared with 5.5% of cisgender males and 9.1% of cisgender females.
  • Medically treated attempts: 16.5% of transgender students reported a suicide attempt requiring medical treatment, versus 2.1% of cisgender males and 2.5% of cisgender females.

Study aim

To update the clinical characteristics and prevalence of gender dysphoria in Ireland and discuss service pathway needs.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review; 2005–2014)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–69
SampleN=218; MtF (72.9%), FtM (27.1%)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depression: A history of depression was reported in 34.4% of the cohort, making it the most common psychiatric comorbidity.
  • Self-harm and suicide attempts: Among those with depression, 25.3% reported a history of deliberate self-harm; overall, ~8.7% of the cohort had a documented history of self-harm or suicide attempt.
  • Severe mental illness: Diagnosed schizophrenia was present in 3.7%, and bipolar affective disorder in 2.3% of patients.
  • Neurodevelopmental conditions: 2.3% had a documented diagnosis of Asperger’s syndrome/autism spectrum condition.
  • Substance misuse: 3.7% reported damaging alcohol use; smoking was common but not categorised as a psychiatric diagnosis.
  • Mental health service engagement: 94.0% had attended at least one mental health professional prior to or during endocrine care; 6.0% had no documented mental health assessment.
  • Regret and psychological outcomes: Regret following hormonal or surgical treatment was low (1.8%), all occurring in transfeminine patients.
  • Overall pattern: Psychiatric comorbidity—particularly depression and self-harm—was common, supporting the need for structured and ongoing mental health involvement in care pathways.

Study aim

To describe demographics, referral pathways, and comorbidities among individuals attending an Irish gender service.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–30
SampleN=167; FTM/AFAB (62.3%), MTF/AMAB (35.3%), NB (2.4%)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Depression: 49.1% of young adults had a documented diagnosis of depression; an additional 15.6% had documented low mood.
  • Anxiety: 26.3% had a documented anxiety disorder.
  • Self-harm: 12.0% disclosed engaging in deliberate self-harm.
  • Suicidal ideation: 15.0% reported a history of suicidal ideation.
  • Suicide attempts: 3.0% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Other mental health conditions: Borderline personality disorder (4.2%), panic attacks (2.4%), eating disorders (1.2%), and OCD (1.2%) were also documented.
  • Neurodevelopmental comorbidity: 11.3% had a diagnosed autism spectrum disorder, with an additional 2.9% showing clinical features consistent with ASD.
  • Overall pattern: Mental health comorbidity was common in this young adult cohort, with high rates of depression, anxiety, self-harm, and suicidality alongside increased neurodiversity.

Study aim

To investigate health needs, indicators, and determinants among Polish non-binary individuals compared with transgender men and women.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 24.3)
SampleN=98; non-binary (n=53), binary trans (n=45)
Validated toolsDHEQ, SPP-25, CESD-R, RSES

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms (CES-D): Mean depressive symptom scores did not differ significantly between non-binary participants and transgender men/women after age adjustment.
  • Resilience (CD-RISC): Non-binary participants had significantly lower resilience scores than transgender men and women; resilience showed a strong inverse association with depressive symptoms in multivariable models.
  • Self-esteem (Rosenberg): After adjustment for stigma, resilience, and gender expression discomfort, non-binary identity was associated with higher self-esteem scores.
  • Stigma exposure: Expectations of rejection and vicarious trauma scores were significantly higher among non-binary participants and were positively associated with depressive symptoms.
  • Predictive modelling: In regression analyses, resilience and stigma variables were the primary predictors of depressive symptoms; gender identity category alone was not.

Study aim

To report clinical characteristics of children assessed for gender dysphoria and examine family–clinician dynamics during assessment.

DesignMixed-methods (clinical assessment and self-report questionnaires)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)8.4–15.9
SampleN=79; biological males (n=33), biological females (n=46)
Validated toolsDASS

Key findings

  • Psychiatric comorbidity: 88.6% of children had at least one comorbid mental health diagnosis or clinically significant symptoms.
  • Anxiety: Diagnosed in 63.3% of the cohort.
  • Depression: Diagnosed in 62.0% of children.
  • Self-harm: 49.4% had a history of self-harm; 16.3% were engaging in current self-harm.
  • Suicidal ideation and attempts: 41.8% reported past or current suicidal ideation; 10.1% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Other diagnoses: Eating disorders (2.5%) and psychosis (1.3%) were uncommon but present.

Study aim

To explore variation in mental health and psychosocial functioning by age, gender, dysphoria level, and informant type.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12–18
SampleN=149; AFAB (64.4%), AMAB (35.6%)
Validated toolsYSR, CBCL

Key findings

  • Overall mental health burden: Around 50% of adolescents reported clinically significant internalising problems on self-report measures, indicating substantial baseline psychological distress.
  • Depression: Clinically elevated depressive symptoms were reported by 30–39% of adolescents across informants.
  • Anxiety: 20–33% met clinical thresholds for anxiety problems.
  • Obsessive–compulsive symptoms: Elevated symptoms reported by 15–34%, higher on adolescent self-report than parent report.
  • Post-traumatic stress symptoms: Clinically significant PTSD symptoms were reported by 15–30% of participants.
  • Internalising problems by gender: Affirmed males had significantly higher internalising symptom scores than affirmed females, with moderate–large effect sizes.
  • Externalising problems: Externalising symptoms were less common, with clinical elevations in ~12–17% of participants.
  • Psychosocial functioning: Reduced overall competence was common, with 42–58% scoring in the clinically impaired range, driven mainly by low engagement in activities.
  • Informant differences: Adolescents consistently reported higher symptom severity than parents, though differences were generally small.

Study aim

To describe clinical experience with endocrine treatment of transsexual individuals over two decades.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 36.3)
SampleN=242; MtF (80%), FtM (20%)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Overall psychiatric morbidity: Psychiatric disease was present in 56.2% of patients, indicating a high burden of mental health comorbidity among individuals seeking hormone therapy.
  • Mood disorders: Mood disorders (including depression, dysthymia, adjustment disorder, and mania) were the most common psychiatric conditions, affecting 44% of the cohort.
  • Age-related patterning: Psychiatric morbidity was inversely correlated with age at presentation; mood disorders were less prevalent in those initiating hormone therapy before age 32 (36.3%) compared with those initiating after age 32 (51.7%, p = .011).
  • Disability and employment: 17% were on disability, with the majority attributed to mental health conditions, and 28% were unemployed or on disability overall.
  • Substance use: Drug or substance abuse was reported in 11% of patients, more commonly among transfeminine individuals.
  • Mental health and treatment progression: Among transfeminine patients, the presence of mood disorders was associated with significantly longer time to gender-affirming surgery, suggesting mental health burden may delay care pathways.
  • Overall pattern: Mental health difficulties—particularly mood disorders—were common and appeared to interact with social functioning, employment, and access to surgical care, supporting the importance of early and integrated mental health support.

Study aim

To assess health-related quality of life among transgender adults compared with the general population and examine socioeconomic correlates.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–64
SampleN=140; trans men (n=63), trans women (n=77)
Validated toolsSF-36 subscales

Key findings

  • Mental health–related quality of life: Transgender men reported significantly lower mental health–related QOL scores than the general male population, particularly for mental health and vitality domains.
  • Comparison by gender identity: Transgender women’s mental health–related QOL did not differ significantly from the general female population.
  • Within-group differences: After adjustment, no significant mental health differences were observed between transgender men and transgender women on SF-36 mental health or role-emotional subscales.
  • Hormone duration effects: Time since hormone initiation was not significantly associated with mental health subscale scores for either transgender men or women.
  • Surgical status: Mental health–related QOL did not differ between those who had undergone genital surgery and those who had not.
  • Socioeconomic factors: Lower education, unemployment, lower income, older age, and being single were each associated with worse mental health and role-emotional functioning.
  • Psychosocial vulnerability: Reduced mental health–related QOL appeared more strongly linked to social and economic disadvantage than to medical transition variables.

Study aim

To assess long-term mortality outcomes in transsexual individuals receiving cross-sex hormone therapy.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study; from 1975)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)16–76
SampleN=1,331; MtF (n=966), FtM (n=365)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Suicide mortality: Transfeminine participants had a significantly elevated suicide risk, with a standardised mortality ratio (SMR) of 5.70, indicating nearly six times higher suicide mortality compared with the general population.
  • External causes linked to mental health: Deaths due to suicide and illicit drug use contributed substantially to excess mortality; deaths from external causes were almost eightfold higher than expected.
  • Timing of suicides: No suicides occurred within the first two years of hormone treatment; suicides occurred after longer treatment durations (2–5 years, 5–10 years, and >10 years).
  • Psychiatric history: 76% of those who died by suicide had received psychiatric treatment prior to death, suggesting pre-existing or ongoing mental health needs.
  • Hormone treatment and suicide: No association was found between current cyproterone acetate use and suicide risk.
  • Transmasculine participants: No statistically significant increase in suicide or overall mental-health–related mortality was observed in transmasculine participants compared with the general population.

Study aim

To investigate veteran status, healthcare utilisation, and barriers to care among transgender women.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)26–79
SampleN=141; MtF transsexual (n=80), MtF cross-dresser (n=61)
Validated toolsSF-12

Key findings

  • Mental health functioning: Mental health–related quality of life was substantially reduced, with a mean SF-12 mental health score of 32.6 (SD 8.3), almost 2 standard deviations below population norms.
  • Comparison with non-veterans: Poor mental health functioning was also observed among non-veteran transgender participants (mean 34.6, SD 8.1), indicating distress was not specific to veteran status.
  • Mental health service use: 34.9% of transgender veterans accessed mental health services in the past year (VA 9.3%, non-VA 25.6%).
  • Conditions treated: Mental health care addressed gender identity counselling, depression, PTSD, anxiety, relationship difficulties, sleep problems, and grief/bereavement.
  • Depression: Treatment for depression was reported by 11.7% of transgender veterans (VA 4.7%, non-VA 7.0%).
  • PTSD: 4.7% received treatment for PTSD, exclusively within VA services.
  • Barriers to mental health care: Fewer barriers were reported for mental health care than medical care; concerns about provider reactions to gender identity or sexual orientation were endorsed by ~7%.
  • Overall pattern: Markedly reduced mental health functioning and moderate mental health service use suggest substantial unmet psychological needs, likely influenced by stigma, discrimination, and trauma exposure rather than veteran status alone.

Study aim

To assess prevalence and comorbidity of psychiatric and substance-use disorders among young transgender women.

DesignQuantitative (observational baseline study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–29
SampleN=298; transfeminine spectrum
Validated toolsMINI v6

Key findings

  • Any psychiatric or substance dependence diagnosis: 41.5% had ≥1 diagnosis; 20.1% had ≥2 comorbid diagnoses.
  • Depression: Lifetime major depressive episode 35.4%; current major depressive episode 14.7%.
  • Suicidality: 20.2% reported moderate/high suicidal risk in the past 30 days.

Study aim

To examine rates and predictors of self-harm and suicidality among children referred for gender dysphoria compared with controls.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)3–12
SampleN=2,778; gender-referred children, siblings, and controls
Validated toolsCBCL

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation: 19.1% of gender-referred children were rated by parents as talking about killing themselves, compared with 5.8% of siblings and 1.7% of non-referred children.
  • Self-harm / suicide attempts: 6.5% of gender-referred children were rated as deliberately self-harming or attempting suicide, compared with 2.2% of siblings and 0.2% of non-referred children.
  • Age effects: Suicidal ideation increased with age; by ages 10–12 years, approximately 30% of gender-referred children were rated as expressing suicidal ideation.
  • Comparative risk: After adjustment for age, gender-referred children were 5.1 times more likely than non-referred children to talk about suicide and 8.6 times more likely to self-harm or attempt suicide.
  • Behavioural correlates: General behavioural and emotional problems were strongly associated with suicidality; controlling for these reduced but did not eliminate elevated risk.
  • Peer relations: Poor peer relationships were associated with suicidality, but this association was no longer significant after accounting for overall behavioural problems.
  • Sibling risk: Siblings of gender-referred children also showed elevated risk, being 2.7–3.6 times more likely than non-referred children to show suicidal ideation or self-harm.
  • Overall pattern: A sizeable minority of gender-referred children demonstrated clinically significant suicidality, particularly in later childhood, supporting the need for routine screening and comprehensive mental health assessment.

Study aim

To assess psychopathology among hormone-treated transsexual individuals and compare outcomes with matched control groups.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=84; MtF (n=52), FtM (n=32)
Validated toolsSCL-90-R

Key findings

  • Overall psychopathological burden: 100% of transsexual participants reported significantly higher psychopathological symptom levels across all SCL-90-R subscales compared with healthy controls (all p < .001).
  • Depressive symptoms (SCL-90-R): Mean depression scores were significantly higher in transfeminine participants (0.85) compared with transmasculine participants (0.45) (p = .001), representing a medium effect size.
  • Anxiety and phobic anxiety: Transsexual participants reported significantly elevated anxiety and phobic anxiety symptoms compared with controls (all p < .001), with stronger sex effects observed when compared to phenotypic-sex–matched controls.
  • Paranoid ideation and interpersonal sensitivity: Significantly higher scores were observed across these domains in transsexual participants compared with healthy controls (all p < .001).
  • Global psychological distress (GSI): Overall psychological distress was significantly elevated in transsexual participants compared with controls (all p < .001), with transfeminine participants showing the highest mean GSI scores.
  • Clinical depression diagnosis: A documented lifetime diagnosis of depression was reported by 13.5% of transfeminine and 15.6% of transmasculine participants, with no significant difference between groups.
  • Psychopathological profile similarity: Transmasculine participants’ symptom profiles most closely resembled phenotypic male controls (r = .945), while transfeminine participants showed slightly greater similarity to phenotypic female controls (r = .698) than genetic-sex controls.
  • Overall pattern: Transsexual participants demonstrated a broad elevation in psychological distress, with depressive symptoms most pronounced among transfeminine individuals, supporting phenotypic-sex–matched controls as the most appropriate comparison group.

Study aim

To explore mental health and wellbeing, including suicidality, among transgender people in the UK.

DesignQualitative (large-scale survey with thematic analysis)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=889; female (39.9%), male (24.8%), non-binary/uncertain (32.1%)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation (lifetime): 84% of respondents reported having thought about ending their life.
  • Recent suicidal ideation: 63% reported suicidal thoughts in the past year, 27% in the past week, and 4% daily.
  • Suicide attempts (lifetime): 35% of the total sample reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Repeated suicide attempts: 25% reported more than one suicide attempt; 3% reported more than ten attempts.
  • Recent suicide attempts: 11% reported a suicide attempt within the past year.
  • Current suicide planning: 3.2% reported actively planning to attempt suicide; 11% were unsure about near-future intent.
  • Comparative risk: Trans participants were estimated to be up to seven times more likely to attempt suicide than the general population.
  • Impact of transition: 67% reported suicidal thoughts were more frequent before transition, compared with 3% reporting increased suicidality after transition.
  • Overall pattern: Very high levels of suicidality were observed, with marked reductions following social and/or medical transition.

Study aim

To examine associations between perceived discrimination and depression among low-income Latina transgender women.

DesignMixed-methods (survey and interviews)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)19–57
SampleN=220; MtF transgender women
Validated toolsPHQ-9

Key findings

  • Depression (PHQ-9): 35% of participants met criteria for moderately severe to severe depression (PHQ-9 ≥15).
  • Any depressive symptoms: 65% scored above minimal depression (PHQ-9 ≥10), indicating at least moderate depressive symptoms.
  • Suicidal ideation (recent): 32–33% reported thoughts of self-harm or that they would be better off dead within the previous two weeks.

  • Overall pattern: Depression and suicidality were highly prevalent in this low-income Latina transfeminine cohort and were strongly associated with discrimination and partner violence.

Study aim

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–79
SampleN=913; transgender (AMAB and AFAB)
Validated toolsHADS

Key findings

  • Possible anxiety disorder: 32.8% met criteria for a possible anxiety disorder.

Study aim

To investigate sociodemographic and clinical characteristics of older transgender people and effects of hormone therapy on psychopathology.

PopulationAdult
Age range (years)>50
SampleN=77; trans women (n=71), trans men (n=3)
Validated toolsHADS, Rosenberg Self-Esteem, Self-Injury Questionnaire

Key findings

Study aim

To examine mental health outcomes, victimisation, social support, and predictors of depression among transgender Australians.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–73
SampleN=255; AFAB (n=87), AMAB (n=168)
Validated toolsCES-D

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms (CES-D): 59% scored above the clinical threshold for depression.
  • Suicide attempts (lifetime): 44% reported at least one previous suicide attempt.
  • Attribution of suicide attempts: 62% of those who attempted suicide attributed this to their transgender status.
  • Alcohol problems: 30% reported problematic alcohol use.
  • Other substance problems: 19% reported a problem with non-alcohol substances.
  • Gender-related victimisation: 69% reported at least one instance of gender-related victimisation.
  • Economic discrimination: 34% experienced at least one form of economic discrimination.
  • Healthcare discrimination: 25% reported discrimination in healthcare settings.

  • Perceived social support: Mean perceived social support was significantly lower than population norms.
  • Predictors of depression: Lower perceived social support was the strongest predictor of depressive symptoms; gender-affirming surgery was associated with lower depression scores.
  • Overall pattern: Depression, suicidality, and victimisation were highly prevalent, with social support acting as a key protective factor.

Study aim

To examine differences in suicidality between transgender and cisgender individuals and potential explanatory factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional population survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–84
SampleN=533 transgender; plus cisgender comparison group
Validated toolsGHQ-5

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms (CES-D): 59.3% scored above the clinical threshold for depression.
  • Suicide attempts (lifetime): 43.6% reported having previously attempted suicide.
  • Attribution of suicide attempts: 62.3% of those who attempted suicide attributed this to their transgender status.
  • Alcohol problems: 29.6% reported having a problem with alcohol.
  • Other substance problems: 18.5% reported having a problem with non-alcohol substances.
  • Any gender-related victimisation: 68.6% reported at least one instance of gender-related victimisation.
  • Economic discrimination: 33.7% reported at least one form of economic discrimination.
  • Healthcare discrimination: 25.1% reported discrimination in healthcare settings.
  • Housing discrimination: 7.9% reported housing-related discrimination.
  • Perceived social support: Mean perceived social support was significantly lower than population norms.
  • Predictors of depression: Lower perceived social support was the strongest predictor of depressive symptoms; gender-affirming surgery was independently associated with lower depression scores.
  • Overall pattern: Depression, suicidality, and victimisation were highly prevalent, with social support acting as a key protective factor.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence of mood and anxiety disorder healthcare use by gender incongruence diagnosis and treatment status.

DesignQuantitative (nationwide registry-based cohort study; 2005–2015)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=9,747,324 population sample; n=2,679 with diagnosis of gender incongruence
Validated toolsNational Patient Register; Prescribed Drug Register

Key findings

  • Lifetime suicidal ideation: 36.1% of transgender participants reported having ever seriously considered suicide.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 15.5% reported at least one lifetime suicide attempt.
  • Past 12-month suicidal ideation: 13.3% reported suicidal ideation in the previous year.
  • Past 12-month suicide attempts: 1.9% reported a suicide attempt in the previous year.
  • Comparative risk (lifetime): Transgender participants had approximately fourfold higher odds of lifetime suicidal ideation and suicide attempts compared with cisgender individuals.
  • Comparative risk (past 12 months): Odds of suicidal ideation and attempts in the past year were 3–5 times higher among transgender participants than cisgender participants.
  • Depressive symptoms: 30.6% reported current elevated depressive symptoms, compared with 16.2% of cisgender participants.
  • Lack of social support: 24.0% reported lacking social support, compared with 13.2% of cisgender participants.
  • Exposure to discrimination: 37.5% reported recent experiences of discrimination.
  • Threats or violence: 10.0% reported exposure to threats of violence in the past year.
  • Mediation of suicidality disparity: Clinical mental health factors and lack of social support together explained over one-quarter of the increased suicidality risk among transgender participants.
  • Overall pattern: Suicidal ideation and attempts were substantially more prevalent among transgender individuals in this population-based sample, with disparities partially explained by depression, social support deficits, and minority stress exposures.

Study aim

To examine relationships between social support, coping strategies, depression, and anxiety among genderqueer individuals.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–60
SampleN=64; genderqueer
Validated toolsCES-D, BAI

Key findings

  • Clinically significant depression: 53% scored above the clinical cutoff for depressive symptoms.
  • Clinically significant anxiety: 39% scored above the clinical cutoff for anxiety.
  • Comparative prevalence: Depression and anxiety rates were higher than general population estimates.
  • Social support: Greater perceived social support was associated with lower depression and anxiety.
  • Avoidant coping: Avoidant coping was strongly associated with higher depression and anxiety.
  • Facilitative coping: Facilitative coping was associated with lower anxiety, but not depression.
  • Moderation: Low social support amplified anxiety risk among those using avoidant coping.
  • Overall pattern: Depression and anxiety were common, with social support and coping style acting as key modifiers.

Study aim

To explore associations between peer victimisation, gender nonconformity, health-risk behaviours, and depressive symptoms among Thai students.

DesignQualitative (self-administered school-based surveys)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)13–20
SampleN=NR; secondary school students (male and female)
Validated toolsCES-D

Key findings

  • Clinically significant depressive symptoms: 14.7% met the CES-D threshold for depression.
  • Depression by sex: 16.5% of female adolescents and 12.2% of male adolescents reported clinically significant depressive symptoms.
  • Gender non-conformity: Gender non-conforming girls had the highest prevalence of depressive symptoms among all gender groups.
  • Any peer victimisation (past month): 56.9% reported social victimisation, 53.6% physical victimisation, 52.1% verbal victimisation, and 30.0% sexual victimisation.
  • Sexual victimisation and depression: 30.0% of adolescents who experienced sexual victimisation had clinically significant depressive symptoms.
  • Victimisation dose–response: Higher frequency of social and sexual victimisation was associated with increasing prevalence of depressive symptoms.
  • Social and sexual victimisation: These forms showed the strongest association with depressive symptoms after adjustment for confounders.
  • Overall pattern: Depression was common among Thai adolescents and was strongly patterned by peer victimisation, particularly social and sexual victimisation, with heightened vulnerability among gender non-conforming youth.

Study aim

To evaluate psychiatric symptoms in transsexual individuals and changes following hormonal intervention.

DesignQuantitative (prospective clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 29.2)
SampleN=107; MtF (n=78), FtM (n=29)
Validated toolsSAS, SDS, SCL-90-R

Key findings

  • Any DSM-IV psychiatric comorbidity: 14% had a diagnosed Axis I psychiatric disorder at baseline.
  • Anxiety symptoms (pre-treatment): 50% had clinically significant anxiety symptoms before hormone therapy.
  • Depressive symptoms (pre-treatment): 42% had clinically significant depressive symptoms before hormone therapy.

  • Psychological distress (post-treatment): 11% reported clinically significant psychological symptoms after hormone therapy.
  • Functional impairment (post-treatment): 10% reported substantial functional impairment after hormone therapy.
  • Overall pattern: Subthreshold anxiety, depression, psychological distress, and functional impairment were common before treatment and significantly reduced following cross-sex hormone therapy.

Study aim

To investigate interpersonal problems among treatment-seeking transgender individuals and the role of depression.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 36.5)
SampleN=104; trans women (n=64), trans men (n=40)
Validated toolsSCL-90-R

Key findings

  • Depression (self-report): Trans participants had significantly higher depressive symptom scores than controls (SCL-90-R), indicating elevated depression burden.
  • Interpersonal vulnerability (global): Trans participants showed significantly greater interpersonal difficulties, particularly in sociability, supportiveness, and involvement with others.
  • Depression-adjusted effects: After controlling for depression, difficulties with sociability, supportiveness, and involvement remained significantly elevated.
  • Overall pattern: Social isolation, unemployment, and elevated depressive symptoms clustered with persistent interpersonal difficulties, indicating psychosocial vulnerability beyond depression alone.

Study aim

To examine a model linking gender dysphoria, perceived social support, and psychological wellbeing.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–72
SampleN=103; women (n=63), men (n=40)
Validated toolsSCL-90-R, GSI

Key findings

  • Elevated psychopathology: Trans participants had significantly higher psychological distress across all SCL-90-R domains than controls.
  • Depressive symptoms: Depression scores were significantly higher in the trans group than controls.
  • Perceived social support: Trans participants reported significantly lower overall perceived social support than controls.
  • Gender differences in support: Trans women reported lower total and family social support than control women; no significant differences were observed for trans men.
  • Depression-adjusted effects: Differences in perceived social support were no longer significant after controlling for depression.
  • Quality of life and life satisfaction: Perceived social support explained 22% of the variance in life satisfaction and was a significant predictor of mental health–related quality of life.
  • Overall pattern: Elevated psychological distress co-occurred with reduced perceived social support, particularly among trans women, with social support influencing quality of life more strongly than psychopathology.

Study aim

To assess prevalence of suicidal thoughts and behaviours in adolescents using self- and parent-report.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)>13
SampleN=2,771; AMAB and AFAB
Validated toolsCBCL, YSR

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation (CBCL, parent-report): 22.4–39.2% across clinics.
  • Suicidal behaviour (CBCL, parent-report): 8.6–50.8% across clinics.
  • Suicidal ideation (YSR, self-report): 27.3–55.3% across clinics.
  • Suicidal behaviour (YSR, self-report): 14.5–45.2% across clinics.
  • Sex differences: Suicidality was consistently higher among birth-assigned females.
  • Comparative context: Rates were similar to other clinically referred adolescents but substantially higher than non-referred peers.
  • Behavioural correlates: Suicidality was strongly associated with overall emotional and behavioural problems.
  • Overall pattern: A substantial minority of clinic-referred transgender adolescents reported suicidal ideation and behaviour.

Study aim

To examine associations between minority stress, coping styles, and suicidality among sexual and gender minority young adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–29
SampleN=1,432; cisgender sexual minority and gender minority individuals
Validated toolsInternalized Homonegativity Inventory, SCQ, Utrecht Coping List

Key findings

  • Lifetime suicidal ideation: 60.6% of sexual minority young adults and 82.0% of gender minority young adults reported lifetime suicidal ideation.
  • Past-year suicidal ideation: 28.9% of sexual minority and 49.8% of gender minority young adults reported suicidal ideation in the past year.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 17.2% of sexual minority and 36.1% of gender minority young adults reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Past-year suicide attempts: 3.5% of sexual minority and 10.2% of gender minority young adults reported a suicide attempt in the past year.
  • Victimisation exposure: A majority reported at least some lifetime homophobic or transphobic victimisation, with higher suicidality among those reporting low or high victimisation.
  • Stigma consciousness: Higher stigma consciousness was associated with increased suicidal ideation and attempts across both sexual and gender minority groups.
  • Coping style – active: Higher active coping was associated with lower likelihood of suicidal ideation and attempts.
  • Coping style – passive: Higher passive coping was associated with higher likelihood of suicidal ideation and attempts.
  • Coping style – avoidant: Avoidant coping showed mixed effects, acting as a risk factor for ideation in some models and a short-term buffer for attempts in others.
  • Overall pattern: Suicidal ideation and attempts were highly prevalent, particularly among gender minority young adults, and were strongly patterned by minority stress and coping style.

Study aim

To examine psychiatric comorbidity among adolescents diagnosed with gender identity disorder.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12–17
SampleN=105; natal males and natal females
Validated toolsDISC, WISC, CBCL

Key findings

  • Any psychiatric comorbidity (past year): 32.4% had at least one DSM-IV psychiatric diagnosis.
  • No psychiatric comorbidity: 67.6% had no concurrent psychiatric disorder.
  • Anxiety disorders: 21.0% met criteria for an anxiety disorder.
  • Mood disorders: 12.4% met criteria for a mood disorder.
  • Disruptive disorders: 11.4% met criteria for a disruptive behaviour disorder.
  • Major depression: 8.6% met criteria for major depressive disorder.
  • Social anxiety disorder: 9.5% met criteria for social anxiety disorder.
  • Multiple comorbidity: 15.2% had two or more psychiatric diagnoses; 2.9% had three or more.
  • Sex differences: Birth-assigned males had higher prevalence of mood disorders (20.8% vs 3.8%) and social anxiety (15.1% vs 3.8%) than birth-assigned females.
  • Eligibility timing: Psychiatric comorbidity was more common among adolescents with delayed eligibility for medical intervention.
  • Overall pattern: Most adolescents had no psychiatric comorbidity, but a substantial minority experienced anxiety, mood, or disruptive disorders, particularly birth-assigned males and those with delayed treatment eligibility.

Study aim

To analyse mental health outcomes among transgender individuals through the lens of minority stress and cognitive dissonance theories.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–71
SampleN=279; transgender participants (adult and adolescent subsamples)
Validated toolsCSI-15, DASS-21, RSES

Key findings

  • Clinically severe depression (DASS-21): 33.2% scored in the severe or extremely severe range.
  • Clinically severe anxiety (DASS-21): 35.4% scored in the severe or extremely severe range.
  • Clinically severe stress (DASS-21): 20.3% scored in the severe or extremely severe range.
  • Overall mental distress (DASS-general): 35.2% scored in the severe or extremely severe range.
  • Low self-esteem (RSES): 49.3% scored in the low self-esteem range.
  • Self-harm (lifetime): 49.8% reported a history of self-harm.
  • Suicidal ideation (lifetime): 76.2% reported having experienced suicidal ideation.
  • Suicide attempts (lifetime): 36.4% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Help-seeking after self-harm: 72.0% reported seeking mental health support following self-harm.
  • Help-seeking after suicidal ideation: 61.0% reported seeking mental health support.
  • Help-seeking after suicide attempt: 66.0% reported seeking mental health support.
  • Predictive model: Reduced self-esteem, school non-belonging, and exposure to violence together explained 53.2% of the variance in mental distress.
  • Overall pattern: Mental distress, suicidality, and low self-esteem were highly prevalent, with distress strongly patterned by minority stress, violence exposure, and internalised vulnerability.

Study aim

To examine prevalence of self-injurious thoughts and behaviours among transgender individuals with eating disorders.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional secondary data analysis)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (median 21)
SampleN=678; transgender (within total cohort of 365,749)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Past-year non-suicidal self-injury: 74.8% of transgender individuals with an eating disorder reported NSSI.
  • Past-year suicidal ideation: 75.2% reported suicidal ideation.
  • Past-year suicide attempts: 74.8% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Eating disorder prevalence (past year): 17.6% of transgender participants reported a diagnosed or treated eating disorder.
  • Comparative risk – NSSI: Rates were substantially higher than cisgender individuals with eating disorders and transgender individuals without eating disorders.
  • Comparative risk – suicidal ideation: Transgender individuals with eating disorders had significantly higher prevalence than all comparison groups.
  • Comparative risk – suicide attempts: Suicide attempt prevalence was markedly higher than cisgender individuals with eating disorders and transgender individuals without eating disorders.
  • Independence from depression: Elevated rates of self-injurious thoughts and behaviours persisted after adjustment for depression and demographic factors.
  • Overall pattern: The co-occurrence of transgender identity and eating disorders was associated with extremely high levels of self-injury and suicidality, indicating a compounding mental health risk.

Study aim

To assess prevalence of personality disorders among individuals with gender identity disorder compared with controls.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical interview study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 30.4)
SampleN=30; FtM (n=9), MtF (n=21)
Validated toolsSCID-II

Key findings

  • Any personality disorder: 66.7% of participants with GID met criteria for at least one DSM-IV Axis II personality disorder.
  • Comorbid personality disorders: 46.6% met criteria for more than one personality disorder.
  • Single personality disorder: 20.0% met criteria for exactly one personality disorder.
  • Paranoid personality disorder: 43.3% met criteria, significantly higher than cisgender controls.
  • Avoidant personality disorder: 26.7% met criteria, significantly higher than controls.
  • Borderline personality disorder: 33.3% met criteria overall.
  • Obsessive–compulsive personality disorder: 30.0% met criteria.
