  • Sex differences (trans men): 44.4% of trans men met criteria for paranoid personality disorder, significantly higher than cisgender women.
  • Overall pattern: Personality disorders—particularly paranoid and avoidant types and comorbid PDs—were common among adults with GID, with a more severe personality psychopathology profile observed in trans women.

Study aim

To assess psychopathological features among gender-dysphoric adolescents compared with non-referred peers.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean 16)
SampleN=46; MtF (n=20), FtM (n=26)
Validated toolsGIDYQ-AA, MAST, BUT, YSR

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation: Suicidal ideation was reported by 83.3–90.0% of gender-dysphoric adolescents, with no significant difference by affirmed gender.
  • Suicide attempts: Suicide attempts were reported by 11.1–16.7% of gender-dysphoric adolescents.
  • Overall psychological distress: 17.4% scored in the clinical range for total psychological problems, compared with 10.9% of non-referred peers.
  • Internalising problems: Clinically significant internalising difficulties were present in 47.8%, compared with 17.4% of non-referred adolescents.
  • Depression and anxiety: Gender-dysphoric adolescents showed significantly higher anxiety/depression and withdrawal/depression than non-referred peers.
  • Suicide risk profile: Elevated attraction to death and repulsion by life, alongside reduced attraction to life, indicated increased suicide risk.
  • Overall pattern: Markedly elevated suicidality and internalising mental health difficulties were observed, supporting routine suicide risk screening and comprehensive mental health assessment.

Study aim

To examine frequencies of self- and parent-reported suicidal thoughts and behaviours in children and adolescents with gender dysphoria.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)5.2–18.5
SampleN=392; AFAB (84%), AMAB (16%)
Validated toolsCBCL, YSR

Key findings

  • Self-harm / suicide attempts (parent-report, children): 6% of children were reported by parents to engage in self-harming behaviour or suicide attempts.
  • Suicidal ideation (parent-report, children): 16% of children were reported by parents to talk about killing themselves.
  • Self-harm / suicide attempts (parent-report, adolescents): 29% of adolescents were reported by parents to engage in self-harming behaviour or suicide attempts.

Study aim

To examine associations between transgender identity and severe suicidal ideation among Finnish adolescents.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional cohort study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)15–16
SampleN=1,425; majority cisgender, minority transgender
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Severe suicidal ideation (overall): 2.0% of adolescents in the general population reported severe suicidal ideation.
  • Transgender identity prevalence: 3.1% of adolescents identified as transgender or non-binary.
  • Severe suicidal ideation (transgender adolescents): 14.3% of transgender adolescents reported severe suicidal ideation.
  • Severe suicidal ideation (cisgender adolescents): 1.6% of cisgender adolescents reported severe suicidal ideation.
  • Relative risk: Transgender adolescents were approximately nine times more likely to report severe suicidal ideation than cisgender peers.
  • Depression association: Depression was strongly associated with severe suicidal ideation across all models.
  • Peer rejection: Bullying, social exclusion, and lack of close friends did not explain the elevated risk after adjustment.
  • Sex differences: Female natal sex was independently associated with higher risk of severe suicidal ideation.
  • Adjusted association: The association between transgender identity and severe suicidal ideation remained significant after controlling for depression, peer rejection, and sociodemographic factors.
  • Overall pattern: Transgender identity was independently associated with markedly elevated severe suicidal ideation in a population-based adolescent sample, not fully accounted for by depression or peer rejection alone.

Age range (years)NR
SampleN=579; FtM (n=349), MtF (n=230)
Validated toolsNone reported

Study aim

To examine differences in suicidality by gender identity among US adolescents after adjusting for known risk factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional school-based survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean 15.5)
SampleN=17,853; transgender (1.3%), gender questioning (1.4%), cisgender (97.3%)
Validated toolsYRBS

Key findings

  • Gender identity prevalence: 1.3% identified as transgender and 1.4% as gender questioning; 97.3% identified as cisgender.
  • Past-year suicidal ideation (adjusted): Gender questioning youth had significantly higher risk of past-year suicidal ideation compared with cisgender youth after full adjustment.
  • Past-year suicide attempts (adjusted): Transgender youth had significantly higher risk of past-year suicide attempts compared with cisgender youth.
  • Suicide attempts requiring treatment (adjusted): Transgender youth had significantly higher risk of suicide attempts requiring medical treatment.
  • Victimisation exposure: Gender minority youth experienced higher rates of school and dating-related victimisation than cisgender peers.
  • Sadness/hopelessness: Gender minority youth were more likely to report persistent sadness or hopelessness.
  • Substance use: Transgender youth reported higher recent use of tobacco, alcohol, and marijuana.
  • Adjusted models: Elevated suicide risk among gender minority youth persisted after controlling for victimisation, sadness/hopelessness, and substance use.
  • Overall pattern: Gender minority youth represented a small proportion of the population but experienced disproportionately elevated suicide risk, particularly for suicide attempts and attempts requiring treatment.

Study aim

To describe sociodemographic, psychiatric, and developmental characteristics of adolescents referred for gender reassignment in Finland.

DesignMixed-methods (clinical file review and interviews)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean 16)
SampleN=47; natal girls (n=41), natal boys (n=6)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Prior psychiatric treatment: 75% had previous or ongoing specialist child and adolescent psychiatric treatment for reasons other than gender dysphoria.
  • Depression: 64% had received psychiatric treatment for depression.
  • Anxiety disorders: 55% had received treatment for anxiety disorders.
  • Suicidal and self-harming behaviours: 53% had a history of suicidal ideation and/or self-harm.
  • Autism spectrum disorder: 26% had a diagnosed autism spectrum disorder.
  • ADHD: 11% had a diagnosis of ADHD.
  • Psychotic symptoms: 13% had a history of psychotic symptoms.
  • Conduct problems: 9% had conduct-related difficulties.
  • Substance misuse: 4% had substance-related problems.
  • Multiple comorbidity: The mean number of distinct psychiatric problems was 2.3, indicating frequent multimorbidity.
  • Temporal pattern: 68% first presented to psychiatric services for mental health difficulties before gender identity concerns emerged.
  • Overall pattern: Severe and multiple mental health difficulties were common and often preceded gender dysphoria, indicating that adolescent referrals frequently occurred in the context of broader psychopathology and developmental vulnerability.

Study aim

To compare psychopathology among adolescents referred to a gender identity clinic and a general adolescent psychiatric clinic.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean 16.2)
SampleN=84; birth-assigned males and females
Validated toolsICD-10

Key findings

  • No psychiatric diagnosis: 40.9% of gender-referred adolescents had no psychiatric diagnosis during adolescence.
  • Any mood disorder: 34.1% had a mood disorder diagnosis.
  • Any anxiety disorder: 25.2% had an anxiety disorder diagnosis.
  • Suicidal ideation / talk: 70.2% reported suicidal ideation or talk, significantly higher than mental-health-referred peers.
  • Suicide attempts: 10.7% reported a suicide attempt.
  • Self-harming behaviours: 61.4% reported self-harm, higher than mental-health-referred peers.
  • Depressive symptoms: 67.9% had depressive symptoms.
  • Anxiety symptoms: 90.5% reported anxiety symptoms.
  • Eating disorder symptoms: 16.0% reported eating-disorder symptoms, lower than mental-health-referred peers.
  • Alcohol abuse: 6.0% reported alcohol abuse, lower than mental-health-referred peers.
  • Externalising symptoms: Gender-referred adolescents showed significantly fewer externalising symptoms than mental-health-referred adolescents.
  • Family adversities: Gender-referred adolescents had fewer family adversities than mental-health-referred peers.
  • Overall pattern: Gender-referred adolescents showed high levels of internalising symptoms and suicidality, comparable to psychiatric outpatients, despite fewer diagnoses, fewer externalising problems, and fewer family adversities.

Study aim

Study aim

To examine how exposure to stigma, protective factors, and mental health change with age among gender- and sexually-diverse people.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)19–70
SampleN=518; transgender and gender diverse (AMAB, AFAB)
Validated toolsSES, SPP-25, DHEQ, CESD-R

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms: Transgender and gender diverse participants reported higher depressive symptoms than cisgender men.
  • Self-esteem: Self-esteem was lower among transgender and gender diverse participants.
  • Resilience: Resilience levels were lower among transgender and gender diverse participants.
  • Stigma exposure: Transgender and gender diverse participants reported greater minority stress and stigma.
  • Age effects: Depressive symptoms decreased with age, while self-esteem and resilience increased with age.
  • Stigma over time: Exposure to stigma did not decrease with age.
  • Overall pattern: Despite persistent stigma, mental health indicators improved with age, though transgender and gender diverse participants remained more vulnerable than cisgender men.

Study aim

SampleN=153; AFAB (n=28), AMAB (n=125)

Study aim

Study aim

To estimate frequency and determinants of suicide attempts among transgender and gender-diverse individuals.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)3–65
SampleN=6,327; transmasculine (45%), transfeminine (55%)
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Incident suicide attempts: 3.9% experienced a suicide attempt during follow-up.
  • By gender identity: 4.8% of transmasculine and 3.0% of transfeminine participants had a suicide attempt.
  • Age effects: Risk was highest under 18 and declined with increasing age.
  • Baseline risk: Prior suicidal ideation or attempts strongly predicted subsequent suicide attempts.
  • Mental health comorbidity: Risk increased with one or more baseline mental health diagnoses.
  • Overall pattern: Suicide attempt incidence was elevated, particularly among younger individuals and those with prior suicidality or multiple diagnoses.

Study aim

To compare autistic traits between hormone-naïve transgender and cisgender participants and examine change in autistic traits following gender-affirming hormone treatment.

DesignQuantitative (prospective cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥18
SampleN=789; trans men (n=206), trans women (n=182), cis controls
Validated toolsAQ, GIDYQ-AA, BUT, TAS-20, LSAS, SCL-90-R

Key findings

  • Autistic traits (AQ): Transgender participants showed higher autistic trait scores than cisgender women and lower than cisgender men, with scores well below clinical ASD cut-offs.
  • Alexithymia: Transgender participants reported significantly higher alexithymia than cisgender controls at baseline.
  • Social anxiety: Higher social anxiety levels were observed in transgender participants compared with cisgender groups.
  • General psychopathology: No significant group differences were found in overall psychopathology severity.
  • GAHT effects: After 12 months of GAHT, autistic traits, alexithymia, social anxiety, body uneasiness, and general psychopathology all significantly improved.
  • Mediation analysis: Improvements in alexithymia and social anxiety did not explain reductions in autistic trait scores.
  • Overall pattern: Autistic-like traits appeared to reflect gender dysphoria–related distress rather than ASD, with broad mental health improvement following GAHT.

Study aim

To compare psychosocial functioning at hormone-readiness assessment between TGD youth who received pubertal blockade and those who did not.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)13–17
SampleN=438; AMAB and AFAB
Validated toolsYSR

Key findings

  • Suicidal thoughts (blocked group): 12.5% reported suicidal thoughts at hormone readiness assessment.
  • Suicidal thoughts (non-blocked group): 27.2% reported suicidal thoughts.
  • Relative difference: Youth who received pubertal blockade were less likely to report suicidal thoughts than those who did not.
  • Internalising difficulties: Youth who received pubertal blockade showed lower internalising problems than non-blocked peers.
  • Depression and anxiety: Lower levels of depressive and anxiety problems were observed in the blocked group.
  • Overall pattern: Pubertal blockade initiated in early puberty was associated with better psychosocial functioning and reduced suicidality at hormone readiness assessment.

Study aim

To assess prevalence of personality disorders among Iranian patients with gender identity disorder.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 25.3)
SampleN=83; MtF (n=42), FtM (n=31)
Validated toolsMCMI-II

Key findings

  • Any personality disorder: 81.4% met criteria for at least one personality disorder.
  • Multiple personality disorders: The mean number of personality disorder diagnoses was 3.0 per person.
  • Narcissistic personality disorder: 57.1% met criteria, making it the most prevalent disorder.
  • Obsessive–compulsive personality disorder: 38.6% met criteria.
  • Masochistic–sadistic personality disorder: 34.3% met criteria.
  • Paranoid personality disorder: 25.7% met criteria.
  • Borderline personality disorder: 1.4% met criteria, making it the least prevalent.
  • Sex differences: Several personality disorders (schizoid, schizotypal, avoidant, passive–aggressive, dependent) were more common in biologically male participants.
  • Self-harm history: 5.7% reported a history of self-harm, with no significant association with personality disorder prevalence.
  • Overall pattern: Personality disorders were highly prevalent, dominated by narcissistic and other Cluster B traits, while borderline personality disorder was uncommon.

Study aim

To examine the association between gender dysphoria coding and hospitalisation for suicidality and/or self-harm.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional analysis of inpatient database)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)6–21
SampleN=2,117,296; n=6,627 with GD codes
Validated toolsNone reported

Key findings

  • Suicidality (2016): 36% of hospitalised youth with gender dysphoria had documented suicidality.
  • Suicidality (2019): 55% had documented suicidality, indicating a marked increase over time.
  • Self-harm (2016): 13% had documented self-harm.
  • Self-harm (2019): 15% had documented self-harm.
  • Suicidality or self-harm (2016): 41% were hospitalised with suicidality and/or self-harm.
  • Suicidality or self-harm (2019): 66% were hospitalised with suicidality and/or self-harm.

  • Age pattern: Elevated risk was observed in both children (6–12 years) and adolescents (13–21 years).
  • Overall pattern: Hospitalised youth with gender dysphoria showed very high levels of suicidality and self-harm, substantially exceeding rates in peers without gender dysphoria.

Study aim

To explore experiences of double stigma, internalised stigma, and coping strategies related to transphobia.

DesignMixed-methods (cross-sectional survey study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)21–50
SampleN=55; MtF (n=24), FtM (n=20), gender diverse (n=11)
Validated toolsISMI, ITS, SS, TSS, CSI

Key findings

  • Unemployment: 29% were unemployed, over three times the national average at the time of data collection.
  • Outpatient mental health service use: 73% were receiving outpatient mental health services.
  • Psychiatric medication use: 50.9% were taking psychiatric medication.
  • Employment status: 58% were employed at the time of the study.
  • Internalised stigma and employment: Employed participants reported higher internalised transphobia than unemployed participants.
  • Coping and stigma: Greater use and effectiveness of coping strategies were associated with lower internalised and external stigma.
  • Medication and coping: Participants taking psychiatric medication reported more effective coping with stigma.
  • Overall pattern: Mental health service use was common, with employment linked to higher internalised stigma, and coping strategies playing a key protective role against stigma-related distress.

Study aim

To examine associations between gender identity conflict/affirmation and major depression across the life course among transgender women.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective life-course study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)19–59
SampleN=571; transgender women (MtF)
Validated toolsMINI Plus (DSM-IV MDD)

Key findings

  • Major depression: Major depression was substantially more prevalent than in the general population across multiple life stages.
  • Gender identity conflict: Greater gender identity conflict was strongly associated with higher odds of major depression across adolescence and adulthood.
  • Gender identity affirmation: Gender identity affirmation was consistently protective against major depression across life stages.
  • Life-course effects: Increases in conflict predicted subsequent onset of major depression, while increases in affirmation predicted reduced depression over time.
  • Relationship context: Family and peer relationships were most influential earlier in life, while long-term partners became most influential in adulthood.
  • Robustness: Associations persisted after adjustment for age, relationship availability, disclosure, ethnicity, sexual orientation, and hormone use.
  • Overall pattern: Major depression among transgender women appeared closely tied to social responses to gender identity, supporting a relational and minority-stress pathway rather than intrinsic psychopathology.

Study aim

To examine disparities in suicidal ideation between transgender and non-transgender youth and test depression and victimisation as mediators.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional school-based survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean ~15)
SampleCHKS N=576,380 (7,653 transgender); CSS subsample N=25,493 (280 transgender)
Validated toolsSingle-item SI; single-item depression

Key findings

  • Past-year suicidal ideation (transgender youth): 33.7% reported suicidal ideation.
  • Past-year suicidal ideation (non-transgender youth): 18.9% reported suicidal ideation.
  • Comparative disparity: Suicidal ideation was nearly twice as prevalent among transgender youth compared with non-transgender peers.
  • Depressive symptoms: 44.6% of transgender youth reported persistent sadness or hopelessness in the past year.
  • School-based victimisation: Transgender youth reported substantially higher levels of school-based victimisation than non-transgender youth.
  • Mediation effects: Depression accounted for ~18% and victimisation ~15% of the disparity in suicidal ideation.
  • Key correlates (transgender youth): Depressive symptoms and victimisation were the strongest predictors of suicidal ideation.
  • Overall pattern: Transgender youth showed markedly elevated suicidal ideation, partially explained—but not fully accounted for—by depression and school-based victimisation.

Study aim

To examine whether transgender children aged 9–10 report elevated self-injurious thoughts and behaviours compared with cisgender peers.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional analysis of cohort study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)9–10
SampleUnweighted N=7,171 (58 transgender, 7,113 cisgender); weighted N=4,941,862
Validated toolsK-SADS-5 suicide module

Key findings

  • Current non-suicidal self-injury: 10.2% of transgender children reported self-injury in the past two weeks, compared with 2.3% of cisgender children.
  • Lifetime non-suicidal self-injury: 11.3% of transgender children reported lifetime self-injury, compared with 4.0% of cisgender children.

  • Lifetime suicidal behaviour: 19.0% of transgender children reported lifetime suicidal behaviour, compared with 2.0% of cisgender children.
  • Overall pattern: Markedly elevated self-injury, suicidal ideation, and suicidal behaviour were evident in transgender children as young as 9–10 years, indicating early emergence of mental-health disparities.

  • Clinically significant anxiety symptoms: 47.0% scored above the BAI clinical cut-off.
  • Depression by gender identity: 65.3% of transfeminine and 60.8% of transmasculine participants met the depression cut-off.
  • Anxiety by gender identity: 42.7% of transfeminine and 51.4% of transmasculine participants met the anxiety cut-off.
  • Internalised transphobia (alienation): Higher alienation was positively associated with both anxiety and depression.
  • Internalised transphobia (shame): Higher shame was positively associated with depression.
  • Resilience: Higher resilience was associated with lower depressive symptoms.
  • Overall pattern: Clinically significant depression and anxiety were highly prevalent, with minority stress processes strongly linked to mental health burden.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence of suicidal ideation among patients with gender identity disorder referred to a legal medicine office in Ilam Province.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)17.5–20
SampleN=21; noted as female (AMAB/AFAB unclear)
Validated toolsBeck's Scale

Key findings

  • Any suicidal ideation: 100% of participants scored above zero on the Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation.
  • Low-risk suicidal ideation: 19.1% were classified as low risk.
  • High-risk suicidal ideation: 71.4% were classified as high risk.
  • Very high-risk suicidal ideation: 9.5% were classified as very high risk.
  • Age effects: Higher age was associated with greater severity of suicidal ideation.
  • Education effects: Higher educational attainment was associated with higher suicidal ideation scores.
  • Overall pattern: Suicidal ideation severity was extremely high, even in a sample without psychiatric comorbidity, indicating substantial baseline suicide risk in individuals seeking legal confirmation of gender transition.

Study aim

To examine protective factors, violence exposure, and health and wellbeing factors among transgender and non-binary people compared with cisgender people.

Age range (years)14–25
SampleN=782; transgender and non-binary (AMAB/AFAB unclear; youth/adult Ns not disaggregated)

Key findings

Study aim

To examine mental healthcare needs, mental healthcare use, and perceived barriers to mental healthcare among transgender adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)22–79
SampleN=130; 49% MtF transsexual, 36% MtF crossdresser, 7% FtM, 3% gender nonconformist, 4% gender questioning, 1% intersex
Validated toolsSF-12, SUBS

Key findings

  • Psychological distress (SF-12): 52% scored in the distressed range indicating need for mental health services.
  • Mental health service use (past year): 37% accessed treatment for mental health problems other than gender identity counselling.
  • Unmet mental health need: 52% had clinically significant distress but did not access mental health services in the past year.
  • Depression treatment: 20% received treatment for depression or excessive sadness in the past year.
  • Anxiety treatment: 19% received treatment for anxiety, worry, or panic attacks.
  • PTSD treatment: 9% received treatment for post-traumatic stress disorder.
  • Cost as a barrier: 42% endorsed cost of services as a barrier to accessing mental health care.
  • Prior negative experiences: 32% reported that hearing about others’ bad experiences deterred them from seeking care.
  • Stigma-related concerns: 18–22% endorsed fears of being seen as weak, flawed, or less competent.
  • Overall pattern: Over half of participants showed clinically significant psychological distress, with substantial unmet mental health need driven by cost, stigma, and prior negative healthcare experiences.

Study aim

To identify and characterise clinical and sociodemographic profiles among individuals with gender dysphoria awaiting gender-affirming surgery using cluster analysis.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–53
SampleN=100; trans women (n=55), trans men (n=45)
Validated toolsDASS-21, DERS, RRS, SWLS

Key findings

  • Past psychiatric diagnosis: 43.6% reported a lifetime psychiatric diagnosis.
  • Current psychiatric diagnosis: 17.0% had a current psychiatric diagnosis.
  • Psychiatric medication use: 22.2% were taking at least one psychotropic medication.
  • Suicidal ideation (lifetime): 75.8% reported a history of suicidal ideation.
  • Suicide planning (lifetime): 52.5% reported having made a suicide plan.

Study aim

Age range (years)NR (mean 31.6)
SampleN=125; natal males and natal females
Validated toolsClinical assessment

Key findings

  • Self-harm behaviours: 24% had engaged in deliberate self-harm prior to referral.
  • Suicide attempts: 10% had made at least one suicide attempt prior to attending the service.

Study aim

To assess feasibility of clinic-based sampling methods for examining health disparities between transgender and cisgender patients.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey; methodological comparison)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–70
SampleFull cohort N=2,653 (31 transgender, 2,622 cisgender); nested subsample N=155 (31 transgender, 124 cisgender); single “transgender” option (no breakdown)
Validated toolsBrief self-report screening

Key findings

  • Lifetime suicidal ideation: 58.1% of transgender patients reported having ever seriously thought about suicide.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 29.0% reported at least one suicide attempt.

  • Smoking history: 54.8% reported ever smoking cigarettes.

  • Comparative burden: Suicidal ideation and suicide attempts were substantially more prevalent among transgender patients than cisgender patients.

Study aim

To examine longitudinal associations between gender abuse, depressive symptoms, and contextual factors with substance use among transgender women.

DesignQuantitative (prospective cohort study; 2004–2007)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)19–59
SampleN=230; male-to-female transgender spectrum
Validated toolsCES-D

Key findings

  • Any substance use (past 6 months): 76.2% reported use of at least one substance at baseline.
  • Heavy alcohol use: 48.4–60.4% reported heavy alcohol use across assessment points.
  • Cannabis use: 29.1–40.0% reported cannabis use across assessment points.

Study aim

To identify factors distinguishing transgender/GNC adolescents with no self-harm, NSSI only, and NSSI plus suicide attempt.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional school-based survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)14–17
SampleN=1,635; transgender/genderqueer/genderfluid/unsure
Validated toolsPHQ-2, detailed questionnaires on self-harm/suicide

Key findings

  • Any past-year self-harm: 51.6% reported self-harm in the previous year.
  • Non-suicidal self-injury only: 33.6% reported NSSI without suicide attempt.
  • NSSI with suicide attempt: 18.0% reported both NSSI and a suicide attempt.
  • Mental health problem: 68% of those with NSSI only and 86% of those with NSSI plus suicide attempt reported a mental health problem lasting ≥6 months.
  • Depression (positive screen): 69% of the NSSI-only group and 79% of the NSSI plus suicide attempt group screened positive for depression.
  • Suicide attempts (ever): 18.0% reported at least one suicide attempt in the past year.
  • Physical or sexual abuse: 57% of adolescents with NSSI plus suicide attempt reported a history of physical or sexual abuse.
  • Bullying victimisation: 80% of those with NSSI plus suicide attempt reported recent bullying victimisation.
  • Running away from home: 38% of those with NSSI plus suicide attempt reported running away in the past year.
  • Substance use (alcohol): 42% of those with NSSI plus suicide attempt reported alcohol use in the past 30 days.
  • Overall pattern: Over half of transgender/GNC adolescents reported recent self-harm, with very high rates of depression, mental health problems, abuse exposure, and suicidality, particularly among those who both self-injured and attempted suicide.

Study aim

To describe somatic health, pubertal development, psychosocial background, and surgical interest among adolescents with gender dysphoria seeking GAHT.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective clinical record review)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)16–17
SampleN=124; FtM and MtF transgender adolescents
Validated toolsClinical diagnosis reports

Key findings

  • Depression: 29.0% had a diagnosed depressive disorder at referral.
  • Anxiety disorders: 19.4% had a diagnosed anxiety disorder.
  • Antidepressant use: 25.6% were using antidepressant medication.
  • Any psychiatric comorbidity: ~33% had at least one diagnosed psychiatric condition.

Study aim

To compare mental wellbeing of TGNC adolescents in China with cisgender peers.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR (mean 15.8)
SampleN=12,108; cisgender, transgender, non-binary, questioning (incl. AMAB/AFAB subdivisions)
Validated toolsPHQ-9, GAD-7, Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index, self-harm/suicide risk questionnaire

Key findings

  • Past-month self-harm thoughts: 27–34% of TGNC students reported thoughts of self-harm, compared with 11–15% of cisgender students.
  • Past-month deliberate self-harm: 20–23% of TGNC students reported deliberate self-harm, compared with 9–13% of cisgender students.
  • Past-month suicidal ideation: 27–32% of TGNC students reported suicidal ideation, compared with 11–15% of cisgender students.
  • Past-month suicide planning: 8–15% of TGNC students reported making a suicide plan, compared with ~3% of cisgender students.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 10–15% of TGNC students reported a suicide attempt, compared with 4% of cisgender students.
  • School bullying (past year): 16–19% of TGNC students reported being bullied at school, compared with ~9% of cisgender boys and ~5% of cisgender girls.
  • Depressive symptoms (PHQ-9): TGNC students reported significantly higher depressive symptom levels than cisgender peers.
  • Anxiety symptoms (GAD-7): TGNC students reported significantly higher anxiety symptom levels than cisgender peers.
  • Sleep problems: TGNC students reported poorer sleep quality than cisgender students.
  • Overall pattern: TGNC adolescents showed consistently elevated self-harm, suicidality, depression, anxiety, sleep problems, and bullying, with disparities evident across all TGNC subgroups.

Study aim

To examine self-rated health, disability, and quality of life among trans people and identify associated predictors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional web survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)15–94
SampleN=796; gender nonbinary, trans masculine, trans feminine, transvestites
Validated toolsQoL measurement

Key findings

  • Poor self-rated health: 18% of respondents reported poor or very poor general health.
  • Self-reported disability: 53% reported a physical or mental condition that impaired daily functioning or work ability.
  • Reduced quality of life: 44% reported quality-of-life scores below the sample median.
  • Negative healthcare experiences: 69% reported at least one negative healthcare experience related to being trans.
  • Gender nonbinary vulnerability: Gender-nonbinary respondents showed significantly higher likelihood of poor health and disability compared with trans feminine respondents.
  • Legal gender recognition: Inability to access legal gender recognition was associated with poorer health and lower quality of life.
  • Social support: 26% reported lacking someone to confide in; reduced social support was associated with poorer health and lower quality of life.
  • Overall pattern: High levels of disability and reduced quality of life were common, with structural factors (healthcare experiences, legal recognition, social support) strongly linked to poorer mental health outcomes.

Study aim

To characterise health indicators among non-binary adults using secondary analysis of a non-probability sample.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional secondary analysis)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–75
SampleN=452; binary (n=267) vs non-binary (n=185)
Validated toolsCES-D-10; single-item internalized transphobia; lifetime diagnoses; AUDIT-C

Key findings

  • Lifetime self-harm: 50.2% of non-binary adults reported lifetime self-harm, compared with 46.8% of binary transgender adults.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 31.9% of non-binary adults reported a suicide attempt, similar to 33.7% of binary adults.
  • Recent depressive distress: 25.9% of non-binary adults met criteria for clinically significant depressive symptoms in the past 7 days, compared with 22.1% of binary adults.
  • Lifetime depression diagnosis: 46.0% of non-binary adults reported a diagnosis of depression, compared with 47.9% of binary adults.
  • Lifetime anxiety diagnosis: 37.8% of non-binary adults reported an anxiety diagnosis, compared with 44.9% of binary adults.
  • Mental health treatment (past year): 41.3% of non-binary adults accessed mental health treatment in the past 12 months, compared with 48.3% of binary adults.
  • Hazardous alcohol use: 44.7% of non-binary adults screened positive for hazardous alcohol use, compared with 38.3% of binary adults.
  • Overall pattern: Non-binary adults demonstrated high prevalence of self-harm, suicidality, depressive distress, and hazardous alcohol use, alongside lower engagement with mental health services compared with binary transgender peers.

PopulationYouth
Age range (years)10–16
SampleN=21; referred for GD; 13 AMAB, 8 AFAB
Validated toolsClinical interviews and record review

Key findings

  • Adolescents with anxiety and/or depression: 100% of adolescents assessed for hormone treatment reported symptoms of anxiety and/or depression.
  • Suicidal ideation: 100% of adolescents assessed for hormone treatment reported suicidal ideation.

Study aim

To compare mental health, substance use, and sexual risk behaviours between rural and non-rural transgender adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥18
SampleN=1,299; trans women (rural n=214, non-rural n=478), trans men (rural n=130, non-rural n=393)
Validated toolsBSI-18, Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale

Key findings

  • Lifetime suicide attempts (trans women): 25–27% reported a prior suicide attempt, with no significant difference between rural and non-rural participants.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts (trans men): 38–41% reported a prior suicide attempt, with similarly high prevalence in rural and non-rural groups.
  • Clinically significant psychological distress (trans men): Rural trans men showed higher overall psychological distress than non-rural trans men.
  • Depressive symptoms (trans men): Rural trans men reported higher levels of depressive symptoms than non-rural trans men.
  • Somatic symptoms (trans men): Rural trans men reported higher somatization symptoms than non-rural trans men.
  • Self-esteem (trans men): Rural trans men reported lower self-esteem than non-rural trans men.
  • Mental health (trans women): No significant rural–urban differences were observed in depression, anxiety, self-esteem, or suicidality among trans women.
  • Overall pattern: High lifetime suicide attempt prevalence was observed across all groups, with rural residence conferring additional mental health vulnerability for trans men, but not for trans women.

Study aim

To examine associations between psychological wellbeing and outness among transfeminine transsexual adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)25–73
SampleN=105; MtF transsexuals
Validated toolsBDI-II, BAI, Rosenberg Self-Esteem, Bem Sex Role Inventory, Outness Scales

Key findings

  • Ever experienced trans-related violence: 10.5% reported having been a victim of violence due to being transgender.
  • Never experienced trans-related violence: 89.5% reported no history of trans-related victimisation.
  • Not out at all: 13.3% reported being completely non-disclosed about their transgender identity.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence of mental health problems and suicide risk among TGNC young adults and examine correlates of psychiatric symptoms.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 19.6)
SampleN=89,342; TGNC n=2,352 within total sample; identities include “trans man”, “trans woman” (others not specified here)
Validated toolsPHQ-9, GAD-7, TSQ, SBQ-R, self-reported NSSI

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms: 71.0% of TGNC and 66.5% of cisgender LGB students reported clinically elevated depressive symptoms, compared with 47.3% of cisgender heterosexual peers.
  • Anxiety symptoms: 56.9% of TGNC and 51.8% of cisgender LGB students reported elevated anxiety, compared with 33.9% of cisgender heterosexual students.

Study aim

To test whether self-focused attention mediates the association between community connectedness and depression among TNB young adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=309; multiple identity options (trans man/trans masculine; trans woman/trans feminine; non-binary; genderqueer; agender; Two-Spirit; questioning; self-describe)
Validated toolsPHQ-9, GMSR, GRRS

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms (PHQ-9): Depression severity was high overall in this TNB young adult sample, but prevalence at diagnostic thresholds was not reported.
  • Community connectedness: Community connectedness was not directly associated with depressive symptom severity.
  • Gender rumination: Higher gender-related rumination was strongly associated with greater depressive symptoms.
  • Preoccupation with others’ perceptions: This was strongly associated with depression and mediated 35.9% of the relationship between community connectedness and depression.
  • Gender reflection: Gender reflection showed a weak positive association with depression in bivariate analyses and was not a significant mediator in multivariable models.
  • Overall pattern: Depression among TNB young adults appeared to be driven less by community connectedness itself and more by maladaptive self-focused attention, particularly rumination and hypervigilance about others’ perceptions.

Study aim

To identify risk factors for current psychiatric comorbidity among adults with gender identity disorder in Japan.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=326; MtF and FtM
Validated toolsDSM-IV clinical interviews

Key findings

  • Any current psychiatric comorbidity: 17.8% of the total sample had at least one current Axis I psychiatric disorder.
  • Psychiatric comorbidity by gender identity: 24.0% of transfeminine individuals had psychiatric comorbidity, compared with 15.0% of transmasculine individuals.

Study aim

PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=500; MtF and FtM

Key findings

  • Lifetime self-mutilation (including suicide attempts): 31.8% reported a history of self-mutilation or suicide attempt.

  • Self-mutilation among those with suicidal ideation: 43.3% of those with suicidal ideation reported self-mutilation.
  • Self-mutilation without suicidal ideation: 2.1% reported self-mutilation in the absence of suicidal ideation.
  • Overlap of behaviours: 97.9% of individuals with self-mutilation had experienced suicidal ideation.
  • Education gradient (self-mutilation): 50.0% among those with junior-high education, 34.3% with high-school education, and 18.2% with university-level education.
  • Overall pattern: Very high prevalence of suicidal ideation and substantial self-mutilation were observed even in patients without psychiatric comorbidity, with lower education strongly associated with self-mutilation risk.

Study aim

To examine disparities across the spectrum of suicidality between transgender and cisgender adolescents and examine within-group risk among transgender subgroups.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)14–18
SampleN=2,020; cis boys/girls; trans boys (AFAB), trans girls (AMAB), non-binary (AFAB/AMAB), questioning
Validated toolsYRBS and C-SSRS adapted items

Key findings

  • Passive death wish (lifetime): 94.3% of transgender adolescents reported ever wishing they were dead, compared with 77.0% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Suicidal ideation (lifetime): 84.8% of transgender adolescents reported suicidal ideation, compared with 60.4% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Suicide planning (lifetime): 72.5% of transgender adolescents reported having made a suicide plan, compared with 49.8% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Suicide attempts (lifetime): 50.3% of transgender adolescents reported at least one suicide attempt, compared with 31.4% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Suicide attempts requiring medical care: 13.2% of transgender adolescents reported an attempt requiring medical treatment, compared with 5.6% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Non-suicidal self-injury (lifetime): 86.9% of transgender adolescents reported a history of NSSI, compared with 59.1% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Highest-risk subgroups: Transgender males (88.6% ideation; 54.5% attempts) and transgender females (90.5% ideation; 57.1% attempts) showed the highest suicidality prevalence.
  • Overall pattern: Transgender adolescents demonstrated substantially elevated suicidality across the full spectrum, with particularly high burden among transgender males and females.

Study aim

To compare mental health symptoms and social support between treatment-seeking binary and non-binary transgender youth.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–25
SampleN=388; binary transgender (n=331), non-binary transgender (n=57)
Validated toolsHADS, RSE, SIQ-TR

Key findings

  • Non-binary identification: 14.7% of participants identified as non-binary, while 85.3% identified as binary.
  • History of non-suicidal self-injury (non-binary): 61.4% reported a history of NSSI.
  • History of non-suicidal self-injury (binary): 58.3% reported a history of NSSI.

Study aim

To examine changes in depression, anxiety, and suicidality over the first 12 months of gender-affirming care and assess associations with initiation of puberty blockers or gender-affirming hormones.

DesignQuantitative (prospective longitudinal cohort study; 12-month follow-up)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)13–20
SampleN=104; transmasculine (60.6%), transfeminine (26.0%), non-binary/gender fluid (9.6%), unknown/missing (3.8%)
Validated toolsPHQ-9, GAD-7, PHQ-9 item 9

Key findings

  • Moderate–severe depression (baseline): 56.7% met criteria for moderate or severe depression at intake.
  • Moderate–severe anxiety (baseline): 50.0% met criteria for moderate or severe anxiety at intake.
  • Self-harm or suicidal thoughts (baseline): 43.3% reported self-harm or suicidal thoughts in the prior 2 weeks at baseline.
  • Receipt of puberty blockers or hormones (12 months): 66.3% had initiated puberty blockers, gender-affirming hormones, or both within 12 months.
  • Depression over follow-up: Youths who initiated puberty blockers or hormones showed lower prevalence of moderate–severe depression over time compared with those who had not initiated treatment.
  • Suicidality over follow-up: Youths who initiated puberty blockers or hormones showed lower prevalence of self-harm or suicidal thoughts over time compared with those who had not initiated treatment.

Study aim

To examine the association between gender-related discrimination and psychological distress and test whether family support, community connectedness, gender literacy, or transgender activism moderates this relationship.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional interview-administered survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–87
SampleN=330; transgender man, transgender woman, non-binary, genderqueer, gender nonconforming (youth/adult Ns not disaggregated)
Validated toolsBSI-18

Key findings

  • Psychological distress (overall): Psychological distress occurred at 2.6–4.0 times the rate observed in general U.S. community norms.
  • Anxiety: 39.7% of participants scored in the clinical range for anxiety symptoms.
  • Depression: 34.2% met the clinical threshold for depression; prevalence was higher among transfeminine individuals (40.5%) than transmasculine individuals (28.0%).
  • Somatization: 26.4% reported clinically significant somatic symptoms.
  • Discrimination exposure: Greater exposure to gender-related discrimination was associated with higher psychological distress.
  • Family support: Higher levels of family support were associated with lower psychological distress.
  • Community connectedness: Transgender community connectedness showed a weak and non-significant association with distress after adjustment.
  • Gender literacy and activism: Higher gender literacy and greater engagement in transgender activism were each associated with higher psychological distress.
  • Overall pattern: A substantial proportion of transgender adults experienced clinically significant anxiety, depression, and somatic distress, with psychological burden strongly patterned by discrimination exposure and family support, and mixed associations observed for community and identity-related factors.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence of mental health concerns among Canadian transgender youth and compare these with population-based estimates, including subgroup comparisons by gender identity and age.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional online survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–25
SampleN=923; trans girls/women, trans boys/men, non-binary
Validated toolsAdapted BCAHS and CCHS measures

Key findings

  • Poor or fair overall mental health: ~75% rated their mental health as poor or fair; ~25% reported good or excellent mental health.
  • Self-harm (past year): 74.9% of 14–18-year-olds and 48.0–60.7% of 19–25-year-olds reported self-harm at least once in the past year.
  • Suicidal ideation (past year): 65.2% of 14–18-year-olds and 36.9–46.2% of 19–25-year-olds seriously considered suicide in the past year.
  • Suicide attempts (past year): 36.1% of 14–18-year-olds and 8.8–11.4% of 19–25-year-olds reported at least one suicide attempt in the past year.

  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 37.8% of 19–25-year-olds reported having attempted suicide.
  • Major depressive episode (past year, ages 19–25): 71.1% met criteria for a major depressive episode in the past year.

Study aim

To compare psychological functioning between adult and adolescent transsexuals and examine differences by sex, sexual orientation, and age using MMPI measures.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)13–65
SampleN=376 total; adults N=293 (18–65), adolescents N=83 (13–18); MtF and FtM
Validated toolsMMPI-2 and MMPI-A

Key findings

  • Adults with ≥2 clinical-range MMPI scales: 62.8% of adults scored in the clinical range on two or more MMPI clinical scales.
  • Adults with ≥4 clinical-range scales: 35.5% of adults scored in the clinical range on four or more clinical scales.
  • Adolescents with ≤1 clinical-range scale: 67.5% of adolescents scored in the clinical range on none or only one MMPI scale.
  • Adolescents with ≥4 clinical-range scales: 16.9% of adolescents scored in the clinical range on four or more scales.
  • Adult paranoia (clinical range): 49.8% of adults scored in the clinical range for paranoia, compared with 18.1% of adolescents.
  • Adult psychasthenia (clinical range): 36.9% of adults scored in the clinical range for psychasthenia, compared with 13.3% of adolescents.
  • Adult depression (clinical range, MtF): 31.4% of transfeminine adults scored in the clinical range for depression.
  • Adult depression (clinical range, FtM): 19.8% of transmasculine adults scored in the clinical range for depression.
  • Overall pattern: Adults with gender identity disorder demonstrated a substantially higher burden of clinically significant psychological distress than adolescents, with over one-third exhibiting multi-domain psychopathology, while the majority of adolescents showed minimal or no clinical-range psychological dysfunction.

Study aim

To examine suicide risk in transgender adolescents and identify associated sociodemographic and familial factors.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical interview study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR (mean 27.5)
SampleN=141; transsexual/transgender individuals
Validated toolsSemi-structured interview

Key findings

  • Lifetime suicidal ideation: 55.3% reported having thought about suicide at some point in their lives.
  • Current suicidal ideation: 9.2% reported suicidal thoughts at first psychiatric assessment.
  • Lifetime suicide attempts: 29.8% reported at least one suicide attempt.
  • Age at suicide attempt: 76.7% of suicide attempts occurred before age 21.
  • Suicidal ideation (by gender): 55.6% of transmasculine and 54.8% of transfeminine participants reported lifetime suicidal ideation.
  • Suicide attempts (by gender): 29.3% of transmasculine and 31.0% of transfeminine participants reported a suicide attempt.
  • Family attitudes toward sexuality (restrictive): 69.2% reported current suicidal thoughts and 44.9% reported lifetime suicidal ideation when family attitudes toward sexuality were restrictive.
  • Family attitudes toward sexuality (supportive/moderate): 7.7–23.1% reported current suicidal thoughts and 21.8–33.3% reported lifetime suicidal ideation when family attitudes were supportive or moderate.
  • Overall pattern: Over half of transgender individuals reported lifetime suicidal ideation and nearly one-third reported suicide attempts, with most attempts occurring in adolescence and markedly higher suicidality associated with restrictive family attitudes toward sexuality, while rates were similar across transmasculine and transfeminine participants.

Study aim

To describe and compare clinically referred adolescents with gender identity disorder versus transvestic fetishism regarding demographics, behavioural problems, and psychosexual characteristics.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical comparative study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=329; adolescents referred for GID or transvestic fetishism; stratified by assigned sex and sexual orientation
Validated toolsClinical assessment protocols for behavior problems and psychosexual measures

Key findings

  • Overall Psychological Distress: High levels of behavioural and emotional difficulties were observed across all groups, with mean scores comparable to or exceeding those seen in clinically referred youth.
  • Internalising Difficulties: Elevated internalising symptoms (e.g. anxiety, depression, social withdrawal) were common, with male adolescents with gender identity disorder showing significantly higher internalising scores than other groups.
  • Externalising Difficulties: Externalising problems (e.g. aggression, hyperactivity) were also elevated across groups, though differences between groups were less pronounced than for internalising symptoms.
  • Clinical-Range Symptoms: A substantial proportion of adolescents across all groups scored within the clinical range for total behavioural problems on parent- and self-report measures.
  • Peer Relationship Difficulties: Poor peer relations were highly prevalent, including high rates of teasing, social rejection, and difficulties getting along with peers.
  • Self- vs Parent-Report: Parent-reported difficulties were generally more severe than self-reported difficulties, though both indicated clinically meaningful levels of distress.
  • Comorbidity Considerations: Behavioural and emotional difficulties appeared alongside gender-related distress, suggesting overlapping psychosocial and mental health needs.
  • Overall Pattern: Adolescents referred for gender-related concerns or transvestic behaviours showed consistently high levels of internalising distress and peer difficulties, with mental health profiles resembling or exceeding those of other clinically referred youth, highlighting substantial psychosocial vulnerability.

DesignQuantitative (register-based longitudinal cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,812 transgender persons; 38,120 matched controls; identified via ICD-10 (AFAB/AMAB stratified; non-binary not identifiable)

Key findings

Study aim

To examine changes over time (1996–2019) in psychiatric needs among people seeking gender reassignment in Finland versus matched controls and by gender reassignment status.

DesignQuantitative (register-based longitudinal follow-up study; 1996–2019)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,665 gender service contacts; 29,292 matched controls; identified via ICD-10 (registered sex stratified; non-binary not identifiable)
Validated toolsSpecialist-level psychiatric treatment contacts; ICD-9/10 psychiatric diagnoses

Key findings

  • Any specialist-level psychiatric treatment (ever): 71.5% of individuals contacting gender identity services had a history of specialist-level psychiatric treatment, compared with 24.2% of matched population controls.
  • Psychiatric inpatient treatment (ever): 21.8% had a history of psychiatric inpatient care, compared with 6.0% of controls.
  • Psychiatric treatment before first GIS contact: 33.0% had received specialist psychiatric treatment prior to contacting gender identity services, compared with 13.7% of controls.
  • Psychiatric treatment after GIS contact: 60.6% required specialist-level psychiatric treatment after contacting gender identity services, compared with 14.5% of controls.
  • Psychiatric diagnoses: Mood disorders (33.6–43.4%) and anxiety disorders (21.9–30.4%) were the most common diagnoses recorded in psychiatric care.
  • Medical transition and psychiatric care: 52.9% of those who proceeded to medical gender reassignment required subsequent psychiatric treatment, compared with 66.7% of those who did not.
  • Overall pattern: Individuals seeking gender reassignment showed markedly elevated and increasing psychiatric treatment needs compared with the general population, with high levels of mood and anxiety disorders and persistent psychiatric care requirements regardless of whether medical gender reassignment occurred.

Study aim

To explore experiences of autistic transgender and/or non-binary adults seeking gender identity healthcare and identify barriers and recommendations for improvement.

DesignQualitative (semi-structured interview study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–46
SampleN=17; transgender (n=10), non-binary (n=3), both (n=4)
Validated toolsQualitative self-report during interviews

Key findings

  • Anxiety and distress: Participants described heightened anxiety and emotional distress linked to uncertainty, unclear processes, and fear of care being denied or withdrawn.
  • Gatekeeping-related distress: Many reported suppressing discussion of mental health difficulties due to concern that disclosure would be misinterpreted and used to restrict access to gender-affirming care.
  • Misdiagnosis and invalidation: Autistic traits were frequently misinterpreted as psychiatric pathology, contributing to distress, delayed care, or service exclusion.
  • Sensory-related stress: Overstimulating clinical environments and disruption to routines were described as exacerbating anxiety and emotional dysregulation.
  • Communication burden: Limited communication options and poor responsiveness from services increased stress and feelings of being unsupported.
  • Structural stressors: Long waiting lists, bureaucratic complexity, and insurance barriers were described as having a sustained negative impact on mental well-being.
  • Overall pattern: Autistic transgender and non-binary adults reported significant mental health burden driven by structural, sensory, and gatekeeping-related barriers, with distress arising less from gender identity itself and more from how services were organised, delivered, and interpreted autistic embodiment.

Study aim

To compare mental healthcare diagnoses, visits, and psychotropic medication use among TGD youth versus cisgender siblings and examine changes following initiation of gender-affirming pharmaceutical treatment.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,754 TGD youth; 6,603 cis siblings; military health system cohort (adult subset post-treatment not disaggregated)
Validated toolsICD-9/10 diagnostic codes; HCUP classifications; psychotropic medications

Key findings

  • Any mental health diagnosis: TGD youth were more likely to have a mental health diagnosis than cisgender siblings (89.3% vs 50.1%; adjusted OR 5.45).
  • Mood/depressive disorders: Mood or depressive disorders were most common among TGD youth (64.3% vs 18.9%; adjusted OR 6.12).
  • Anxiety disorders: Anxiety disorders were more prevalent in TGD youth than siblings (50.8% vs 18.4%; adjusted OR 3.30).
  • Suicidal ideation/self-harm: TGD youth had markedly higher rates of suicidal ideation or self-harm (18.2% vs 2.5%; OR 7.45).
  • Psychotic disorders: Psychotic disorders were more frequently diagnosed in TGD youth than siblings (9.7% vs 1.6%; OR 5.38).
  • Mental healthcare utilisation: TGD youth had higher mental healthcare use (mean 5.5 vs 3.1 visits/year; adjusted IRR 2.22).
  • Psychotropic medication use: Psychotropic medication use was higher among TGD youth (75.1% vs 37.7%; adjusted IRR 2.57).
  • Post–gender-affirming pharmaceuticals: Mental healthcare use did not significantly change after initiation, while psychotropic medication days increased (adjusted IRR 1.67).
  • Overall pattern: TGD youth experienced substantially higher mental health burden and service use than cisgender siblings across diagnoses, care utilisation, and medication prescribing, with needs persisting following initiation of gender-affirming pharmaceutical treatment.

Study aim

To examine long-term psychological functioning and wellbeing outcomes in young adults who received puberty suppression in adolescence followed by hormones and surgery.

DesignQuantitative (longitudinal cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)11.1–22.8
SampleN=55 longitudinal cohort; intake 11.1–17.0; follow-up 19.5–22.8; trans women (n=22), trans men (n=33)
Validated toolsUGDS, BIS, CGAS, BDI, STAI, TPI, CBCL/ABCL, YSR/ASR

Key findings

  • Psychological functioning: Global functioning improved significantly from adolescence to young adulthood (CGAS 71.1 to 79.9; p < .001).
  • Depressive symptoms: Mean depression scores declined over time (BDI 7.9 to 5.4), with large associations between reduced depression and later wellbeing (r ≈ –0.60 to –0.80).
  • Anxiety symptoms: Trait anxiety showed modest reductions across follow-up (STAI 39.6 to 37.6), with greater reductions associated with better quality of life (r ≈ –0.58 to –0.64).
  • Emotional and behavioural problems: Internalising and externalising problems decreased significantly, with the proportion scoring in the clinical range dropping from 30% at baseline to 7% in young adulthood.
  • Gender dysphoria (psychological distress): Gender dysphoria scores reduced markedly from adolescence to young adulthood (UGDS 53.5 to 15.8; p < .001).
  • Overall mental health pattern: Across long-term follow-up, participants showed substantial reductions in depression, anxiety, and clinical-range psychological problems, with overall psychological functioning in young adulthood comparable to the general population.

Study aim

To examine trajectories of depressive symptoms, emotional health, and suicidality over 24 months among TNB youth initiating gender-affirming medical care beginning with puberty suppression.

DesignQuantitative (prospective longitudinal observational study; 24-month follow-up)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)8–16
SampleN=94 analytic baseline (n=59 at 24 months); transgender and non-binary youth (detailed categories not enumerated)
Validated toolsBDI-Y, NIHTB-EB, YRBS items, CBCL

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms: Depression scores remained stable and largely non-clinical over 24 months; 72% were in the average range at baseline and 71% at follow-up, with no significant worsening over time.
  • Suicidality: Suicidal ideation decreased from 12% at baseline (11/94) to 8% at 24 months (5/59); suicide plans reduced from three cases to none, and attempts decreased from two to one.
  • Emotional health: No significant changes were observed across emotional health domains (e.g. loneliness, rejection, self-efficacy), with mean scores remaining below clinical concern thresholds.
  • Behavioural and emotional problems: Parent-reported internalising and externalising problems remained stable and below clinical cut-offs; a small but significant reduction in rule-breaking behaviour was observed.
  • Age effects: Older baseline age was associated with higher baseline depression and internalising symptoms, but did not predict worsening mental health trajectories.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Over 24 months, youth receiving puberty suppression showed stable mental health with low and non-worsening rates of depression, suicidality, and behavioural problems, suggesting prevention of mental health deterioration over time.

Study aim

To examine etiological factors and psychiatric comorbidities associated with gender dysphoria, with a focus on personality disorders.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional clinical study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–72
SampleN=255; “transsexual” M→F and F→M (non-binary not included)
Validated toolsPICI-1

Key findings

  • Personality disorder traits: 97.5% of participants were reported to have at least five dysfunctional personality traits within Cluster B (e.g. borderline, narcissistic, histrionic, antisocial).
  • Anxiety- and mood-related traits: 56.6% were reported to have at least three dysfunctional traits within Cluster A, including anxious, obsessive, depressive, phobic, and somatic traits.
  • Psychiatric comorbidity framing: High prevalence of anxiety, depressive, obsessive, phobic, post-traumatic, and personality-related psychopathological traits was reported based on clinical interview data.
  • Age-related reporting: Early recognition of gender dysphoria was common, with 100% of F→M and 84.1% of M→F participants reporting awareness before age 36, without reported mental health stratification.
  • Overall mental health pattern: The study reports very high prevalence of psychopathological traits, particularly personality-related traits, within the sampled population.

Study aim

To characterise longitudinal trajectories of self-reported gender identity among SGM youth and assess associations between gender identity variability and depressive symptoms over time.

DesignQuantitative (longitudinal cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–21
SampleN=366 SGM youth; cisgender, binary transgender, genderqueer/non-binary (assessed longitudinally)
Validated toolsBDI-Y

Key findings

  • Depressive symptoms (overall levels): Across all waves, participants reported mild depressive symptoms on average (mean BDI-Y score 13.6), remaining broadly stable over time.
  • Gender identity variability: 18.3% of youths reported a change in gender identity over approximately 3.5 years; frequency of gender identity variability was not associated with levels of depressive symptoms (β = 2.43, p = .33) or within-person changes in depression over time (β = 0.23, p = .75).
  • Baseline group differences: Youths transitioning from cisgender to TGD reported higher depressive symptoms at baseline compared with consistently cisgender youths (β = 4.66, p = .03), but this difference was no longer significant after adjusting for exposure to LGBT violence (β = 3.31, p = .16).
  • Trajectories over time: No gender identity trajectory group differed in the rate of change of depressive symptoms across follow-up; depressive symptom trajectories were stable across all groups.
  • Role of victimisation: Cumulative exposure to LGBT violence was significantly associated with higher depressive symptoms over time (β = 2.59, p < .001), accounting for observed group differences.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Changes or variability in gender identity were not associated with worsening depressive symptoms over time; observed differences in depression were largely explained by exposure to LGBT-related violence rather than gender identity trajectories themselves.

Study aim

To compare parent-reported gender variance rates among youth with ASD, ADHD, other neurodevelopmental disorders, and non-referred controls.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)6–18
SampleN=554 clinically referred + 1,605 normative controls; gender variance via CBCL parent item (no self-ID categories)
Validated toolsCBCL: Anxious/Depressed, Withdrawn/Depressed subscales

Key findings

  • Prevalence of ASD: 22.5% of trans and gender-diverse young people reported a diagnosis of autism spectrum disorder.
  • Psychiatric diagnoses: Trans young people with ASD were significantly more likely to report prior psychiatric diagnoses than those without ASD, including depression (OR 4.87), anxiety disorders (OR 8.00), PTSD (OR 7.97), personality disorders (OR 10.96), psychosis (OR 16.89), eating disorders (OR 6.82), and substance use disorders (OR 12.67).
  • Current depressive symptoms: Moderate to moderately severe depressive symptoms were more common in the ASD-diagnosed group (OR 1.47).
  • Current anxiety symptoms: Severe anxiety symptoms were more prevalent among those with ASD (OR 2.18).
  • Self-harm behaviours: Lifetime self-harm was more frequently reported in the ASD-diagnosed group (OR 1.74).
  • Suicidal ideation: Lifetime suicidal thoughts were more common among trans youth with ASD (OR 1.72).
  • Suicide attempts: Lifetime suicide attempts were reported more frequently by the ASD-diagnosed group (OR 1.66).
  • Risk-taking behaviours: Reckless behaviours intended to put life at risk were more prevalent among those with ASD (OR 1.89).
  • Overall mental health pattern: Trans and gender-diverse young people with co-occurring ASD experienced substantially higher mental health burden, including elevated psychiatric diagnoses, anxiety, depression, self-harm, and suicidality, compared with their non-ASD peers.

Study aim

To examine prevalence of ASD diagnoses among TGD young people and compare mental health, self-harm/suicidality, negative experiences, and access to care by ASD status.

DesignMixed-methods (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–25
SampleN=859 TGD young people; open-text gender; ASD subgroup: male/trans male (38.8%), female/trans female (19.4%), non-binary (41.8%)
Validated toolsSelf-reported psychiatric diagnoses; PHQ-A; GAD-7

Key findings

  • Internalising problems (pre-treatment): Transgender adolescents at referral showed higher internalising problems than cisgender peers (mean 11.67 vs 9.71; d = 0.24), with 31.3% scoring in the clinical range compared with 22.9% of cisgender adolescents.
  • Self-harm and suicidality (pre-treatment): Suicidality was more frequently endorsed among adolescents at referral (27.2%) compared with cisgender peers (11.9%).
  • Peer relation difficulties (pre-treatment): Adolescents at referral reported poorer peer relations than cisgender peers (mean 1.08 vs 0.41; d = 0.62).
  • Internalising problems (puberty suppression): Adolescents receiving puberty suppression had lower internalising scores than those at referral (mean 7.76 vs 11.67; d = 0.52) and a lower clinical-range prevalence (16.3%).
  • Self-harm and suicidality (puberty suppression): Suicidality rates among adolescents receiving puberty suppression (12.4%) were similar to cisgender peers (11.9%) and lower than those at referral.
  • Externalising problems: No significant differences in externalising problems were observed between groups; clinical-range externalising scores were comparable across transgender and cisgender adolescents.
  • Comparison with cisgender peers (post-treatment): Adolescents receiving puberty suppression showed similar or fewer emotional and behavioural problems compared with cisgender adolescents on internalising and suicidality measures.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Transgender adolescents demonstrated elevated internalising problems, suicidality, and peer difficulties prior to treatment; following initiation of gender-affirmative care involving puberty suppression, mental health outcomes were comparable to or better than those of cisgender peers from the general population.
