Study aim

To compare health status and healthcare experiences between autistic and allistic transgender and non-binary people.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥14
SampleN=2,873 total (analytic n=2,858); man/boy, woman/girl, Indigenous cultural genders (e.g., Two-Spirit), non-binary/genderqueer/agender
Validated toolsSelf-rated mental health item (excellent → poor)

Key findings

  • Self-rated mental health: Fair or poor mental health was reported by 68.0% of diagnosed autistic and 72.4% of self-identified autistic TNB participants, compared with 53.1% of allistic participants.
  • Mental health disparity (adjusted): Compared with allistic participants, self-identified autistic individuals had substantially higher odds of reporting fair or poor mental health (AOR 5.73), while diagnosed autistic participants also had elevated odds (AOR 1.79).
  • Unmet mental health needs: Unmet health care needs (including mental health care) were reported by 54.8% of diagnosed autistic and 60.9% of self-identified autistic participants, compared with 42.6% of allistic participants.
  • Negative mental health care experiences: Autistic participants more frequently reported negative experiences with mental health providers, including repeated misgendering (≈19–21% vs 13.6%) and providers being unable to separate mental health concerns from TNB identity (≈14–19% vs 11.1%).
  • Disclosure avoidance during assessments: Among those undergoing mental health or readiness assessments for gender-affirming care, 25.5% of diagnosed and 36.1% of self-identified autistic participants avoided disclosing autism due to concerns about access to care.
  • Provider knowledge burden: Autistic participants more often reported needing to educate mental health providers about TNB-related needs (25.9% diagnosed; 40.0% self-identified vs 26.8% allistic).
  • Overall mental health pattern: Autistic transgender and nonbinary individuals experienced significantly poorer self-rated mental health, higher unmet mental health needs, and more negative mental health care experiences than allistic peers, with the greatest disparities observed among self-identified autistic individuals.

Study aim

To identify social, medical, and transition-related factors associated with reduced past-year suicide ideation and attempts and estimate potential population-level impacts.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥16
SampleN=433 recruited; n=380 with past-year suicide outcomes; MtF spectrum, FtM spectrum, gender-fluid/third-gender
Validated toolsSelf-reported past-year suicide ideation and suicide attempts; self-reported diagnosis of major MH disorders

Key findings

  • Suicidal ideation (past year): 35.1% of transgender participants reported serious consideration of suicide in the past year.
  • Suicide attempts (past year): 11.2% reported at least one suicide attempt in the past year.
  • Social support: High overall social support (90th vs 10th percentile) was associated with a 49% reduction in suicide ideation (RR 0.51) and an 82% reduction in attempts among those with ideation (RR 0.18).
  • Parental support: Strong parental support for gender identity was associated with reduced suicide ideation (RR 0.43), corresponding to a potential prevention of 17% of ideation cases at the population level.
  • Transphobia: Lower transphobia exposure (10th vs 90th percentile) was associated with a 66% reduction in suicide ideation (RR 0.34) and a further 76% reduction in suicide attempts among those with ideation (RR 0.24).
  • Identity document concordance: Having at least one identity document concordant with lived gender was associated with reduced suicide ideation (RR 0.56) and attempts (RR 0.26).
  • Medical transition: Among those who desired medical transition, completion was associated with reduced suicide ideation (RR 0.38); among those with ideation, being “in process” was associated with increased attempt risk (RR 2.91).
  • Overall mental health pattern: Transgender people experienced very high rates of recent suicidal ideation and attempts; lower suicide risk was strongly associated with modifiable social and structural factors—particularly reduced transphobia, strong parental support, identity document concordance, and access to completed medical transition—highlighting suicide risk as highly sensitive to social and policy environments.

Study aim

To examine short-term physical, psychological, and safety outcomes of GnRHa treatment in young people with persistent and severe gender dysphoria.

DesignQuantitative (prospective uncontrolled observational study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12.0–15.3
SampleN=44; GnRHa cohort (gender identity recorded; analysis by birth-registered sex; categories not enumerated)
Validated toolsCBCL, YSR, self-harm indices

Key findings

  • Overall psychological functioning: Parent-reported CBCL total problem scores showed no significant change from baseline to 12, 24, or 36 months, indicating stable psychological functioning during puberty suppression.
  • Internalising and externalising problems: CBCL and YSR internalising and externalising scores remained stable across follow-up, with no significant changes over time.
  • Self-harm and suicidal ideation: Parent- and self-reported self-harm indices showed no significant change over 36 months, with consistently low median scores.
  • Clinician-rated functioning: CGAS scores showed small, non-significant increases over time, suggesting stable to slightly improved global functioning.
  • Mood experiences (self-reported): At follow-up interviews, around half reported improved mood, with smaller proportions reporting mixed or negative changes.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Across three years of puberty suppression, adolescents showed stable mental health with no evidence of deterioration, alongside modest improvements in some self-reported mood experiences.

Study aim

To compare all-cause and suicide mortality among youth referred to gender identity services with matched controls and assess roles of psychiatric morbidity and gender reassignment in mortality.

DesignQuantitative (nationwide register-based cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)8–22
SampleN=2,083 gender-referred; 16,643 matched controls; identified via ICD-10 (registered sex stratified; non-binary not identifiable)
Validated toolsSpecialist-level psychiatric treatment contacts as proxy for severe psychiatric morbidity

Key findings

  • All-cause mortality: All-cause mortality did not significantly differ between gender-referred adolescents and matched controls (0.5% vs 0.3%); rates were 0.81 vs 0.40 per 1,000 person-years.
  • Suicide mortality (unadjusted): Suicide deaths were more frequent among gender-referred adolescents than controls (0.3% vs 0.1%); suicide mortality rates were 0.51 vs 0.12 per 1,000 person-years.
  • Suicide mortality (adjusted for psychiatric morbidity): After adjusting for specialist-level psychiatric treatment, suicide mortality no longer differed between groups (HR 1.8, 95% CI 0.6–4.8).
  • Role of psychiatric morbidity: Increasing intensity of specialist psychiatric care strongly predicted suicide mortality, with highest risk among those with ≥101 psychiatric contacts (HR 11.1).
  • Sex differences: Male registered sex was associated with higher suicide mortality risk (adjusted HR 3.8).
  • Impact of gender-affirming treatment: Suicide mortality did not differ between those who proceeded to gender-affirming treatment and those who did not once psychiatric morbidity was accounted for (GR+ HR 0.8; GR− HR 3.2; both non-significant vs controls).
  • Overall mental health pattern: Suicide mortality among gender-referred adolescents was rare and, once psychiatric morbidity was accounted for, did not differ from the general population; psychiatric morbidity—not gender dysphoria or gender-affirming treatment—was the primary predictor of suicide risk.

Study aim

To compare chronic pain incidence in transgender versus cisgender people and examine associations with GAHT, anxiety, depression, and chronic pain diagnoses.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=98,352 transgender (56,470 trans men; 41,882 trans women) plus cis comparisons; non-binary not distinguishable
Validated toolsICD-10 diagnoses (MDD F33.x; GAD F41.1) as exposure variables

Key findings

  • Depression (prevalence): Major depressive disorder was more prevalent among transgender and gender-diverse individuals than cisgender cohorts, particularly among those receiving gender-affirming hormone therapy.
  • Depression as a risk factor: Depression was associated with increased risk of adverse health outcomes, including higher hazard of chronic pain among transgender women (HR 1.45) and transgender men (HR 1.35).
  • Anxiety (prevalence): Generalised anxiety disorder was more common in transgender cohorts than cisgender comparators.
  • Anxiety as a risk factor: Anxiety was associated with increased risk of adverse outcomes, including chronic pain among transgender women (HR 1.65) and transgender men (HR 1.48).
  • Comparative impact: The strength of associations between depression/anxiety and outcomes was similar across transgender and cisgender groups.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Depression and anxiety were common and acted as strong predictors of adverse health outcomes in transgender and gender-diverse individuals, indicating mental health morbidity as a key driver of burden.

Study aim

To examine whether TGD individuals have higher rates of autism diagnoses, autistic traits, and related diagnoses than cisgender individuals in large non-clinic cohorts.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional multi-cohort analysis)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥15 (varies)
SampleN=641,860 across 5 datasets; identity ascertainment varied (trans/non-binary/gender diverse where available)
Validated toolsSelf-reported professional diagnoses (autism, ADHD, depression, bipolar, OCD, schizophrenia, learning disorder)

Key findings

  • Autism diagnosis: Transgender and gender-diverse individuals were significantly more likely to report an autism diagnosis than cisgender individuals (adjusted ORs across datasets ranged from ~3.0 to 6.4).
  • Depression: Rates of depression were elevated among transgender and gender-diverse individuals compared with cisgender individuals, with effect sizes comparable to those observed for autism (OR ~3.5).
  • ADHD: Transgender and gender-diverse individuals had higher odds of ADHD compared with cisgender individuals (ORs generally ~3–4 across datasets).
  • Bipolar disorder: Bipolar disorder was more prevalent among transgender and gender-diverse individuals than cisgender individuals (ORs ~3–4).
  • Obsessive–compulsive disorder: Elevated rates of OCD were observed among transgender and gender-diverse individuals compared with cisgender individuals (ORs ~2–3).
  • Schizophrenia: Schizophrenia diagnoses were markedly elevated in one large dataset (OR >20), though estimates were attenuated after excluding autistic individuals and were not consistently replicated across datasets.
  • Relative contribution of conditions: In multivariable models, autism and depression showed the strongest independent associations with transgender and gender-diverse identity, with other psychiatric conditions showing smaller or attenuated effects.
  • Overall mental health pattern: Transgender and gender-diverse individuals exhibited substantially higher prevalence of multiple psychiatric diagnoses—particularly autism, depression, and ADHD—compared with cisgender individuals, indicating a broad elevation in mental health burden rather than condition-specific risk.

Study aim

To estimate age at first experience of gender dysphoria among adults seeking genital surgery and quantify years lived with untreated gender dysphoria.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–68+
SampleN=210; trans women (n=155), trans men (n=55)
Validated toolsSelf-reported history

Key findings

  • History of depression and anxiety: Over 90% of transgender men and women reported a lifetime history of depression, anxiety, or both.
  • Current depression/anxiety: 53% of transgender men and 46% of transgender women reported currently experiencing depression, anxiety, or both at time of surgical consultation.
  • Suicide attempts: A history of at least one suicide attempt was reported by 18% of transgender men and 30% of transgender women; mean number of lifetime attempts was approximately two.
  • Suicidal ideation (current): Current suicidal ideation was uncommon at presentation (2% of transgender men; 8% of transgender women).
  • Change following transition-related care: Among those with prior suicide attempts, only 10–27% reported ongoing suicidal ideation after initiating hormone therapy or social transition, with no further suicide attempts reported.
  • Overall mental health pattern: This cohort showed a very high lifetime burden of depression, anxiety, and suicidality, with substantially lower current suicidal ideation following initiation of gender-affirming treatment, suggesting improvement in acute suicide risk despite persistent mental health morbidity.

Study aim

To compare health status and healthcare experiences between autistic and allistic transgender and non-binary adults

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional survey study using self-completed questionnaires)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥14
SampleN=2,858; man/boy, woman/girl, non-binary, Indigenous gender identities

Key findings

  • Among Trans PULSE participants, 14.3% identified as autistic (8.1% formally diagnosed; 6.2% self-identified). Compared with allistic participants, autistic individuals were younger and had lower educational attainment, employment, and income levels, and were more likely to identify as asexual. Autistic participants reported poorer overall health, higher levels of unmet healthcare needs, and greater mental health disparities. Although few participants reported being explicitly denied gender-affirming care (3.7% diagnosed; 1.1% self-identified), a substantial proportion avoided disclosing autism during gender-affirming care readiness assessments (25.5% diagnosed; 36.1% self-identified).

Study aim

To describe the prevalence and severity of mental health problems among transgender and gender nonconforming youth

DesignQUANT (Retrospective cohort study using electronic medical records)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)3–17
SampleN=1,347; transfeminine (n=588), transmasculine (n=745)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) were more prevalent among transgender and gender-nonconforming children and adolescents than among matched cisgender peers. ASD prevalence was higher in transfeminine than transmasculine youth (approximately 7% vs. 3%), with prevalence ratios indicating several-fold increased likelihood relative to cisgender reference groups. ADHD was among the most common diagnoses, with prevalence ratios ranging from approximately 2 to 13 times higher than in cisgender peers depending on age, gender modality, and timing of assessment. ASD and ADHD were analysed within broader mental health diagnostic categories, and outcomes such as self-harm, suicidality, or access to gender-affirming care were not examined separately.

Study aim

To assess psychosocial and psychological vulnerability among clinic-referred adolescents with gender dysphoria

DesignMIXED (Intake assessment study using telephone interviews with adolescents and/or parents and review of background reports)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–20
SampleN=50; natal males (n=17), natal females (n=33)

Key findings

  • A substantial proportion of adolescents presenting with gender dysphoria had co-occurring neurodevelopmental and psychiatric diagnoses. ADHD was commonly identified, either alone or alongside other mental health conditions, and autism spectrum conditions (including Asperger’s disorder and pervasive developmental disorder–NOS) were present in a smaller subset of the cohort. Over half of the sample had at least one DSM-IV diagnosis other than gender dysphoria, and multiple comorbid diagnoses were frequent. Neurodevelopmental diagnoses occurred within a context of high overall psychosocial vulnerability, including elevated rates of prior mental health service use, psychopharmacological treatment, self-harm, and suicidal ideation.

Study aim

To explore experiences, barriers, and recommendations related to accessing gender identity healthcare among autistic TNB adults

DesignQUAL (Qualitative study using semi-structured interviews conducted via video, text, or email)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)18–46
SampleN=17; transgender (n=10), non-binary (n=3), both (n=4)

Key findings

  • This thematic analysis identified multiple structural and professional barriers affecting autistic gender-diverse individuals’ access to gender-affirming healthcare. Key challenges included limited professional knowledge of autism and gender-diverse healthcare needs, inadequate communication methods and accommodations, and misattribution or misdiagnosis of mental health difficulties. Participants also reported significant accessibility barriers, such as unmet sensory needs, disruption to routines, lack of local services, and prolonged waiting times. Bureaucratic and economic obstacles—including unclear care pathways, rigid standardisation of services, and complex or inaccessible insurance processes—further impeded access to care. Participants emphasised the importance of incorporating service-user perspectives to improve the accessibility and quality of gender-affirming healthcare.

Study aim

To describe clinical characteristics and mental health comorbidities among adolescents attending a transgender clinic

DesignQUANT (Retrospective chart review)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–18
SampleN=203; AFAB (n=156), AMAB (n=47)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) were reported among adolescents attending a gender clinic at rates higher than expected in the general population. Previously diagnosed ASD was reported in approximately 5–6% of adolescents, with similar prevalence across assigned sex at birth. ADHD was more prevalent in assigned males, with 14.9% of AMAB adolescents reporting a prior ADHD diagnosis, while no AFAB adolescents reported ADHD. ASD and ADHD were identified within a broader context of high mental health burden, including elevated rates of depression, anxiety, suicidal ideation, and self-harm; however, outcomes were not analysed separately by neurodevelopmental diagnosis.

Study aim

To establish prevalence of autism spectrum disorder among gender dysphoric children and adolescents and describe clinical characteristics

DesignQUANT (Diagnostic assessment study using standardised clinical interviews and psychometric testing)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)8–18
SampleN=204; gender dysphoric children/adolescents

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) was identified in 7.8% of children and adolescents referred to a gender identity clinic, a rate substantially higher than population prevalence estimates. ASD was significantly more common among individuals with subthreshold or atypical gender presentations (GID-NOS) than among those meeting full GID criteria. Children with ASD had significantly lower IQ scores than non-ASD peers, while adolescents with ASD were older at assessment but did not differ in IQ. Most individuals with co-occurring ASD met criteria for autistic disorder, and gender dysphoria frequently alleviated over time in children but persisted in adolescents.

Study aim

To examine psychiatric comorbidity among adolescents diagnosed with gender identity disorder

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional study using standardised psychometric assessments)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12–17
SampleN=105; natal males and natal females

Key findings

  • Most gender-dysphoric adolescents did not have co-occurring psychiatric disorders, although a substantial minority had at least one DSM-IV diagnosis. ADHD was infrequently identified (ADHD diagnosed in ~10.1% (combined/inattentive/hyperactive)), with no significant differences by gender or treatment eligibility status. Autism spectrum disorder was not assessed in this cohort, and the authors note that its exclusion likely resulted in an underestimation of neurodevelopmental comorbidity. Emotional disorders, particularly anxiety and mood disorders, were more common than disruptive disorders, and adolescents with delayed eligibility for medical intervention had higher odds of psychiatric comorbidity alongside lower IQ, older age, and less stable family living arrangements.

Study aim

To describe chronic disease burden among transgender Medicare beneficiaries compared with cisgender beneficiaries

DesignQUANT (Register-based cohort study using Medicare claims data – CMS Chronic Conditions Data Warehouse)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)Mean 53.1
SampleN=7,454; transgender

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) was more prevalent among transgender Medicare beneficiaries than among cisgender beneficiaries (3.0% vs. 0.3% overall), with higher prevalence observed among those entitled to Medicare through disability (4.0% vs. 1.3%). ADHD was not reported as a discrete diagnostic category in this dataset. Neurodevelopmental conditions, including ASD, occurred within a broader context of substantially higher mental health and neurological comorbidity among transgender beneficiaries, particularly among those under 65 years of age. ASD-specific outcomes such as self-harm, suicidality, or access to gender-affirming care were not examined separately.

Study aim

To examine sociodemographic characteristics of pediatric patients hospitalised for suicidal behaviours with gender dysphoria

DesignQUANT (Retrospective cohort study using Nationwide Inpatient Sample administrative data)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)6–26
SampleN=319,430 (TGD subsample n=4,840); gender dysphoria

Key findings

  • Among children, adolescents, and transitional-aged youth hospitalized for suicidal behaviors, neurodevelopmental disorders were present in 27.4% of those with a co-diagnosis of gender dysphoria, compared with 24.2% of those without gender dysphoria. Neurodevelopmental disorders were independently associated with a higher likelihood of a gender dysphoria diagnosis (OR = 1.17, 95% CI 1.09–1.26). ASD and ADHD were not disaggregated within the neurodevelopmental category. Neurodevelopmental diagnoses occurred alongside high rates of anxiety, PTSD, eating disorders, and other psychiatric comorbidities in this high-risk inpatient population.

Study aim

To describe patterns of service access, interventions, discharge, re-referral, and detransitioning among service users

DesignQUANT (Retrospective chart review using electronic patient records)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)Median 25
SampleN=175; MtF (n=101), FtM (n=59), non-binary (n=14)

Key findings

  • Neurodevelopmental disorders (including autism spectrum disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, dyslexia, and dyspraxia) were documented in 12.7% of adults discharged from a UK gender identity clinic, with higher prevalence among those under 25 years of age. Neurodevelopmental diagnoses were independently associated with poorer access to care, showing a markedly increased likelihood of not completing the treatment pathway (adjusted OR = 5.7, 95% CI 1.7–19). Neurodevelopmental disorders occurred alongside high levels of mental health comorbidity, adverse childhood experiences, and substance misuse. ASD and ADHD were not disaggregated in outcome analyses, and ASD- or ADHD-specific clinical trajectories were not examined separately.

Study aim

To describe experiences of hormone treatment among children and adolescents attending a specialist gender clinic

DesignQUANT (Chart review with descriptive statistical analysis)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)10–16
SampleN=21; AMAB (n=13), AFAB (n=8)

Key findings

  • Neurodevelopmental conditions were present in a subset of children and adolescents presenting with persistent gender dysphoria. Autism spectrum disorder (including Asperger syndrome) was identified in approximately one in seven adolescents assessed for hormone treatment eligibility, alongside other psychiatric comorbidities. ADHD was not specifically reported. Neurodevelopmental diagnoses occurred in the context of high overall psychological distress, including universal reports of anxiety or depressive symptoms and frequent suicidal ideation. The authors note a previously reported association between paediatric gender dysphoria and ASD, although mechanisms underlying this relationship remain unclear.

Study aim

To examine demographic characteristics and associated psychosocial difficulties among children and adolescents referred for gender dysphoria

DesignQUANT (Clinical file audit of consecutive referrals over a 12-month period)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)5–17
SampleN=218; natal males and natal females

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum conditions (ASC) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) were commonly reported among children and adolescents presenting with features of gender dysphoria. ASC was significantly more prevalent in natal males than natal females (approximately 18.5% vs. 10.2%, including suspected cases), while ADHD was reported more frequently in natal males (12.3% vs. 5.8%). The prevalence of ASC and ADHD did not vary significantly by age group, in contrast to other associated difficulties such as self-harm and low mood, which increased with age. Neurodevelopmental conditions occurred alongside high levels of bullying, depression, self-harm, and suicidal ideation, though outcomes were not analysed separately by ASD or ADHD status.

Study aim

To investigate psychiatric comorbidity and life events among patients with gender identity disorder in Japan

DesignQUANT (Clinical assessment study using interviews and clinical records)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)Mean 26.5–32
SampleN=579; FtM (n=349), MtF (n=230)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder was rarely identified among patients diagnosed with gender identity disorder, with only four individuals meeting criteria for Asperger’s disorder within the cohort. Overall, the majority of patients did not have a current psychiatric comorbidity, although lifetime suicidal ideation and self-harm were highly prevalent across both male-to-female and female-to-male groups. When present, psychiatric comorbidity was associated with significantly higher rates of suicidal ideation and self-harm, particularly among female-to-male patients. Neurodevelopmental conditions, including ASD and ADHD, were not prominent contributors to clinical complexity in this cohort.

Study aim

To describe the prevalence and characteristics of gender dysphoria in Ireland and implications for care pathways

DesignQUANT (Retrospective cohort study based on medical record review of gender clinic referrals)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–69
SampleN=218; MtF (72.9%), FtM (27.1%)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder was reported in a small proportion of adults attending an Irish gender dysphoria service, with Asperger’s syndrome identified in approximately 2.3% of the cohort. ADHD was not reported as a discrete diagnosis. Neurodevelopmental conditions occurred alongside a broader pattern of psychiatric comorbidity, most notably depression, which affected over one-third of patients, and histories of deliberate self-harm. Overall, ASD was not a prominent contributor to psychiatric complexity in this adult cohort, and ASD-specific outcomes such as access to gender-affirming care or treatment trajectories were not examined separately.

Study aim

To examine changes in psychiatric needs among individuals seeking gender reassignment in Finland over time

DesignQUANT (Register-based longitudinal follow-up study using national administrative registers)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,665 GD service users; 29,292 population controls (registry-based)

Key findings

  • Individuals seeking gender reassignment had substantially higher levels of specialist-level psychiatric care than matched population controls, both before and after contact with gender identity services. Diagnoses with onset in childhood and adolescence (ICD-10 F90–99, which includes ADHD) and developmental disorders (F80–89, including autism spectrum disorder) were more prevalent among those seeking gender reassignment than controls. The prevalence of developmental disorders increased over time in both groups but remained consistently higher among those seeking gender reassignment. Neurodevelopmental diagnoses occurred alongside high rates of mood and anxiety disorders, and elevated psychiatric treatment needs persisted regardless of whether individuals proceeded to medical gender reassignment

Study aim

To describe sociodemographic, psychiatric, and developmental characteristics of adolescents seeking gender-related care in Finland

DesignMIXED (Mixed-methods study using medical file review and multidisciplinary clinical assessments)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)Mean 16
SampleN=47; natal girls (n=41), natal boys (n=6)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder was highly prevalent among adolescents presenting to a gender identity service, with approximately 26% diagnosed with an autism spectrum condition, markedly exceeding population prevalence estimates. Autism spectrum problems were frequently identified prior to the onset of gender dysphoria and occurred alongside severe psychiatric morbidity, including depression, anxiety, self-harm, and suicidality. ADHD was also reported, affecting around 11% of adolescents, and was more common among natal males. Neurodevelopmental conditions were embedded within a broader pattern of significant developmental difficulties, social isolation, and prior engagement with specialist mental health services, and were particularly prominent among adolescents with later-onset gender dysphoria and identity confusion.

Study aim

To describe demographics, referral pathways, and comorbidities among individuals attending an Irish adult gender service

DesignQUANT (Retrospective chart review of National Gender Service medical records)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)18–30
SampleN=167; AFAB/FTM (62.3%), AMAB/MTF (35.3%), NB (2.4%)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) was common among young adults attending an Irish gender service, with 11.3% having a formal ASD diagnosis and an additional 2.9% displaying clinical features consistent with ASD without formal diagnosis. ASD was most frequently identified among transmasculine/AFAB individuals but was present across gender identities. ADHD was not reported as a discrete diagnosis. Neurodevelopmental conditions occurred alongside high levels of mental health comorbidity, including depression, anxiety, self-harm, and suicidal ideation, and in some cases required additional preparation or support prior to gender-affirming medical or surgical interventions.

Study aim

To describe clinical presentations of children assessed for gender dysphoria and contextual factors influencing assessment and care

DesignMIXED (Clinical assessment study using self-report questionnaires and clinician assessment)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)8.4–15.9
SampleN=79; biological males (n=33), biological females (n=46)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) were frequently identified among children and adolescents presenting with gender dysphoria. ASD was diagnosed in 13.9% of the cohort, and ADHD in 16.5%, rates notably higher than population estimates. Neurodevelopmental conditions occurred alongside high levels of psychological distress, including anxiety, depression, self-harm, and suicidal ideation. Study aim

To examine variations in mental health and psychosocial functioning by age, gender, dysphoria severity, and informant

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional psychometric survey study using YSR and CBCL)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12–18
SampleN=149; AFAB (64.4%), AMAB (35.6%)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) diagnoses were not directly assessed or reported in this cohort of adolescents seeking gender-affirming hormone therapy. However, clinically elevated attention-deficit/hyperactivity problems were reported in approximately 10–11% of adolescents based on youth and parent ratings, indicating notable attentional and executive-function difficulties. High rates of internalizing symptoms, including depression, anxiety, obsessive–compulsive symptoms, and post-traumatic stress symptoms, were observed across the cohort. The authors note that ASD has been reported at elevated rates in other gender-dysphoric youth samples, but ASD-specific mental health or psychosocial outcomes were not examined here.

Study aim

To compare psychopathology and quality of life between gender-diverse children with and without autism spectrum disorder

DesignQUANT (Cohort study using data from the GENTLE cohort)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)Mean 14.6
SampleN=104; AMAB (n=25), AFAB (n=79)

Key findings

  • Autistic traits and autism spectrum disorder (ASD) were highly prevalent among gender-diverse children and adolescents attending a specialist clinic. Clinically indicated ASD was identified in approximately 22% of the cohort based on DSM-5–aligned screening, and around 10% had a pre-existing formal ASD diagnosis at intake, both substantially exceeding population prevalence estimates. Youth with indicated ASD showed significantly higher levels of internalising symptoms (including anxiety, depression, somatic complaints, and thought problems) and markedly poorer quality of life across physical, emotional, social, and school domains compared with non-ASD peers. ASD status was strongly associated with overall clinical-range psychopathology, highlighting autistic gender-diverse youth as a particularly vulnerable subgroup within clinical services.

Study aim

To compare autistic traits between transgender and cisgender individuals and assess changes following hormone therapy

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional study using standardised psychometric instruments)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)≥18
SampleN=789; trans men (n=206), trans women (n=182); cis comparators included

Key findings

  • Autistic traits were elevated among transgender adults compared with cisgender individuals, with transgender participants scoring higher on the Autism Spectrum Quotient (AQ), alongside higher levels of alexithymia and social anxiety. AQ scores in transgender participants were below the clinical threshold for ASD, suggesting elevated autistic traits rather than diagnosed autism. Following gender-affirming hormonal treatment, autistic trait scores significantly decreased over 12 months, particularly in domains related to attention switching and communication, while cisgender comparison groups showed stable scores. Changes in autistic traits were not explained by reductions in alexithymia or social anxiety, indicating that autistic-like traits may reflect a state associated with gender dysphoria and distress rather than stable ASD.

Study aim

To examine prevalence of mental health diagnoses, self-injury, victimisation, and hormone therapy coverage among transgender adolescents

DesignQUANT (Retrospective chart review with medical record abstraction)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)9–18
SampleN=79; transgender males (n=51), transgender females (n=28)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) was identified in 6.3% of transgender adolescents receiving care within a multidisciplinary paediatric gender programme, with similar prevalence across transgender males and females. ADHD was not reported as a discrete diagnosis in this cohort. ASD occurred alongside very high levels of overall psychiatric morbidity, including depression, anxiety, post-traumatic stress disorder, eating disorders, and bipolar disorder. Neurodevelopmental conditions were embedded within a context of marked psychosocial vulnerability, characterised by high rates of suicidal ideation, self-harm, suicide attempts, and school victimisation, although outcomes were not analysed separately by ASD status.

Study aim

To assess prevalence of autistic traits among adults with gender dysphoria and differences by sex assigned at birth and sexual orientation

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional observational study using interviews and standardised questionnaires)
PopulationAdults
Age range (years)Mean 27–45
SampleN=91; MtF (n=63), FtM (n=28)

Key findings

  • Autistic traits consistent with a possible autism spectrum disorder (ASD) were identified in 5.5% of adults with gender dysphoria based on Autism Spectrum Quotient (AQ) scores meeting the recommended clinical cut-off, a rate higher than general population estimates. Overall AQ scores did not differ significantly between male-to-female and female-to-male participants, although female-to-male individuals showed modestly higher scores on selected subdomains (social skills, attention switching, and imagination) compared with cisgender female controls. Individuals classified as non-homosexual with respect to natal sex had significantly higher AQ scores than homosexual individuals. The findings indicate elevated autistic traits among adults with gender dysphoria, though most participants scored below thresholds indicative of clinical ASD.

Study aim

To examine prevalence of suicidality, self-injury, and body dissatisfaction among transgender adolescents and emerging adults

DesignQUANT (Retrospective chart review with clinical and anthropometric data extracted from EMR)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–22
SampleN=96; FtM (n=54), MtF (n=31), non-binary/gender fluid (n=15)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) were present in a subset of transgender adolescents and emerging adults presenting with gender dysphoria. Study aim

To compare prevalence of mental health problems and suicidality between transgender and cisgender children in a population sample

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional analysis of a nationally representative cohort using parent-reported CBCL)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)9–10
SampleN=7,169 (TGD n=58; cisgender n=7,111)

Key findings

  • Transgender children aged 9–10 years showed higher levels of mental health difficulties than cisgender peers across multiple DSM-5–oriented domains. Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD)–related problems were more common, with transgender children having higher mean CBCL attention problem scores and increased odds of scoring in the borderline or clinical range, although this did not reach statistical significance. Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) was not directly assessed or reported in this dataset. Neurodevelopmental difficulties occurred within a broader pattern of elevated internalising and externalising symptoms, including depression, anxiety, conduct problems, and markedly higher odds of suicidality.

Study aim

To examine prevalence of gender variance among children and adolescents with ASD, ADHD, other neurodevelopmental conditions, and controls

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional chart review with parent-report questionnaires)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)6–18
SampleN=554 + 1,605 normative controls; parent-reported gender variance

Key findings

  • This chart review found significantly higher rates of parent-reported gender variance among children with autism spectrum disorder (5.4%) and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (4.8%) compared with medical neurodevelopmental (1.7%) and non-referred groups (0–0.7%). Children with ASD were 7.59 times more likely and children with ADHD were 6.64 times more likely to express gender variance than non-referred peers. No increased likelihood of gender variance was observed in children with medical neurodevelopmental disorders. Gender variance was associated with elevated emotional symptoms in ADHD but not ASD, and occurred at similar rates in females and males after adjusting for sex ratios.

Study aim

To characterise mental health outcomes among TGD youth in Australia and associations with adverse life events.

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional online survey study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–25
SampleN=859; male/trans male (29.7%), female/trans female (15.0%), non-binary (48.5%), other (6.8%)

Key findings

  • Autism spectrum disorder was reported by 22-25% of trans and gender diverse young people in this large Australian community sample. Unlike other psychiatric diagnoses, ASD was not significantly associated with most measured negative life experiences in adjusted analyses. However, the cohort experienced high levels of social and educational adversity overall, which may disproportionately affect autistic individuals. The absence of ASD-specific outcome analyses limits conclusions regarding differential mental health risk and highlights a gap in population-level research on autistic TGD youth.

Study aim

To examine associations between autism risk factors and autistic traits in children with gender dysphoria

DesignQUANT (Questionnaire-based survey study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)≤12
SampleN=49; natal males and natal females

Key findings

  • Clinically significant autistic traits were identified in a substantial proportion of gender dysphoric children, with 44.9% scoring in the clinical range for autism-related social and behavioural difficulties. Elevated autistic traits were associated with greater gender nonconformity, older age, lower IQ, and higher levels of general behavioural and emotional problems. Study aim

To examine prevalence of autism diagnoses, autistic traits, and psychiatric conditions among TGD individuals in population cohorts

DesignQUANT (Cross-sectional multi-cohort study using online questionnaires and population cohort data)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)~15–90
SampleN=641,860 across 5 datasets; cisgender and TGD (varied ascertainment)

Key findings

  • Using five large population-based datasets (total N ≈ 642,000), transgender and gender-diverse individuals were consistently more likely to have an autism diagnosis than cisgender individuals, with adjusted odds ratios ranging from approximately 3.0 to 6.4 across cohorts. Transgender and gender-diverse participants also reported higher levels of autistic traits, systemising, and sensory sensitivity, and lower empathy, compared with cisgender males and females, even after controlling for age, education, and autism diagnosis. Elevated rates of other neurodevelopmental and psychiatric conditions were observed, though autism showed one of the strongest and most consistent associations with gender diversity. Exploratory analyses suggested higher rates of suspected undiagnosed autism among transgender and gender-diverse individuals.

Study aim

To estimate VTE incidence in trans people receiving GAHT and evaluate the utility of thrombophilia screening.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study; 1995–2007)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)17–61
SampleN=251; MtF (n=162), FtM (n=89)

Physical health findings

  • VTE/thrombophilia: 18/251 (7.2%) thrombophilic defects (17 aPC resistance; 1 combined aPC + protein C deficiency); no VTE events during follow-up.
  • Comparisons: MtF 8.0% vs FtM 5.6% thrombophilia (p=0.6).
  • Self-treatment link: prior hormone self-treatment common (37% MtF; 2% FtM) and associated with higher aPC resistance (p=0.04).
  • Lab tools: aPC resistance, antithrombin III, protein C activity, free protein S antigen.

Overall findings and implications

  • VTE rare with contemporary regimens in this cohort.
  • Routine thrombophilia screening not indicated except where personal/family history or risk factors.
  • Risk management may allow treatment even with aPC resistance.

Study aim

To assess health and wellbeing of transgender youth accessing specialist gender-affirming care and identify service gaps/strengths.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)14–23
SampleN=100; trans female (n=31), trans male (n=65), non-binary (n=4)

Physical health findings

  • Self-rated health: 75% good/very good/excellent.
  • Healthcare access: 95% had a usual GP/clinic; 14% unable to access care when needed (cost/transport/appointments).
  • Primary care capability: 64% comfortable discussing trans care with GP; 30% felt GP knowledgeable; 26% had to educate GP; 29% had not disclosed gender identity to GP.
  • Tools: NZ Health Survey–adapted items.

  • Need improved referral pathways, primary care training, and equitable access to treatments.

Study aim

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)≥40
SampleN=1,078; trans women (n=70), trans men (n=25), non-binary (n=19), cis women (n=517), cis men (n=447)

Physical health findings

  • CVD/VTE (self-report dx): Any CVD 38.5% (trans) vs 51.2% (cis); VTE 7.8% (trans) vs 3.1% (cis).
  • Adjusted odds: trans vs cis VTE aOR 3.35 (95% CI 1.07–10.46); trans women vs cis women aOR 3.94 (1.24–12.51).

Study aim

To estimate depressive symptom prevalence and associated risk/protective factors among FTM Ontarians.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–64
SampleN=205; female-to-male (FTM) spectrum

Physical health findings

  • Chronic physical health conditions: An estimated 38.7% of female-to-male (FTM) participants reported at least one major chronic physical health condition.
  • Conditions included: HIV, cancer, fibromyalgia, osteoporosis, and diabetes, as well as ongoing physical discomfort or pain.
  • Physical–mental health overlap: Presence of a chronic physical health condition was associated with higher odds of depressive symptomatology (OR 2.47, 95% CI 0.97–6.30), though this association did not reach statistical significance after adjustment.
  • Measurement/tools: Self-reported diagnosis of specified chronic conditions and physical discomfort.

Overall findings and implications

  • A substantial proportion of transmasculine adults reported living with chronic physical health conditions.
  • Physical health comorbidity appeared to contribute to overall health burden, particularly when co-occurring with psychological distress.
  • Findings highlight the importance of integrated physical and mental healthcare, though physical health outcomes were not examined in depth or stratified by treatment status.

Study aim

To evaluate feminising GAHT regimens and associated cardiovascular risk factors in ageing trans people.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=269; trans individuals on feminizing therapy

Physical health findings

  • Cardiometabolic risk factors (≥45 years): Hypertension was the most prevalent risk factor (29%), followed by current smoking (24%), obesity (20%), dyslipidaemia (16%), and diabetes (9%).
  • Body mass index: Median BMI was 27.1 kg/m² (IQR 25.0–30.1), within the overweight range; individuals using transdermal estradiol had higher median BMI (29.6 kg/m²).
  • Hormone regimen and physiology: Serum estradiol concentrations did not differ significantly by age group (≥45 vs <45 years) or route of administration; sex steroid levels were within expected therapeutic ranges.
  • Route-specific patterns: Transdermal estradiol use was more common in individuals aged ≥45 years (31% vs 8%), while oral estradiol doses were lower in this age group.
  • Cardiovascular events: Prevalence of ischaemic heart disease, cerebrovascular disease, and VTE was low in this cohort.
  • Laboratory measures: Routine clinical markers included BMI, blood pressure, lipids, glucose, SHBG, and serum estradiol.

Overall findings and implications

  • Older transgender women receiving feminizing hormone therapy commonly present with modifiable cardiometabolic risk factors, particularly hypertension, smoking, and obesity.
  • Despite elevated risk-factor prevalence, overt cardiovascular events were uncommon in this cohort.
  • Findings support proactive cardiovascular risk screening and management alongside ongoing feminizing hormone therapy, with particular attention to aging individuals.

Study aim

To compare dementia risk factors and risk scores across cisgender, transgender and non-binary adults.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=955; AMAB, AFAB, gender non-binary (plus cisgender controls)

Physical health findings

  • Dementia/AD risk: Higher overall late-life Alzheimer’s disease risk in transgender men, transgender women, and non-binary adults compared with cisgender men and women.
  • Cardiometabolic conditions: Higher prevalence of heart attack, coronary heart disease, diabetes, hypertension, and kidney disease in transgender and non-binary groups, particularly non-binary adults.
  • Lifestyle factors: Higher rates of physical inactivity, smoking, and heavy alcohol use in some transgender and non-binary groups.
  • Depression (relevant physical risk factor): Markedly higher prevalence in non-binary adults and transgender men, a known contributor to dementia risk.

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender and non-binary adults show greater physical health risk profiles relevant to dementia and Alzheimer’s disease compared with cisgender peers.
  • Findings suggest gender-related health inequities may contribute to elevated late-life neurological risk.
  • Highlights the need for inclusive prevention strategies, improved access to cardiometabolic care, and validation of dementia risk tools in transgender and gender-diverse populations

Study aim

PopulationMixed

SampleN=151; transgender women

Study aim

To describe clinical characteristics and comorbidities among adolescents presenting to a transgender clinic and compare with prior reports.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)12–18
SampleN=203; AFAB (n=156), AMAB (n=47)

Physical health findings

  • Anthropometrics: Mean BMI was 24.2 kg/m² (AFAB) and 23.0 kg/m² (AMAB); 35% of AFAB and 27% of AMAB were overweight or obese.
  • Baseline laboratory results: Baseline blood tests were largely within reference ranges; minor lipid abnormalities were common (elevated cholesterol or triglycerides in ~25–40%).
  • Sex steroid levels: Baseline estradiol and testosterone levels were within expected ranges for sex assigned at birth, with mild testosterone elevation in a small number of AFAB participants.
  • Puberty suppression: Among those receiving GnRH analogues, follow-up blood tests demonstrated effective suppression of gonadotropins and sex steroids, with expected clinical effects (e.g. menses suppression, reduced erections).
  • Hormone therapy monitoring: Early monitoring after initiation of gender-affirming hormones showed no clinically significant laboratory abnormalities, aside from expected changes in haemoglobin.

Overall findings and implications

  • Baseline physical health in adolescents presenting to a gender clinic was generally unremarkable, with overweight/obesity the most consistent physical health issue.
  • Laboratory abnormalities were typically mild and non-specific, supporting streamlined baseline and follow-up testing.
  • Findings support the physical safety of puberty suppression and early gender-affirming hormone treatment when accompanied by routine clinical monitoring.

Study aim

To assess change in 30-year Framingham CVD risk after initiation of GAHT in a large transgender cohort.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–59
SampleN=309; transmen (n=165), transwomen (n=144)

Physical health findings

  • Cardiometabolic: lipid profile changes with GAHT (TW: ↓TC, ↓TG; TM: ↑TC, ↑TG).
  • BP: TM no change; TW slight ↓SBP (~2.13 mmHg).
  • CVD risk: baseline elevated vs “optimal”; TM lipid-based CVD risk ↑ by 1.44% (general/hard events).

Study aim

To test whether gender minority stress decreases after GAHT initiation and identify social and hormonal predictors over time.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=85; transmen (n=47), transwomen (n=38)

Physical health findings

  • Physical health outcomes: Direct physical health conditions, diagnoses, or physiological measures were not empirically assessed.
  • Physical health pathways: Physical health was discussed conceptually through links between chronic stress exposure and downstream outcomes such as cardiovascular and inflammatory risk.
  • Measurement/tools: No physical health measurements, clinical data, or self-reported physical health indicators were included.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health disparities were framed as indirectly shaped by minority stress processes, rather than intrinsic to gender identity.
  • The absence of measured physical health outcomes highlights a gap in empirical evidence linking stress pathways to physical morbidity in transgender populations.
  • Findings support the need for future research integrating objective physical health indicators alongside psychosocial measures.

Study aim

To estimate health indicators and socioeconomic status by transgender status in a representative household sample.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–64
SampleN=131; male sounding, female sounding

Physical health findings

  • General health status: Transgender adults reported similar overall physical health to non-transgender adults in this household sample.
  • Body weight: Transgender adults were less likely to be overweight than non-transgender peers (OR 0.4, 95% CI 0.2–0.8), though obesity prevalence did not differ significantly.
  • Smoking: Transgender adults were more likely to be current smokers than non-transgender adults (OR 2.7, 95% CI 1.3–5.6).
  • Chronic conditions: Prevalence of diabetes, heart disease, and asthma did not differ significantly between transgender and non-transgender adults.
  • Disability and poor physical health days: Rates of activity limitation due to disability and ≥15 poor physical health days in the past month were similar across groups.
  • Healthcare access: Transgender adults reported comparable health insurance coverage and routine check-up rates to non-transgender adults.

Overall findings and implications

  • Few physical health differences were observed between transgender and non-transgender adults in this stably housed population.
  • Elevated smoking prevalence represents a key modifiable physical health risk.
  • Findings likely underestimate physical health disparities among transgender people due to exclusion of unstably housed and more marginalised populations.

Study aim

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)

SampleN=144; transgender men (n=61), transgender women (n=83)

Study aim

To describe characteristics of individuals attending a multidisciplinary gender dysphoria consultation service.

DesignQuantitative (clinical cohort; study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=114; FtM, MtF

Physical health findings

  • Smoking: 32.5% of participants were current smokers.

Study aim

To examine the incidence of breast cancer in a large North American sample of transgender veterans, assess exposure to cross-sex hormones (CSH), and compare incidence with European trans populations.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort using administrative data)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–64 (mean ≈55 years)
SampleN≈5,135 transgender veterans (VHA users); breast cancer cases n=10

Physical health findings

  • Breast cancer incidence: 10 confirmed cases (0.2% of cohort): 7 FtM, 2 MtF, 1 natal male with transvestic fetishism.
  • Incidence rate: 20.0 per 100,000 patient-years (expected 14.8/100,000).
  • Hormone exposure: No male patients prescribed CSH by VHA had breast cancer; 3 cases occurred in individuals who had received CSH.
  • Treatment: 8 underwent mastectomy and/or lumpectomy; 2 MtF patients did not have surgery.
  • Adjuvant therapy: Tamoxifen used in 1 MtF, 1 TF, and 4 FtM patients.
  • Mortality: 4 deaths prior to end of follow-up.
  • Tools: VHA Medical SAS Datasets; Corporate Data Warehouse; ICD-9-CM codes; pathology-confirmed diagnoses.

Overall findings and implications

  • Breast cancer among transgender veterans appears uncommon overall.
  • Late-stage presentation in MtF cases highlights importance of routine breast/chest screening.
  • Residual breast tissue after mastectomy in FtM patients may carry ongoing cancer risk, supporting continued surveillance.
  • Findings reinforce adherence to standard cancer screening recommendations tailored to anatomy and treatment history.

Study aim

To examine physical and mental health of older trans adults and identify modifiable factors associated with health risk.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=174; transgender

Physical health findings

  • Transgender older adults reported significantly poorer self-rated physical health and higher disability rates compared with non-transgender LGB peers.
  • Higher prevalence of obesity (≈40%) and physical inactivity (≈23%), both associated with poorer physical health and disability.
  • Fear of accessing healthcare and financial barriers were common and linked to worse physical health outcomes.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health disparities were largely mediated by modifiable factors, particularly stigma, victimisation, obesity, physical inactivity, and fear of healthcare access.
  • Risk factors explained a substantial proportion of poor physical health and disability, suggesting structural and social determinants play a major role.
  • Findings support targeted interventions addressing stigma, healthcare accessibility, physical activity, and social support to improve physical health in transgender older adults.

Study aim

To examine associations between sexual orientation identity and medical morbidity in a large sample of transfeminine patients.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–76
SampleN=330; MtF

Physical health findings

  • Medical morbidity: The majority of medical conditions did not differ by sexual orientation identity among transfeminine participants.
  • HIV: 3% overall were HIV-positive; HIV prevalence was higher among heterosexual-identified transfeminine participants compared with lesbian or bisexual participants (age-adjusted OR 9.07).
  • Cardiometabolic conditions: Prevalence of hypertension (10%), diabetes (6%), coronary artery disease (3%), asthma (6%), and dyslipidaemia was low and similar across groups.

  • HIV risk differed by sexual orientation, highlighting the importance of tailored sexual health screening and prevention.
  • Findings support the use of standard medical screening protocols, with added attention to HIV risk stratification in transfeminine care.

Study aim

To estimate incidence of VTE, ischaemic stroke and myocardial infarction among transgender people.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=6,456; transfeminine (n=2,842), transmasculine (n=2,118)

Physical health findings

  • Venous thromboembolism (VTE): Transfeminine participants had higher VTE incidence than cisgender men and women (adjusted HR ~1.9–2.0 overall); risk increased with longer follow-up.
  • Ischaemic stroke: Overall incidence was similar across groups, but higher rates emerged among transfeminine participants initiating oestrogen, particularly after longer follow-up.
  • Myocardial infarction: Rates in transfeminine participants were higher than cisgender women but similar to cisgender men; no clear increase observed in transmasculine participants.
  • Hormone initiation effects: Elevated VTE and stroke risks were most pronounced among transfeminine individuals starting oestrogen during follow-up; risk appeared to rise after 2–6 years.
  • Transmasculine outcomes: No statistically significant increase in VTE, stroke, or myocardial infarction compared with cisgender reference groups.
  • Measurement/tools: EMR-ascertained diagnoses of VTE, ischaemic stroke, and myocardial infarction; prescription records for hormone exposure.

Overall findings and implications

  • Cross-sex oestrogen therapy in transfeminine individuals was associated with increased VTE risk and possibly ischaemic stroke over time.
  • Cardiovascular risk patterns differed from those seen in cisgender women using menopausal hormone therapy.
  • Findings support the need for long-term cardiovascular monitoring, particularly for transfeminine patients receiving oestrogen.
  • No clear evidence of increased acute cardiovascular risk was observed for transmasculine individuals within available follow-up.

Study aim

To describe access to care and service-use pathways, identify factors linked to access/outcomes, and estimate rates of detransitioning.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=175; MtF (n=101), FtM (n=59), non-binary (n=14)

Physical health findings

  • Pre-existing physical health conditions: 45.1% of service users had ≥1 documented physical health condition at assessment; obesity was most common (14.9%).
  • Physical health as a barrier to care: Physical health conditions (including obesity and other medical issues) were cited as reasons for not accessing gender-affirming surgery in a subset of service users.
  • Hormone access: 94% of those seeking hormone therapy accessed it; physical health concerns rarely prevented hormone initiation.
  • Surgical access: 56.6% accessed feminising genital surgery and 25.8% accessed masculinising genital surgery where sought; physical health conditions contributed to non-access in a minority.
  • Measurement/tools: Electronic health record documentation of diagnosed physical conditions and interventions accessed.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health comorbidity was common among adults discharged from a UK gender identity clinic.
  • Physical health conditions occasionally acted as barriers to surgical care, but less so for hormone therapy.
  • Findings highlight the need for integrated management of physical health (e.g. obesity, chronic illness) within gender services to support equitable access to interventions.

Study aim

To estimate all-cause and cause-specific mortality among TGD people compared with cisgender people.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,315; transfeminine (n=1,951), transmasculine (n=1,364)

Physical health findings

  • Overall mortality: Higher all-cause mortality in TGD adults compared with cisgender people (transfeminine vs cis men MRR 1.34; vs cis women 1.60. Transmasculine vs cis women 1.75).
  • Cause-specific mortality: Significantly higher deaths from external causes (suicide, homicide, accidental poisoning), particularly compared with cisgender women (transfeminine MRR 1.92; transmasculine MRR 2.77).
  • Cancer mortality: Transfeminine adults had lower cancer mortality than cisgender women (MRR 0.52); no increased cancer mortality detected overall.
  • Endocrine causes: Higher mortality from endocrine, nutritional, and metabolic diseases compared with cisgender women (MRR 2.95).
  • Cardiovascular disease: No clear increase in CVD mortality detected, likely reflecting younger cohort age.

Overall findings and implications

  • TGD populations experience elevated mortality driven primarily by external causes, not cardiometabolic or cancer disease.
  • Findings highlight the role of structural and social determinants (violence, marginalisation, minority stress) rather than hormone-related physical risk alone.
  • Results support prioritising suicide prevention, violence reduction, and social protection, alongside routine physical health monitoring.
  • Longitudinal follow-up is needed as cohorts age to clarify long-term CVD and cancer risks.

Study aim

To assess guideline concordance for feminising and masculinising GAHT initiation in the Veterans Health Administration.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–60+
SampleN=4,676; MtF (n=3,547), FtM (n=1,129)

Physical health findings

  • Hematocrit monitoring: 91.8% had hematocrit measured prior to testosterone initiation; 96.5% did not have elevated hematocrit (>50%).
  • Baseline comorbidity burden: Veterans initiating feminising GAHT had higher prevalence of cardiometabolic conditions (diabetes, hypertension, hyperlipidaemia, MI) and higher overall comorbidity scores than those initiating masculinising GAHT.
  • Cancer and bone screening: Post-initiation screening was infrequent, including breast cancer screening (~30%), bone densitometry (~22%), and PSA monitoring (~18%).
  • Laboratory values: Mean post-initiation labs (lipids, liver enzymes, creatinine, hematocrit) were within expected clinical ranges.

Overall findings and implications

  • Lower concordance for masculinising GAHT highlights areas for improvement in contraindication assessment and hematocrit screening.
  • High cardiometabolic comorbidity among transfeminine veterans underscores the need for integrated chronic disease management alongside GAHT.
  • Gaps in cancer and bone health screening suggest missed opportunities for preventive physical healthcare during follow-up.

Study aim

To describe characteristics and prevalence of gender dysphoria presentations in Ireland and outline implications for care pathways.

DesignQuantitative (study design not specified)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–69
SampleN=218; MTF (72.9%), FTM (27.1%)

Physical health findings

  • Baseline medical comorbidity: Overall rates of co-existing physical illness were low; most common conditions were dyslipidaemia (21.6%), asthma (11.5%), and hypertension (9.2%).
  • Cardiometabolic risk factors: 25% had elevated total cholesterol (>5.3 mmol/L) and 31% had elevated LDL (>2.6 mmol/L) at baseline; ~20% reported a family history of vascular disease.
  • Glucose metabolism: Elevated fasting glucose was uncommon; no confirmed diabetes on repeat testing.
  • Bone health: Among those undergoing DEXA (17.9% of cohort), 20.5% had osteopenia and 7.7% osteoporosis.
  • Hormone therapy exposure: 74.3% were receiving hormone therapy; 20.2% had used hormones prior to clinic presentation (including non-prescribed use).
  • Surgical interventions: 22.3% had undergone surgery; 9.2% had gender-affirming genital surgery.
  • Adverse physical effects: 17% reported adverse effects related to hormonal or surgical treatment (e.g. dyslipidaemia, hot flushes, headaches); serious complications were rare.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health comorbidity was present but generally low in this relatively young adult cohort.
  • Cardiometabolic risk factors and bone health abnormalities were identified, supporting the need for routine screening prior to and during hormone therapy.
  • Adverse physical effects of treatment were uncommon and mostly mild, with low regret rates.
  • Findings support the importance of structured endocrine monitoring and integrated physical health care within gender services.

Study aim

To describe demographics, referral pathways and comorbidities among attendees of an Irish adult gender service (2014–2020).

DesignQuantitative (retrospective chart review)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–30
SampleN=167; FTM/AFAB (62.3%), MTF/AMAB (35.3%), FTM(AFAB)/NB (2.4%)

Physical health findings

  • Hormone and blocker use: 71.8% were receiving gender-affirming hormone therapy and a further 5.3% were receiving GnRH blockers alone; 97% expressed a desire for hormonal treatment.
  • Body mass index: Among those with recorded BMI, 23.8% were overweight and 15.5% were obese; underweight was uncommon (10.7%).
  • Smoking and alcohol: 22.2% were current smokers; 62.3% reported alcohol use.
  • Physical comorbidity: Asthma (11.3%), migraines (4.2%), and diabetes (1.7%) were the most frequently documented physical conditions.
  • Surgical interventions: 16.1% had undergone surgery, most commonly bilateral mastectomy; genital surgeries were rare and almost exclusively performed abroad.
  • Hormone-related laboratory findings: Elevated haematocrit (>0.51) was observed in 5% of those with available blood results, more commonly among AFAB individuals.

Overall findings and implications

  • Most young adults attending the Irish gender service were physically well, with low rates of serious medical comorbidity.
  • Overweight/obesity, smoking, and asthma were the most relevant physical health considerations.
  • Hormone therapy was widely accessed with few clinically significant physical complications, supporting its physical safety with routine monitoring.
  • Limited access to surgery, particularly within Ireland, represents a structural barrier to comprehensive physical care.

Study aim

To compare needs for gender-affirming interventions and health indicators among non-binary people versus trans men and trans women in Poland.

DesignQuantitative (online cross sectional survey)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=98; non-binary (n=53), transgender men and women (n=45)

Physical health findings

  • Somatic symptom burden: Mean PHQ-15 scores indicated moderate physical symptom severity across the sample, with no significant difference between non-binary and binary transgender participants.
  • Predictors of physical symptoms: Higher perceived stigma exposure (DHEQ total score) was significantly associated with greater somatic symptom severity.
  • Protective factors: Higher resilience was significantly associated with lower physical symptom severity.

  • Minority stress, rather than medical transition status, appears central to physical symptom burden.
  • Findings highlight the importance of addressing stigma and strengthening resilience as part of holistic physical health care.
  • The absence of objective medical data underscores a gap in biomedical physical health research, particularly for non-binary populations.

Study aim

To describe a provider’s long-term clinical experience delivering endocrine care for trans people.

DesignQuantitative (retrosepctive chart review)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=242; MtF (80%), FtM (20%)

Physical health findings

  • Hormone therapy exposure: 100% received clinician-prescribed GAHT; 31% had used hormones prior to clinic presentation, including 7.8% without medical supervision.
  • Thromboembolic events: 5 cases of DVT (2.1%) occurred over 1,380 patient-years, all in transfeminine individuals; most events occurred prior to clinic care or with older oestrogen regimens (ethinyl estradiol or conjugated equine oestrogens).
  • Current oestrogen safety: No DVT events were observed among patients treated with 17β-estradiol.
  • HIV prevalence: 6.6% of the cohort was HIV-positive; most had initiated hormones without medical supervision.
  • Surgical interventions: 31% of transfeminine and 64% of transmasculine participants accessed gender-affirming surgery; lack of insurance coverage limited surgical access.
  • General medical conditions: Low prevalence of documented chronic physical illness; no excess serious medical morbidity reported during follow-up.

Overall findings and implications

  • GAHT delivered within guideline-based endocrine care appeared physically safe, particularly with modern oestrogen formulations.
  • Thromboembolic risk was concentrated in unsupervised hormone use and older regimens, supporting structured medical oversight.
  • Limited insurance coverage constrained access to surgery, shaping physical health trajectories.
  • Findings reinforce the importance of early, supervised endocrine care to minimise preventable physical health risks.

Study aim

To describe care pathways and early metabolic effects of GAHT and associated quality of life during the first treatment year.

DesignQuantitative (Prospective observational cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=83; MtF (n=56), FtM (n=27)

Physical health findings

  • Hormone regimens: MtF treated with 17β-estradiol plus anti-androgens; FtM treated with transdermal or intramuscular testosterone.
  • Thromboembolic/metabolic safety: No VTE events reported during the first year; lipid and glucose profiles largely stable, with pre-existing dyslipidaemia improving under monitoring.
  • Weight/BMI: Minimal weight change overall; 8.9% MtF and 18.5% FtM obese at 1 year.
  • Haematological effects: No anaemia (MtF) or polycythaemia (FtM); electrolytes remained normal with spironolactone.
  • Endocrine effects: Expected suppression of testosterone in MtF and physiological male-range testosterone in FtM; >20% MtF developed mild hyperprolactinaemia, without prolactinoma.
  • Other conditions: 3.6% cholelithiasis in MtF; isolated benign breast lesions; HIV-positive participants safely continued GAHT.

Overall findings and implications

  • Clinically supervised GAHT was physically safe over the first year, with no major cardiometabolic or thrombotic complications.
  • Modern hormone regimens showed favourable metabolic profiles compared with older treatments.
  • Findings support routine monitoring rather than restriction of GAHT in medically screened individuals.
  • Longer-term follow-up is required to assess risks beyond the first treatment year.

Study aim

To compare health-related QoL in trans men and trans women versus the general population and examine associations with socioeconomic/medical indicators.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–64
SampleN=140; trans men (n=63), trans women (n=77)

Physical health findings

  • Physical functioning & general health: Transgender women scored lower than trans men on physical functioning and general health; bodily pain scores were better among trans women.
  • Comparison with general population: Physical health–related QOL of transgender women was similar to the general female population; transgender men showed lower general health and vitality than general male norms.
  • Hormone exposure: 95.5% were using GAHT; longer duration of hormone use was associated with less bodily pain and better general health in transgender women, but worse role-physical scores in transgender men.
  • Surgical status: No significant differences in physical health scores between those with vs without genital or breast surgery; transgender men with an erection prosthesis reported better vitality.
  • Medical comorbidity: Chronic physical illness was not a major driver of physical health scores in this cohort.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health–related quality of life was broadly comparable to population norms, particularly among transgender women receiving stable care.
  • Medical transition status alone did not strongly predict physical health outcomes.
  • Socioeconomic factors (age, education, employment, income, relationship status) had a stronger association with physical health functioning than medical interventions.
  • Findings support the importance of integrated medical care alongside social and economic supports to optimise physical health outcomes.

Study aim

To examine associations between transgender identity and cardiovascular risk factors and myocardial infarction.

DesignQuantitative (Cross-sectional, population-based survey analysis)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,055; transgender men (n=1,267), transgender women (n=1,788)

Physical health findings

  • Myocardial infarction (self-reported): Higher prevalence among transgender adults compared with cisgender comparators.
  • Adjusted odds: – Transgender men: >2-fold higher odds of MI vs cisgender men (OR 2.53, 95% CI 1.14–5.63) and ~5-fold vs cisgender women (OR 4.90, 95% CI 2.21–10.90). – Transgender women: >2-fold higher odds vs cisgender women (OR 2.56, 95% CI 1.78–3.68); no significant difference vs cisgender men after adjustment.
  • Cardiovascular risk factors: Higher prevalence of smoking, lower physical activity, and comparable rates of hypertension, diabetes, hypercholesterolaemia, and kidney disease relative to cisgender groups.
  • Age: Transgender respondents were younger on average than cisgender comparators, yet still demonstrated elevated MI prevalence.
  • Hormone data: No information on hormone therapy exposure or duration was available.

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender adults showed elevated myocardial infarction risk independent of traditional cardiovascular risk factors.
  • Risk appeared highest among transgender men, suggesting sex-specific or treatment-related vulnerabilities.
  • Findings highlight the need for enhanced cardiovascular risk assessment and prevention in transgender populations.
  • Lack of hormone exposure data limits causal inference and underscores the need for longitudinal, treatment-linked studies.

Study aim

To estimate mortality rates among trans people receiving long-term GAHT and describe adverse outcomes.

DesignQuantitative (Longitudinal cohort study (retrospective cohort with long-term follow-up).)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)16–76
SampleN=1,331; MtF (n=966), FtM (n=365)

Physical health findings

  • All-cause mortality: Overall mortality was higher than expected compared with the general population, particularly among transfeminine individuals.
  • Cardiovascular mortality: Increased cardiovascular-related deaths observed in transfeminine participants, especially those exposed to ethinyl estradiol.
  • Venous thromboembolism: Elevated mortality from VTE-related causes among transfeminine individuals; risk linked to older oestrogen formulations.
  • Cancer mortality: No overall increase in cancer-related mortality compared with the general population.
  • Transmasculine outcomes: Mortality rates among transmasculine participants were not significantly elevated.
  • Hormone exposure: Higher mortality risks were associated with long-term hormone use, particularly synthetic oestrogens.

Overall findings and implications

  • Long-term hormone treatment, particularly older oestrogen regimens, was associated with increased cardiovascular and thromboembolic mortality in transfeminine individuals.
  • Findings support the transition away from ethinyl estradiol toward safer oestrogen formulations.
  • No evidence of increased cancer mortality provides reassurance regarding malignancy risk.
  • Results emphasise the importance of hormone choice, cardiovascular risk assessment, and long-term monitoring in gender-affirming endocrine care.

Study aim

To describe veteran status among transfeminine individuals and examine healthcare utilisation, health concerns and barriers to care.

DesignQuantitative (Cross-sectional community-based survey study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)26–79
SampleN=141; MtF transsexual (n=80), MtF cross-dresser (n=61)

Physical health findings

  • Physical health status: Mean SF-12 physical health score 49.8 (SD 5.0), comparable to general population norms.
  • Healthcare utilisation: 88.4% reported at least one healthcare visit in the past year (VA or non-VA).
