Study aim

To estimate cancer incidence in transgender people enrolled in three integrated healthcare systems.

DesignQuantitative (Retrospective cohort study using electronic health records from three large integrated healthcare systems, with matched cisgender comparison cohorts and longitudinal follow-up for incident cancer.)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=4,889; transfeminine, transmasculine

Physical health findings

  • Overall cancer incidence: No difference in all-cancer incidence for transfeminine (TF) or transmasculine (TM) participants compared with matched cisgender reference groups.
  • Transfeminine-specific risks: – Lower prostate cancer risk vs cisgender men (HR 0.4). – Higher endocrine gland cancer risk (HR 5.2) and viral infection–related cancers (HR 2.0) vs cisgender men. – Higher lymphatic/haematopoietic cancer risk vs cisgender women (HR 3.0).
  • Transmasculine-specific risks: – Higher breast cancer risk vs cisgender men, but no increase vs cisgender women. – Increased smoking- and viral-related cancers vs cisgender men, driven entirely by cervical cancer.
  • Hormone exposure: Cancer risks were observed in a cohort with access to healthcare; specific hormone dose/duration effects were not directly analysed.
  • Follow-up: Mean follow-up 4.0 years (TF) and 3.5 years (TM).

Overall findings and implications

  • Cancer risk among transgender adults was largely comparable to cisgender populations when matched on age, race, and healthcare access.
  • Observed differences were site-specific and aligned with natal anatomy and screening practices rather than global cancer excess.
  • Findings support the importance of age- and anatomy-appropriate cancer screening (e.g. prostate, breast, cervical) in transgender care.
  • Longer follow-up is required to clarify lifetime cancer risk, particularly with prolonged hormone exposure.

Study aim

To identify factors distinguishing trans/GNC adolescents with no self-harm, NSSI only, and NSSI plus suicide attempt.

DesignQuantitative (Population-based cross-sectional survey analysis)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)14–17
SampleN=1,635; transgender, genderqueer, genderfluid, unsure

Physical health findings

  • Physical health problems: 20–33% reported a long-term physical health condition lasting ≥6 months, with higher prevalence among those reporting NSSI and suicide attempts.
  • Physical abuse exposure: Physical or sexual abuse was common and strongly associated with more severe self-harm profiles.
  • Running away from home: A physically relevant adverse outcome, reported by 38% of adolescents with NSSI + suicide attempt.
  • Substance-related physical risk: Higher rates of alcohol, marijuana, cigarette, and illicit drug use among those with self-harm behaviors.

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender/GNC adolescents experience substantial physical vulnerability alongside mental health risk, particularly among those engaging in self-harm.
  • Physical health problems, exposure to abuse, substance use, and housing instability cluster with more severe outcomes.
  • Findings highlight the need for integrated physical, mental, and safeguarding assessments rather than siloed mental-health-only approaches.
  • Structural and environmental protections (family support, school safety, stable housing) are critical to reducing cumulative health risk.

Study aim

To describe mortality and morbidity outcomes in trans people receiving GAHT.

DesignQuantitative (Retrospective descriptive cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–86
SampleN=1,109; M→F, F→M transsexuals

Physical health findings

  • Mortality: Overall mortality in both transfeminine and transmasculine individuals was not increased compared with the general population.
  • Venous thromboembolism (VTE): Markedly increased incidence among transfeminine individuals using oral oestrogens (≈20-fold increase); risk was substantially lower with transdermal estradiol, particularly in those aged >40 years.
  • Cardiovascular disease: No increase in myocardial infarction mortality or morbidity compared with population expectations.
  • Cancer: No breast cancer cases observed; one prostate cancer case reported. Overall cancer mortality was not elevated.
  • Endocrine/liver effects: Frequent hyperprolactinaemia in transfeminine individuals, reversible with dose reduction; mild liver enzyme elevations occurred, usually transient.
  • Gallbladder disease: Slightly increased incidence of cholelithiasis, consistent with oestrogen exposure.
  • Transmasculine outcomes: No serious morbidity attributable to testosterone, aside from mild liver enzyme elevations and acne.

Overall findings and implications

  • Cross-sex hormone treatment was not associated with increased overall mortality when delivered under medical supervision.
  • VTE represents the most significant physical risk, particularly with older oral oestrogen regimens.
  • Findings strongly support the use of transdermal estradiol to reduce thrombotic risk in transfeminine individuals.
  • Results provide early evidence that gender-affirming hormone therapy can be physically safe, with appropriate regimen choice and monitoring.
  • Long-term surveillance remains important to identify rare or delayed adverse outcomes.

Study aim

To examine disparities in weight and weight-related behaviours by transgender identity.

DesignQuantitative (Population-based cross-sectional survey analysis)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–24
SampleN=53; transgender

Physical health findings

  • Weight status: Transgender students were more likely to be underweight (RR 4.78, 95% CI 1.61–14.18) and more likely to be obese (RR 2.45, 95% CI 1.21–4.93) compared with non-transgender peers; no difference in overweight status.
  • Physical activity: Less likely to meet strenuous physical activity recommendations (RR 1.16, 95% CI 1.01–1.34) and to engage in any strengthening activity (RR 1.32, 95% CI 1.11–1.56).
  • Sedentary behaviour: More likely to exceed recommended screen time (RR 1.20, 95% CI 1.02–1.41).
  • Dietary behaviours: No significant differences in fruit/vegetable intake, breakfast consumption, soda, fast food, or restaurant food consumption.
  • Body-related measures: No significant difference in unhealthy weight-control behaviours; body dissatisfaction slightly more common.

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender college students experience marked weight-related physical health disparities, with a bimodal pattern of underweight and obesity.
  • Lower physical activity and higher sedentary behaviour may contribute to long-term cardiometabolic risk.
  • Findings highlight the need for gender-affirming, inclusive physical activity and weight-related health interventions in college settings.
  • Structural barriers (e.g. safety and inclusion in recreational spaces) should be addressed to support healthier physical behaviours.

Study aim

To describe somatic health, pubertal development and psychosocial background in adolescents seeking GAHT and assess interest in surgery.

DesignQuantitative (Retrospective register-based cohort study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)16–17
SampleN=124; FtM, MtF adolescents

Physical health findings

  • Overall somatic health: Adolescents seeking GAHT were generally physically healthy, with few chronic medical conditions.
  • Body mass index: 33% were overweight or obese (BMI > 25 kg/m²); overweight and obesity were more common in FtM adolescents than in the general adolescent population.
  • Respiratory and other conditions: Asthma (8%) was the most common physical condition; migraine and other chronic illnesses were rare.
  • Blood pressure: MtF adolescents had higher mean systolic BP than FtM adolescents, though values were largely within reference ranges.
  • Laboratory findings: Most baseline labs were within reference ranges; mild abnormalities included elevated LDL cholesterol and prolactin, particularly among those with prior non-prescribed hormone use.
  • Substance use: 14.5% reported regular smoking; no heavy alcohol or illicit drug use reported.
  • Contraindications: No absolute somatic contraindications to GAHT were identified.

Overall findings and implications

  • Adolescents seeking GAHT showed good baseline physical health, supporting the feasibility of medical intervention from a somatic perspective.
  • Overweight/obesity emerged as the main physical health concern, particularly among FtM youth, with early metabolic signals (e.g. HbA1c, ALT) in higher-BMI individuals.
  • Findings highlight the importance of routine physical screening and lifestyle counselling alongside gender-affirming care.
  • The absence of major somatic contraindications suggests that physical health rarely limits access to GAHT, though long-term follow-up is needed.

Study aim

To compare mental wellbeing of TGNC adolescents in China with cisgender peers.

DesignQuantitative (School-based cross-sectional survey)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=12,108; cisgender, transgender, nonbinary, questioning

Physical health findings

  • Self-rated overall health: TGNC adolescents reported significantly poorer overall physical health compared with cisgender peers.
  • Sleep quality: TGNC youth had significantly worse sleep quality, indicating a physical health burden related to rest and recovery.
  • Bullying exposure: TGNC adolescents—particularly AMAB groups—experienced higher rates of school bullying, a factor associated with poorer physical health.
  • Somatic health status: No objective clinical diagnoses, laboratory measures, or physiological assessments were reported; physical health was assessed via self-report (SF-36 item).

Overall findings and implications

  • TGNC adolescents reported poorer perceived physical health and sleep, suggesting early physical health vulnerability alongside psychosocial stressors.
  • Physical health disparities appear closely linked to school victimisation and minority stress, rather than identified medical illness.
  • Findings highlight the importance of school-based protections and supportive environments to mitigate physical health impacts.
  • The absence of objective medical data underscores the need for integrated physical health assessment in adolescent TGNC research.

Study aim

To compare fracture incidence among trans people receiving long-term GAHT with an age-matched reference population.

DesignQuantitative (Nationwide retrospective cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,059; trans women (n=1,089 + 934), trans men (n=1,036)

Physical health findings

  • Fracture risk (trans women): – Overall fracture risk not increased compared with reference populations. – Higher fracture risk in trans women ≥50 years compared with age-matched cisgender men (OR 1.90), but similar to cisgender women. – Younger trans women showed a trend toward higher fracture risk vs cis women, but not vs cis men.
  • Fracture type: Trans women had a higher proportion of osteoporotic fractures (hip, spine, forearm, humerus) compared with cis men.
  • Bone density: Lower lumbar spine T-scores were associated with higher fracture risk; findings suggest low pre-treatment BMD rather than GAHT effects.
  • Trans men: No increased fracture risk; risk was lower than cis men and similar to cis women.
  • Hormone levels: Estradiol, testosterone, and LH levels were not independently associated with fracture risk.

Overall findings and implications

  • Long-term GAHT was not associated with excess fracture risk overall, supporting its physical safety from a bone perspective.
  • Older trans women represent a group with increased skeletal vulnerability, likely reflecting lower baseline bone mass rather than hormone exposure.
  • Findings support baseline and longitudinal bone health assessment, particularly for trans women and with increasing age.
  • Longer follow-up into older age is needed to clarify lifetime fracture and osteoporosis risk.

Study aim

To examine bone mineral density change over 10 years of GAHT and assess implications for long-term bone monitoring.

DesignQuantitative (Retrospective longitudinal cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=1,254; trans women (n=711), trans men (n=543)

Physical health findings

  • Baseline bone health: Low lumbar spine BMD present before GAHT in 21.9% of trans women and 4.3% of trans men (Z-score < –2.0).
  • Lumbar spine BMD: No significant change in absolute BMD over 10 years in trans women (+0.006 g/cm²) or trans men (+0.008 g/cm²).
  • Z-scores: Lumbar spine Z-scores increased over 10 years (trans women +0.22; trans men +0.34).
  • Hormone associations: – Higher estradiol levels associated with BMD increases in trans women. – Lower LH associated with BMD increases in trans men.
  • Hip/femoral neck: No significant changes detected (limited long-term data).
  • Monitoring tools: DXA scans; routine sex hormone blood tests.

Overall findings and implications

  • Long-term GAHT does not negatively affect bone mineral density over 10 years.
  • Routine DXA monitoring during GAHT is not indicated in the absence of risk factors.
  • High prevalence of low BMD before treatment, particularly in trans women, supports considering baseline BMD assessment.
  • Adequate hormone dosing and treatment adherence are important for bone health optimisation.

Study aim

To evaluate CVD and cancer morbidity during GAHT and compare with matched controls.

DesignQuantitative (case–control / cross-sectional comparative study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)17–86
SampleN=352; trans women (n=214), trans men (n=138)

Physical health findings

  • Venous thromboembolism (VTE): 5.1% of trans women experienced VTE/PE during hormone therapy; nearly half occurred within the first year and several around gender-affirming surgery. Events were commonly associated with smoking, immobilisation, hypercholesterolaemia, or clotting disorders.
  • Cardiovascular disease: Trans women had higher prevalence of myocardial infarction than cisgender women, and higher cerebrovascular disease than cisgender men; rates were similar to cisgender men for MI.
  • Type 2 diabetes: Higher prevalence in both trans women and trans men compared with controls; many diagnoses pre-dated hormone therapy.
  • Obesity/BMI: BMI and obesity prevalence were similar to controls in both groups.
  • Cancer: No increased cancer prevalence compared with control populations; cancer types were heterogeneous and uncommon.
  • Trans men: No increase in MI, cerebrovascular disease, or VTE compared with controls; testosterone appeared relatively safe in the medium term.5.1% of trans women experienced VTE/PE during hormone therapy; nearly half occurred within the first year and several around gender-affirming surgery. Events were commonly associated with smoking, immobilisation, hypercholesterolaemia, or clotting disorders.
  • Cardiovascular disease: Trans women had higher prevalence of myocardial infarction than cisgender women, and higher cerebrovascular disease than cisgender men; rates were similar to cisgender men for MI.
  • Type 2 diabetes: Higher prevalence in both trans women and trans men compared with controls; many diagnoses pre-dated hormone therapy.
  • Obesity/BMI: BMI and obesity prevalence were similar to controls in both groups.
  • Cancer: No increased cancer prevalence compared with control populations; cancer types were heterogeneous and uncommon.
  • Trans men: No increase in MI, cerebrovascular disease, or VTE compared with controls; testosterone appeared relatively safe in the medium term.

Overall findings and implications

  • Overall physical morbidity during gender-affirming hormone therapy was relatively low, particularly in trans men.
  • Trans women showed increased cardiovascular and thromboembolic risk, especially early in treatment and in the presence of modifiable risk factors.
  • Findings support careful cardiovascular risk assessment, smoking cessation, and regimen choice (route/type of oestrogen) in trans women.
  • Cancer risk was not elevated, providing reassurance regarding medium-term malignancy outcomes.
  • Results reinforce the importance of individualised risk management and long-term monitoring during hormone therapy.

Study aim

To describe short-term hormonal/clinical changes, side effects and adverse events during GAHT in trans men and trans women.

DesignQuantitative (prospective multicentre cohort; 1-year follow-up)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=106; trans men (n=53), trans women (n=53)

Physical health findings

  • Serious adverse events: No deaths, cardiovascular events, venous thromboembolism, pulmonary embolism, or osteoporotic fractures observed during 12-month follow-up.
  • Trans men (testosterone): – Increased lean mass, decreased fat mass, and shift to android fat distribution. – Modest increases in LDL-C, total cholesterol, triglycerides; HDL-C decreased. – Hematocrit increased; erythrocytosis occurred in ~4%. – Mild liver enzyme elevations common; clinically significant abnormalities rare.
  • Trans women (oestrogen + antiandrogen): – Increased fat mass, reduced lean mass, gynoid fat redistribution. – Decreases in total cholesterol and LDL-C; HDL-C also decreased. – Fasting insulin increased, but no cases of diabetes. – Prolactin increased, especially with cyproterone acetate; symptomatic hyperprolactinaemia rare.
  • Blood pressure: Small increases observed, but no new hypertension diagnoses.

Overall findings and implications

  • Contemporary GAHT regimens were effective and physically safe in the short term, with a low rate of clinically significant adverse events.
  • Observed metabolic and body-composition changes were expected and sex-steroid related, supporting the biological plausibility of findings.
  • Results reinforce the importance of routine metabolic, hematologic, and liver monitoring, particularly during the first year of treatment.
  • Findings support the continued use of modern oestrogen formulations and dosing strategies to minimise cardiovascular and thrombotic risk.

Study aim

To examine self-rated health, disability and QoL among trans feminine, trans masculine, non-binary and transvestite groups.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional web survey)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–94
SampleN=796; trans feminine, trans masculine, gender nonbinary, transvestites

Physical health findings

  • Self-rated health: ~18–20% reported poor general health; ~51% reported good health.
  • Disability: 53% reported a disability, defined as a physical or mental condition impairing daily functioning or work ability.
  • Predictors of poorer physical health: – Gender nonbinary identity (aOR ~2.2). – History of negative healthcare experiences (aOR ~1.9). – Inability to access legal gender recognition (aOR ~3.1). – Low income, unemployment/long-term sick leave, lack of practical or social support.
  • Healthcare access: High prevalence of negative healthcare encounters, including discrimination and provider incompetence.

Overall findings and implications

  • A substantial proportion of trans people report poor physical health and functional disability compared with the general population.
  • Physical health outcomes are strongly shaped by structural and healthcare-related factors, rather than transition status alone.
  • Lack of legal gender recognition and negative healthcare experiences are key drivers of health inequality.
  • Findings support the need for structural reforms, inclusive healthcare practices, and improved access to legal recognition to reduce physical health disparities.

Study aim

To characterise health of non-binary adults using secondary analysis of a non-probability sample of TGNC adults in Massachusetts.

DesignQuantitative (secondary analysis of cross-sectional survey data)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)18–75
SampleN=452; non-binary (n=185), binary (n=267)

Physical health findings

  • Healthcare utilisation:
  • Non-binary respondents were less likely to be up to date with annual wellness visits and reported lower routine healthcare engagement than binary respondents.
  • No major differences were identified in reported physical health diagnoses between non-binary and binary participants; physical health indicators were largely indirect (healthcare utilisation rather than disease prevalence).

Overall findings and implications

  • Non-binary transgender adults differed from binary adults across healthcare access, mental health, substance use, and social determinants, with poorer engagement in routine healthcare.
  • Findings highlight gaps in access to preventive care and the need for gender-inclusive health systems that recognise non-binary identities and reduce structural barriers to care.

Study aim

To compare health status and healthcare experiences between autistic and allistic transgender and non-binary adults.

DesignQuantitative (cross-sectional survey sub-analysis (Trans PULSE Canada 2019))
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥14
SampleN=2,858; man/boy, woman/girl, non-binary, Indigenous gender identities

Physical health findings

  • General health: Autistic participants reported significantly worse self-rated physical health than allistic peers (higher odds of “fair/poor” health in both diagnosed and self-identified groups).
  • Unmet healthcare needs: 54.8% (diagnosed) and 60.9% (self-identified) reported unmet healthcare needs vs 42.6% of allistic participants (adjusted ORs 1.50–1.79).
  • Primary care access: No significant difference in having a primary care provider, but greater barriers to effective care among autistic participants.
  • Healthcare access barriers: Greater difficulty affording care and higher likelihood of care avoidance due to anticipated discrimination.

Overall findings and implications

  • Autistic TNB individuals experience poorer physical health status and greater unmet healthcare needs than allistic TNB peers.
  • Health inequities appear driven by access barriers, stigma, and structural exclusion rather than disease-specific burden alone.
  • Findings support the need for accessible, autism-affirming, and integrated physical healthcare models.
  • Highlights the importance of needs-based service access rather than diagnosis-restricted eligibility models.

Study aim

To evaluate short-term physical, psychological and safety outcomes of GnRHa in 12–15-year-olds with persistent/severe GD.

DesignQuantitative (prospective uncontrolled observational study)
PopulationYouth
Age range (years)12–15
SampleN=44; self-identified gender recorded (categories NR)

Physical health findings

  • Pubertal suppression: All participants achieved effective suppression of gonadotropins and sex steroids within 6 months, maintained throughout follow-up.
  • Bone health: No significant change in spine or hip BMD at 12 months; lumbar spine BMC and BMD increased at 24 months. Height-adjusted BMD z-scores declined during suppression but remained within expected ranges.
  • Growth and BMI: Height velocity slowed as expected; weight and BMI z-scores were stable to 24 months, with some increase by 36 months.
  • Menstrual and pubertal changes: All post-menarcheal participants became amenorrhoeic; no progression of pubertal development occurred.
  • Safety: Routine biochemistry and haematology remained normal; anticipated side effects (e.g. hot flushes, fatigue) were common but no serious adverse physical events were reported.

Overall findings and implications

  • Short-term pubertal suppression was physiologically effective and generally well tolerated in this selected cohort.
  • Bone density changes were consistent with suppression of pubertal progression, highlighting the need for ongoing bone health monitoring.
  • The absence of major short-term physical safety concerns supports cautious clinical use within specialist services.
  • Authors emphasise the need for larger, controlled, long-term studies to clarify longer-term physical outcomes, particularly skeletal health.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence, determinants and timing of erythrocytosis in trans men receiving testosterone over long-term follow-up.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=1,073; trans men

Physical health findings

  • Erythrocytosis prevalence: – Hematocrit >0.50 L/L occurred in 11% of trans men; >0.52 L/L in 3.7%; >0.54 L/L in 0.5%.
  • Timing: Largest rise in hematocrit occurred in the first year of testosterone therapy, with continued risk accumulating over time (≈38% probability by 10 years for >0.50 L/L).
  • Risk factors: Higher risk associated with smoking, higher BMI, older age at initiation, long-acting injectable testosterone, and pre-existing pulmonary conditions.
  • Testosterone levels: Higher-than-target testosterone levels were not strongly associated with erythrocytosis after adjustment.

Overall findings and implications

  • Erythrocytosis is a common and clinically relevant long-term physical outcome of testosterone therapy in trans men.
  • Risk persists beyond early treatment, supporting the need for ongoing hematological monitoring throughout therapy.
  • Modifiable factors (smoking, BMI, administration route) represent key risk-reduction targets.
  • Findings support guideline recommendations for regular hematocrit surveillance and individualized risk management rather than routine treatment cessation.

Study aim

To estimate prevalence of PCOS and hyperandrogenism among adolescent transmasculine patients presenting for gender-affirming care.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)8–20
SampleN=105; transmasculine patients identifying as male

Physical health findings

  • PCOS prevalence: 23.8% met criteria for PCOS, higher than estimates for the general adolescent population (8–13%).
  • Body mass index: Patients with PCOS had significantly higher BMI than those without PCOS (mean 33.1 vs 25.6 kg/m²).
  • Hyperandrogenism: PCOS group had higher total and free testosterone levels compared with non-PCOS peers.
  • Metabolic markers: Higher LDL cholesterol observed in the PCOS group; triglycerides and HDL did not differ significantly.
  • Glucose metabolism: Low prevalence of prediabetes (4.8%) and diabetes (0.9%); no significant difference by PCOS status.
  • Other endocrine conditions: No cases of CAH, Cushing’s disease, glucocorticoid resistance, or pregnancy were identified.

Overall findings and implications

  • PCOS appears more prevalent among transmasculine adolescents than in the general population.
  • PCOS in this group clusters with obesity, hyperandrogenism, and dyslipidaemia, indicating early cardiometabolic risk.
  • GAHT initiation may mask PCOS features, highlighting the importance of early identification and baseline screening.
  • Findings support proactive metabolic and cardiovascular risk assessment and counselling in transmasculine youth presenting for care.

Study aim

To compare chronic pain incidence in transgender vs cisgender people and examine associations with GAHT and mental health conditions.

DesignQuantitative (retrospective cohort study)
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=98,352; transgender men (n=56,470), transgender women (n=41,882)

Physical health findings

  • Chronic pain incidence: Transgender individuals had significantly higher rates of new chronic pain diagnoses than cisgender peers.
  • Trans women: Incidence ≈ 3,260–3,310 per 100,000 person-years, ~1.8× higher than cisgender men.
  • Trans men: Incidence ≈ 3,125–3,923 per 100,000 person-years, ~1.7–2.1× higher than cisgender women.
  • Hormone therapy: After matching, GAHT was associated with higher chronic pain risk in both groups – Trans men on testosterone: HR 1.20 – Trans women on oestrogen: HR 1.19
  • Comorbidities: Depression and anxiety substantially increased chronic pain risk across trans and cis cohorts.

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender populations experience a substantially higher burden of chronic pain compared with cisgender individuals.
  • GAHT is associated with increased chronic pain diagnoses, though causality cannot be established.
  • Mental health comorbidity plays a major role, underscoring the biopsychosocial nature of chronic pain.
  • Findings support the need for routine pain screening and integrated physical–mental health care in transgender services.

Study aim

To compare all-cause and suicide mortality among youth referred to gender identity services with matched controls and assess the role of psychiatric morbidity and GR status.

DesignQuantitative (nationwide register-based cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)8–22
SampleN=2,083; gender-referred individuals

Study aim

To assess longitudinal psychiatric diagnoses and psychotropic prescribing following first recorded transgender-related diagnosis in Denmark.

DesignQuantitative (longitudinal register-based cohort study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)≥3
SampleN=3,812; transgender persons

Physical health findings

  • Baseline somatic morbidity: Low and comparable between transgender individuals and controls (Charlson comorbidity index ≥1 in ~4–5%).
  • Cardiometabolic risk indicators: No excess baseline physical comorbidity detected prior to referral for gender-affirming care.
  • Hormone treatment exposure: Gender-affirming hormone therapy initiated in ~55% of the cohort, with median time to initiation 1–2 years post-diagnosis.
  • Surgical exposure: Gender-affirming surgery occurred in a minority; follow-up insufficient to assess post-surgical physical health outcomes.
  • Mortality/major physical outcomes: No signal of increased somatic mortality or severe physical disease identified within follow-up.

Overall findings and implications

  • Physical health burden at baseline was low and comparable to the general population, suggesting no intrinsic excess somatic disease in transgender individuals entering care.
  • Observed health disparities in this cohort were driven primarily by mental health morbidity rather than physical illness.
  • Findings support the need to separate physical from mental health risk when interpreting outcomes in transgender populations.
  • Longer follow-up is required to assess long-term cardiometabolic and surgical outcomes associated with gender-affirming treatment.

Study aim

To examine changes over time (1996–2019) in psychiatric needs among people seeking gender reassignment versus controls, and differences by GR status.

DesignQuantitative (register-based longitudinal follow-up study)
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)NR
SampleN=3,665; individuals contacting gender identity services

Physical health findings

  • All-cause mortality was higher among individuals who underwent gender reassignment compared with population controls.
  • Cause-specific mortality showed significantly elevated deaths from cardiovascular disease and suicide.
  • No clear increase in deaths from malignancy overall, though numbers were small.
  • Physical health outcomes reflected long-term morbidity rather than short-term treatment complications

Overall findings and implications

  • Transgender individuals undergoing medical transition experienced higher long-term physical health risks compared with the general population.
  • Findings highlight the need for lifelong cardiovascular risk monitoring, preventive care, and integrated physical–mental health follow-up.
  • Authors caution against attributing outcomes solely to treatment, noting the likely contribution of pre-existing health risks and social stressors

Age range (years)≥14
SampleN=2,858; Man or boy; woman or girl; Indigenous or other cultural gender identity (e.g., Two-Spirit); nonbinary, genderqueer, agender, or similar
Determinants examinedEducation; employment; income
Measurement / data sourceSurvey demographics

Key findings

  • Autistic transgender participants reported lower education, employment, and income levels.

Study aim

To characterise mental health outcomes among Australian TGD young people and examine associations with negative life experiences.

DesignQuantitative; cross-sectional survey
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)18–25
SampleN=859; Self-described via open text; categories reported descriptively: trans male/male (29.7%), trans female/female (15.0%), non-binary identities (48.6%), other/unspecified remainder
Determinants examinedBullying; discrimination; peer rejection; housing instability
Measurement / data sourceSelf-report checklist

Key findings

  • High prevalence of bullying, discrimination, peer rejection, and service isolation reported.

Study aim

To examine trajectories of self-reported gender identity among SGM youth and assess whether trajectories/variability relate to depressive symptoms over time.

DesignQuantitative; longitudinal cohort
PopulationMixed
Age range (years)15–21
SampleN=366; Cisgender; binary transgender; genderqueer/nonbinary (assessed at each wave via two-step method)
Determinants examinedLGBT-related violence; socioeconomic proxy
Measurement / data source6-item LGBT violence scale

Key findings

  • Cumulative exposure to LGBT-related violence was associated with higher depressive symptoms.

Study aim

To estimate age of first gender dysphoria experience among adults seeking genital surgery and quantify years lived with untreated dysphoria.

DesignQuantitative; retrospective cohort
PopulationAdult
Age range (years)20–68
SampleN=210; Transgender women (AMAB); transgender men (AFAB); non-binary identities not included
Determinants examinedHealthcare access; generational timing of transition
Measurement / data sourceNot applicable

Key findings

  • Older participants reported longer durations living with untreated gender dysphoria prior to accessing care.
